A d d -dimensional Analyst’s Travelling Salesman Theorem for subsets of Hilbert space Thanks: M. Hyde was supported by The Maxwell Institute Graduate School in Analysis and its Applications, a Centre for Doctoral Training funded by the UK Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (grant EP/L016508/01), the Scottish Funding Council, Heriot-Watt University and the University of Edinburgh.
Matthew Hyde

Abstract
. We are interested in quantitative rectifiability results for subsets of infinite dimensional Hilbert space H H . We prove a version of Azzam and Schul’s d d -dimensional Analyst’s Travelling Salesman Theorem in this setting by showing for any lower d d -regular set E ⊆ H E\subseteq H that ⁡ d ​ i ​ a ​ m ​ ( E ) d + β d ​ ( E ) ∼ ℋ d ​ ( E ) + Error , \mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)^{d}+\beta^{d}(E)\sim\mathscr{H}^{d}(E)+\text{Error}, where β d ​ ( E ) \beta^{d}(E) give a measure of the curvature of E E and the error term is related to the theory of uniform rectifiability (a quantitative version of rectifiability introduced by David and Semmes). To do this, we show how to modify the Reifenberg Parametrization Theorem of David and Toro so that it holds in Hilbert space. As a corollary, we show that a set E ⊆ H E\subseteq H is uniformly rectifiable if and only if it satisfies the so-called Bilateral Weak Geometric Lemma, meaning that E E is bi-laterally well approximated by planes at most scales and locations.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
28A75,28A78,28A12

1.
Introduction

Let
H
be a real separable Hilbert space and
1\leq d<\dim(H)
(the dimension of
H
, possibly infinite). We say a set
E\subseteq H
is
d
-rectifiable
if it can be covered
\mathscr{H}^{d}
-a.e. by countably many Lipschitz images of
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Here,
\mathscr{H}^{d}
stands for the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure. Rectifiable sets are a central object of study in geometric measure theory and form a natural setting in many other areas. In the early nineties, driven by trying to understand the
L^{2}
-boundedness of certain singular integral operators, there was interest in studying more quantitative aspects of rectifiability. Two of the main results to come out of this period were the Analyst’s Travelling Salesman Theorem of Peter Jones
[
Jon90
]
, and the theory of uniform rectifiability, due to David and Semmes
[
DS91
,
DS93
]
.

Peter Jones’ Travelling Salesman Theorem (TST) gives a necessary and sufficient condition for when a set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
can be contained in a rectifiable curve (curve of finite length). This condition is stated in terms of
\beta
-numbers, which give a measure of flatness at a particular location and scale. For sets
E,B\subseteq H,
define

(1.1)

\displaystyle\beta_{E,\infty}^{d}(B)=\frac{2}{r_{B}}\inf_{L}\sup\{\text{dist}(y,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in E\cap B\},

where
L
ranges over
d
-planes in
H.
Jones proved the following in the case that
H=\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
\Delta
denote the collection of dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Then there is a connected set
\Gamma\supseteq E
such that

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\lesssim\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}E+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\Delta\\
Q\cap E\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\beta_{E,\infty}^{1}(3Q)^{2}\ell(Q).

Conversely, if
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is connected and
\mathscr{H}^{1}(\Gamma)<\infty
, then

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}\Gamma+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\Delta\\
Q\cap\Gamma\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\beta_{\Gamma,\infty}^{1}(3Q)^{2}\ell(Q)\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{1}(\Gamma).

In other words, if
E
is flat enough then there exists a rectifiable curve
\Gamma
so that
E\subseteq\Gamma
and there is quantitative control on the length of
\Gamma.
Moreover, the second half of Theorem
1.1
shows there is quantitative control on how non-flat a curve can be. This was later extended to subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by Okikiolu
[
Oki92
]
(with the implicit constants depending on the ambient dimension
n
), and to subsets of infinite dimensional Hilbert space by Schul
[
Sch07b
]
. Notice that if
\Gamma
is a curve, then Theorem
1.1
implies

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\sim\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}\Gamma+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\Delta\\
Q\cap E\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\beta^{1}_{\Gamma,\infty}(3Q)^{2}\ell(Q).

Similar results have been considered in a number of non-Hilbert space settings, for example, the Heisenberg group
[
FFP+07
,
LS16b
,
LS16a
]
, Carnot groups
[
CLZ19
,
Li19
]
, and general metric spaces
[
Hah05
,
Hah08
,
Sch07a
,
DS20
]
. See
[
BNV19
]
for a similar result concerning Hölder curves.

Theorem
1.1
is concerned with quantitative information for 1-dimensional subsets of Euclidean space; for higher dimensional subsets, we are lead to a discussion of uniformly rectifiable sets introduced by David and Semmes in
[
DS93
]
.

Definition 1.2
.

A set
E\subseteq H
is called
uniformly
d
-rectifiable
(UR) if it is
Ahlfors
d
-regular
, meaning there is
A>0
such that

r^{d}/A\leq\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r))\leq Ar^{d}\ \text{for all}\ x\in E,\ r\in(0,\text{diam}E)

and has
big pieces of Lipschitz images of

\mathbb{R}^{d}
, meaning there are constants
L,c>0
such that for all
x\in E
and
r\in(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}E),
there is an
L
-Lipschitz map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow H
such that

\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap f(B_{d}(0,r)))\geq cr^{d}.

Uniform rectifiability is a stronger quantitative analogue of rectifiability (every UR set is rectifiable but not every rectifiable set is UR); it has found applications to singular integrals
[
DS93
,
Tol09
,
NVT+14
]
and harmonic measure, see
[
AHM+20
]
and the references therein. When David and Semmes defined uniform rectifiability in
[
DS93
]
, they proved many equivalent conditions. One important result (now a black-box in the theory of UR) is a characterization of UR sets by the Bilateral Weak Geometric Lemma.

To state the definition, we need some notation. For closed sets
E,F\subseteq H
and a set
B\subseteq H
, recall the local normalized Hausdorff distance

(1.5)

\displaystyle d_{B}(E,F)=\frac{2}{\text{diam}(B)}\max\left\{\sup_{y\in E\cap B}\text{dist}(y,F),\sup_{y\in F\cap B}\text{dist}(y,E)\right\}.

If
B=B(x,r),
we may write
d_{x,r}=d_{B(x,r)}.
Let
E\subseteq H
and
\mathscr{D}
be the Christ-David cubes for
E
(these are subsets of
E
that behave like Euclidean dyadic cubes, see Theorem
2.1
for more details). For a cube
Q\in\mathscr{D}
, let
x_{Q}
denote its “centre”,
\ell(Q)
denote its “side-length” and
B_{Q}=B(x_{Q},\ell(Q)).
Again, see Theorem
2.1
. For constants
A\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
, define

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)=\{Q\in\mathscr{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{AB_{Q}}(E,P)\geq\varepsilon\mbox{ for all $d$-planes }P\}

and for a cube
Q\in\mathscr{D},
define

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q,A,\varepsilon)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\subseteq Q\\
R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}.

Definition 1.3
.

We say
E
satisfies the
Bilateral Weak Geometric Lemma
(BWGL) if for each
A\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
there is a constant
C=C(A,\varepsilon)
so that for any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
,

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q,A,\varepsilon)\leq C\ell(Q)^{d}.

David and Semmes originally stated the BWGL in terms of a
Carleson measure
condition, see
[
DS93
, Definition 2.2]
, however the above formulation is equivalent. See
[
DS93
, Pages 54-55]
, where a similar equivalence is discussed for the
Weak Geometric Lemma
(WGL). In
[
DS93
]
, David and Semmes show that an Ahlfors
d
-regular set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is UR if and only if it satisfies the BWGL. This has been an essential tool in many results concerning UR, for example
[
Tol15
,
HMM+16
,
AH20
]
. As a corollary of our main result, we show the same is true for subsets of infinite dimensional Hilbert space.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
E\subseteq H
be Ahlfors
d
-regular. Then
E
is UR if and only if
E
satisfies the
BWGL
.

Remark 1.5
.

The proof of Theorem
1.4
for subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be found in
[
DS93
, Chapter II.2]
. The proof relies on the existence of a “unit star”
\Sigma_{0}
, which is the intersection of the unit ball with a
finite
number of
d
-planes, chosen in such a way that
\Sigma_{0}
has large projections onto every
d
-plane
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}.
No such set exists in Hilbert space. These star-like sets are also used in the David and Semmes proof of the fact that that WHIP and WTP imply BWGL. See
[
DS93
, Theorem II.3.9]
and its proof beginning in
[
DS93
, Section II.3.5]
.

For sets which are not Ahlfors
d
-regular, some partial results akin to the first half of Theorem
1.1
are obtained in
[
Paj96
,
DT12
]
. However, the second half of Theorem
1.1
(with the
\beta
-numbers as defined in (
1.1
)) is known to be false by an example of Fang
[
Fan90
]
. With a different
\beta
-number, some progress was made in
[
AS18
]
for lower content
d
-regular sets. A set
E\subseteq H
is said to be
lower content
d
-regular
in a ball
B
if there exists a constant
c>0
such that for each
x\in E\cap B
and
r\in(0,r_{B}),

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r))\geq cr^{d}.

Here,
\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}
denotes the Hausdorff content, see Section
2
. Azzam and Schul introduced a new
\beta
-number, based on a
\beta
-number of David and Semmes
[
DS91
, Equation 1.9]
, and proved a version of Jones’ TST for lower content
d
-regular subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Our main result is a version of that in
[
AS18
]
for infinite dimensional Hilbert spaces. We first recall the
\beta
-number from
[
AS18
]
. For
E\subseteq H
and a ball
B
, define

(1.6)

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B)

\displaystyle=\inf_{L}\left(\frac{1}{r_{B}^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in E\cap B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,P)>tr_{B}\})t^{p-1}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where the infimum is taken over all
d
-planes
L
in
H
. Also, define the critical exponent,

(1.7)

\displaystyle p(d)=\begin{cases}\frac{2d}{d-2}&d>2\\
\infty&d\leq 2\end{cases}.

The statement, which separate into two parts, reads as follows.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
1\leq d<\dim(H)
,
1\leq p<p(d)
,
C_{0}>1
and
A>10^{5}
. Let
E\subseteq H
be a lower content
d
-regular set with regularity constant
c
and Christ-David cubes
\mathscr{D}.
There exists
\varepsilon>0
small enough so that the following holds.
Let
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
and

\beta_{E,C_{0},d,p}(Q_{0})\coloneqq\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{Q\subseteq Q_{0}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Then

(1.8)

\displaystyle\beta_{E,C_{0},d,p}(Q_{0})\lesssim_{A,d,c,p,C_{0},\epsilon}\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\emph{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
1\leq d<\dim(H)
,
1\leq p<\infty
,
C_{0}>1
be sufficiently large (we will choose
C_{0}>2\rho^{-1}
with
\rho
as in Theorem
2.1
),
A>1
and
\varepsilon>0.
Let
E\subseteq H
be a lower content
d
-regular set with regularity constant
c
and Christ-David cubes
\mathscr{D}
. For
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
we have

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)\lesssim_{A,d,c,C_{0},\epsilon}\beta_{E,C_{0},d,p}(Q_{0}),

with
\beta_{E,C_{0},d,p}(Q_{0})
as in Theorem
1.6
.

Remark 1.8
.

As already mentioned, this was proven in the case
\dim(H)<\infty
in
[
AS18
]
(with constant depending on
\dim(H)
), see
[
AS18
, Theorem 3.2, Theorem 3.3]
. Our contribution is the case
\dim(H)=\infty
(we also achieve constants independent of
\dim(H)
if
\dim(H)<\infty
).

Instead of asking what kind of sets can imitate the role of rectifiable curves in Theorem
1.1
, Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
focus on proving estimates like (
1.4
) for lower content
d
-regular subsets of
H
. In contrast to (
1.4
), note the appearance of the error term
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).
This accounts for the fact a general lower content
d
-regular set may have holes, while a curve does not. The error terms disappears for example if
E
is Reifenberg flat. Recall that a set
E\subseteq H
is said to be
(\varepsilon,d,\delta_{0})
-
Reifenberg flat
if for each
x\in E
and
0<r<\delta_{0}
there exists a
d
-plane
P_{x,r}
so that

d_{x,r}(E,P_{x,r})\leq\varepsilon.

In fact, the proof of Theorem
1.6
will utilize a similar result specialized to Reifenberg flat surfaces. Again, the following was proved in
[
AS18
]
for the case
\dim(H)<\infty
with constants depending on
n
.

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
1\leq d<\dim(H)
,
1\leq p<p(d)
and
C_{0}>1
. Suppose
E
is an
(\varepsilon,d,\delta_{0})
-Reifenberg flat surface with Christ-David cubes
\mathscr{D}
. There exists
\varepsilon>0
small enough so that for any
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
we have

(1.10)

\displaystyle\beta_{E,C_{0},d,p}(Q_{0})\lesssim_{d,p,C_{0},\epsilon,\delta_{0}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}).

Before moving on, let us mention some related works. Edelen, Naber and Valtorta
[
ENV16
]
give very general results about how well the size of a measure
\mu
is bounded from above by a quantity involving
\beta
-numbers. These
\beta
-numbers are like those introduced in (
1.6
), except one integrates with respect to
\mu
instead of
\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}.
In contrast to the above, their result requires the existence of a locally finite measure. Moreover, even if
\mathscr{H}^{d}|_{E}
is locally finite, the
\beta
-number associated to the measure
\mathscr{H}^{d}|_{E}
can still be quite unstable. See
[
AS18
, Section 1.3]
.

In the Euclidean setting, Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
were extended in
[
AV19
]
to allow for more general error terms in place of
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).
Like
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)
, these error terms are connected to the theory of uniform rectifiability, see
[
AV19
]
for more details. Whereas the results in this paper focus on proving estimates like (
1.4
), a combination of two recent papers gives a
d
-dimensional TST that more closely resembles that in Theorem
1.1
. Working with the
\beta
-numbers as defined in (
1.6
), Villa
[
Vil19
]
shows that if
\beta_{E,C_{0},p}(Q_{0})<\infty
for a lower content
d
-regular set
E
and
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
, then
Q_{0}
can be contained in a
stable
d
-surfaces

\Sigma
with
\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma)\sim\beta_{E,C_{0},p}(Q_{0})
. Stable
d
-surfaces were defined by David in
[
Dav04
]
. In
[
Hyd20
]
, we introduce a new
\beta
-number and show that for
any
set
E
and
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
,
Q_{0}
can be contained in a lower regular set
F
with
\tilde{\beta}_{E,C_{0},p}(Q_{0})\sim\beta_{F,C_{0},p}(Q_{0}),
where
\tilde{\beta}_{E,C_{0},p}
is defined as in the statement of Theorem
1.6
with respect to the
\beta
-number from
[
Hyd20
]
. Combing the two, for any
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
, there exists a stable
d
-surface
\Sigma
so that
Q_{0}\subseteq\Sigma
and
\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma)\sim\tilde{\beta}_{E,C_{0},p}(Q_{0}).

1.1.
Structure of the paper

The proof of the forward direction of Theorem
1.6
will be carried out in Section
5
. This will make use of Theorem
1.9
which is proven in Section
4
. For each of these, it suffices to consider the sets as subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and show that the constants are independent of
n
. These reductions are shown in Appendix
C
. The proof then follows a similar strategy employed by Azzam and Schul in
[
AS18
]
. We will state without proof those results from
[
AS18
]
whose constants do not depend on
n
; we will provide alternative proofs for those results whose constants do depend on
n
. Let us mention here that there is a mistake in Azzam and Schul’s proof of Theorem
1.8
, specifically, in the proof of
[
AS18
, Lemma 10.4]
. We fix the error in this paper, with a bit more work. An important tool will be a Hilbert space version of the Reifenberg parametrization result of David and Toro. A similar result (also for general Banach spaces) has appeared (in fragments) in
[
ENV18
]
, however, David and Toro’s Euclidean formulation is very general and can be applied as soon as one has a
coherent collection of balls and planes
(CCBP), see Definition
3.1
. In Section
3
and Appendix
A
, we describe how to tie these results together to give a Hilbert space version that is also stated in terms of a CCBP. The proofs of Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.4
will be carried out in Section
6
and Section
7
, respectively.

2.
Preliminaries

We gather some notation and results that will be needed throughout. If there exists
C>0
such that
a\leq Cb,
we write
a\lesssim b.
If the constant
C
depends on a parameter
t
, we write
a\lesssim_{t}b.
We will write
a\sim b
if
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a,
and define
a\sim_{t}b
similarly. From here on, we fix
H
to be an infinite dimensional real and separable Hilbert Space. For a ball
B\subseteq H
, we will let
r_{B}
denote its radius. For closed sets
A,B\subseteq H
, let

\displaystyle\text{dist}(A,B)=\inf\{|x-y|\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in A,\ y\in B\}

and

\text{diam}(A)=\sup\{|x-y|\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x,y\in A\}.

For
A\subseteq H,\ d\geq 0
and
0<\delta\leq\infty
define

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\delta}(A)=\inf\left\{\sum_{i}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(A_{i})^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\subseteq\bigcup_{i}A_{i}\mbox{ and }\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}A_{i}\leq\delta\right\}.

The
d
-dimensional Hausdorff
content
of
A
is defined to be
\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(A)
and the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff
measure
of
A
is defined to be

\mathscr{H}^{d}(A)=\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\delta}(A).

2.1.
Christ-David cubes

We will need a version of Christ-David cubes tailored to subsets
E
of
H
. These are a collection of subsets of
E
that behave like Euclidean dyadic cubes. Theorem
2.1
below is a combination of the formulations due to Christ
[
Chr90
]
, and Hytönen and Martikainen
[
HM12
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

There exist constants
c_{0},\rho\in(0,1)
so that the following holds. Let
E\subseteq H
and
X_{k}
be a sequence of maximal
\rho^{k}
-separated nets. Then, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a collection
\mathscr{D}_{k}
of subsets of
E
, which we call cubes, such that:

(1)

If
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathscr{D}=\bigcup_{k}\mathscr{D}_{k}
and
Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}\not=\emptyset,
then
Q_{1}\subseteq Q_{2}
or
Q_{2}\subseteq Q_{1}.

(2)

For
Q\in\mathscr{D},
let
k(Q)
be the unique integer so that
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}
and set
\ell(Q)=5\rho^{k}.
Then there is
x_{Q}\in X_{k}
such that

\displaystyle B(x_{Q},c_{0}\ell(Q))\subseteq Q\subseteq B(x_{Q},\ell(Q))\eqqcolon B_{Q}.

(3)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z},

E\subseteq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}}B_{Q}.

With some additional assumption on the space
H
or the set
E
, we have some more properties.

(4)

If
H=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then for each
k\in\mathbb{Z},\ E=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}}Q.

(5)

If
E
is Ahlfors
d
-regular, then there exists
C\geq 1
so that
\mathscr{H}^{d}(\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,E\setminus Q)\leq\eta\rho^{k}\})\lesssim\eta^{\frac{1}{C}}\ell(Q)^{d}
for all
Q\in\mathscr{D}
and
t>0.

Remark 2.2
.

Properties (1)-(3) only rely on the metric space structure of
H
and hold in both formulations; (3) is not explicitly stated in either but is an easy consequence of the definitions. Property (4) is unique to the cubes constructed in
[
HM12
]
, which are those used in
[
AS18
]
. Finally, (5) is a property of the cubes constructed in
[
Chr90
]
, we will make use of this when proving Theorem
1.4
.

For
Q\in\mathscr{D}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
Q^{(N)}
denote the unique cube
Q^{\prime}
so that
Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}
and
\ell(Q)=\rho^{N}\ell(Q^{\prime}).
We call
Q^{(1)}
the
parent
of
Q
. We let
\text{Child}(Q)
denote the set of
R\in\mathscr{D}
so that
R^{(1)}=Q.
The cubes in
\text{Child}(Q)
are the
children
of
Q
. We introduce a distance function to a collection of cubes
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{D}.
For
x\in H
set

d_{\mathscr{C}}(x)=\inf\{\ell(R)+\text{dist}(x,R)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\in\mathscr{C}\},

and for
Q\in\mathscr{D},
set

d_{\mathscr{C}}(Q)=\inf\{d_{\mathscr{C}}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in Q\}=\inf\{\ell(R)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q,R)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\in\mathscr{C}\}.

The following lemma is standard and can be found in, for example,
[
AS18
]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
and
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}.
Then

(2.2)

\displaystyle d_{\mathscr{C}}(Q)\leq 2\ell(Q)+\emph{dist}(Q,Q^{\prime})+2\ell(Q^{\prime})+d_{\mathscr{C}}(Q^{\prime}).

At various points in the proof we will need to split
\mathscr{D}
into stopping-time regions, defined below.

Definition 2.4
.

A collection of cubes
S\subseteq\mathscr{D}
is called a
stopping-time region
if the following hold.

(1)

There is a
top cube

Q(S)\in S
such that
Q(S)
contains all cubes in
S
.

(2)

If
Q\in S
and
Q\subseteq R\subseteq Q(S),
then
R\in S
.

(3)

If
Q\in S
, then all siblings of
Q
are also in
S
.

The
minimal cubes
of
S
, denoted by
\min(S)
, are those cubes in
S
whose children are not in
S
and let
z(S)
denote the points contained in
Q(S)
for which we never stopped, i.e.

\displaystyle z(S)=Q(S)\setminus\bigcup\{Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\min(S)\}.

2.2.
Choquet integrals and
\beta
-numbers

For a set
E\subseteq H
and a function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, we define the Choquet integral of
f
over
E
by the formula

(2.3)

\displaystyle\int_{E}f^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in E\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f(x)>t\})t^{p-1}\,dt.

We state some properties of Choquet integration. For the first lemma, see
[
Wan11
, Proposition 2.1]
for more details. Note, in
[
Wan11
]
there are additional upper and lower continuity assumption, but these are not required for the following lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
E\subseteq H,

f,g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
f\leq g
, and
\alpha>0
. Then

(1)

\int_{E}f\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}\leq\int_{E}g\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d};

(2)

\int_{E}(f+\alpha)\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}=\int_{E}f\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}+\alpha\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E);

(3)

\int_{E}\alpha f\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}=\alpha\int_{E}f\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}.

Lemma 2.6

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Let
0<p<\infty.
Let
f_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow[0,\infty)
be a countable collection of Borel functions in
H.
If the sets
\emph{supp}\,f_{i}=\{f_{i}>0\}
have bounded overlap, meaning there exists a
C<\infty
such that

\sum\mathds{1}_{\emph{supp}\,f_{i}}\leq C,

then

(2.4)

\displaystyle\int_{H}\left(\sum f_{i}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}\leq C^{p}\sum\int_{H}f_{i}^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}.

We need to know that Choquet integration satisfies a Jensen inequality. The proof of this fact in
[
AS18
]
is unique to
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
We adapt the usual proof of Jensen’s inequality (see for example Rudin
[
Rud06
]
) to work in
H
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose
E\subseteq H
with
0<\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E)<\infty
,
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is convex and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is bounded. Then,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\phi\left(\frac{1}{\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)}\int_{E}f\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)}\int_{E}\phi\circ f\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}.

Proof.

Since
f
is bounded and
0<\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)<\infty
, we can set

t=\frac{1}{\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)}\int_{E}f\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}<\infty.

Since
\phi
is convex, if
0\leq s<t<u<\infty
, then

(2.6)

\displaystyle\frac{\phi(t)-\phi(s)}{t-s}\leq\frac{\phi(u)-\phi(t)}{u-t}.

Let
\gamma
be the supremum of the right-hand side of (
2.6
) taken over all
u\in[0,t).
It is clear then that

(2.7)

\displaystyle\phi(t)\leq\phi(s)+\gamma\cdot(t-s)

for all
s\in[0,\infty).
By rearranging the above inequality, for any
x\in H
we have

\gamma f(x)+\phi(t)\leq\phi(f(x))+\gamma t.

Integrating both sides with respect to
x
over
E
, and using Lemma
2.5
, we have

(2.8)

\displaystyle\gamma\int_{E}f\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}+\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)\phi(t)\leq\int_{E}\phi\circ f\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}+\gamma\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)t,

hence,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)\phi(t)\leq\int_{E}\phi\circ f\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}+\gamma\cdot\left(\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)t-\int_{E}f\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}\right).

By definition of
t
, the second term on the right-hand side of the above inequality is zero, from which the lemma follows.

∎

Let us recall the
\beta
-number of Azzam and Schul.

Definition 2.8
.

For a set
E\subseteq H
, a ball
B
and a
d
-dimensional plane
P
, define

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B,P)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{r_{B}^{d}}\int_{E\cap B}\left(\frac{\text{dist}(x,P)}{r_{B}}\right)^{p}\,\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{r_{B}^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in E\cap B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,P)>tr_{B}\})t^{p-1}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

and set

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B)=\inf\{\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B,P)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\ \text{is a $d$-dimensional plane in}\ X\}.

We shall need the following properties of
\beta^{d,p}_{E}
.

Lemma 2.9

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 2.12]
)
.

Assume
E\subseteq H
and there is
B
centred on
E
so that for all
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
centred on
E
we have
\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E\cap B^{\prime})\geq cr_{B^{\prime}}^{d}
. Then

(2.10)

\displaystyle\beta_{E,\infty}^{d}\left(\frac{1}{2}B\right)\lesssim_{c,d}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B)^{\frac{1}{d+1}}.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and
E\subseteq H
. Then, for any ball
B
centred on
E
with
\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E\cap B)>0,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B)\lesssim\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B)

Proof.

The lemma is trivial for
p=1,
so we can suppose
p>1.
We can assume without loss of generality that
r_{B}=1.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and suppose
L
is a
d
-plane so that

\beta^{d,p}_{E}(B,L)\leq\beta^{d,p}_{E}(B)+\varepsilon.

Define a function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty),
by setting
\phi(x)=x^{p}.
For this range of
p
,
\phi
is convex. Using this with
0<\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E\cap B)<\infty,
Lemma
2.7
implies

(2.12)

\displaystyle\beta^{d,1}_{E}(B)\leq\beta_{E}^{d,1}(B,L)

\displaystyle=\int_{E\cap B}\text{dist}(x,L)\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}

(2.13)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E\cap B)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{E\cap B}\text{dist}(x,L)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(2.14)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{E\cap B}\text{dist}(x,L)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B)+\varepsilon.

This finishes the proof since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary.
∎

Lemma 2.11

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 2.14]
)
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and
E\subseteq H.
Then, for all balls
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
centred on
E,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\beta^{d,p}_{E}(B^{\prime})\leq\left(\frac{r_{B}}{r_{B^{\prime}}}\right)^{1+\frac{d}{p}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(B).

Remark 2.12
.

In
[
AS18
]
, the exponent of
r_{B}/r_{B^{\prime}}
is stated as
d+p.
Examining their proof, one sees that the above exponent is correct.

The following lemma allows us to control the
\beta
-number of a set
E_{1}
by the
\beta
-number of a nearby set
E_{2}
with an error term depending on the average distance between the two sets. It is a Hilbert space version of
[
AS18
, Lemma 2.21]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty.
There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that the following holds. Suppose
E_{1},E_{2}\subseteq H
,
B^{1}
is a ball centred on
E_{1}
and
B^{2}
is a ball of same radius but centred on
E_{2}
such that
B^{1}\subseteq 2B^{2}.
Suppose for
i=1,2
and all balls
B\subseteq 2B^{i}
centred on
E_{i}
we have
\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E_{i}\cap B)\geq c{r_{B}}^{d}
for some
c>0.
Then

\displaystyle\beta_{E_{1}}^{d,p}(B^{1},P)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{c,d,p}\beta_{E_{2}}^{d,p}(2B^{2},P)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\left(\frac{1}{r^{d}_{B^{1}}}\int_{E_{1}\cap 2B^{1}}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,E_{2})}{r_{B_{1}}}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Azzam and Schul’s proof of the above result relies heavily on the use of the Besicovitch Covering Theorem and is unsuitable in our setting. Our proof of Lemma
2.13
follows a similar approach to the proof of
[
Hyd20
, Lemma 2.29]
. An important ingredient is the following.

Lemma 2.14

(
[
ENV18
, Theorem 3.10]
)
.

Let
V
be an affine
d
-dimensional plane in a Banach space
X
, and
\{B(x_{i},r_{i})\}_{i\in I}
be a family of pairwise disjoint balls with
r_{i}\leq R,\ B(x_{i},r_{i})\subseteq B(x,R),
for some
x\in X
and
\emph{dist}(x_{i},V)<r_{i}/2.
Then, there is a constant
\kappa=\kappa(d)
such that

(2.16)

\sum_{i\in I}r_{i}^{d}\leq\kappa R^{d}.

Remark 2.15
.

Lemma
2.14
says that a collection of disjoint balls in
X
lying close to a
d
-plane satisfy a
d
-dimensional packing condition. This will not only be useful for us here, but will play an important role throughout the paper, as it provides us with a way to implement volume arguments in infinite dimensional Hilbert space.

Proof of Lemma
2.13
.

By scaling and translating, we can assume that
B^{1}=\mathbb{B},
the unit ball in
H
centred at the origin. For
t>0
, set

\displaystyle E_{t}=\{x\in E\cap\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,L)>t\}.

It suffices to show

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E_{t})

\displaystyle\lesssim_{c,d,p}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in F\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,L)>t/2\})

(2.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in E\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,F)>t/32\}),

since then

\displaystyle\beta_{E}^{d,p}(\mathbb{B},L)^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E_{t})t^{p-1}\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim_{c,d,p}\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in F\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,L)>t/2\})t^{p-1}\,dt

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in E\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,F)>t/32\})t^{p-1}\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in F\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,L)>2t\})t^{p-1}\,dt

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in E\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,F)>2t\})t^{p-1}\,dt

\displaystyle\sim\beta_{F}^{d,p}(2\mathbb{B},L)^{p}+\int_{E\cap 2\mathbb{B}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,F)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(x),

So, let us prove (
2.17
). We first need to construct a suitable cover for
E_{t}.
For
x\in E_{t}
, let

\delta(x)=\max\{\text{dist}(x,L),16\text{dist}(x,F)\}

and set
X_{t}
to be a maximally separated net in
E_{t}
such that, for
x,y\in X_{t},
we have

(2.19)

\displaystyle|x-y|\geq 4\max\{\delta(x),\delta(y)\}.

Enumerate
X_{t}=\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}.
For
x_{i}\in X_{t}
denote
B_{i}=B(x_{i},\delta(x_{i})).
Notice, these balls are non-degenerate since
x_{i}\in X_{t}\subseteq E_{t}.
By maximality we know the balls
\{4B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
cover
E_{t}
so,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E_{t})\lesssim\sum_{i\in I}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}.

We partition
I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}
, where

\displaystyle I_{1}=\{i\in I\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\delta(x_{i})=\text{dist}(x_{i},L)\}

and

\displaystyle I_{2}=\{i\in I\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\delta(x_{i})=16\text{dist}(x_{i},F)\}.

If
\text{dist}(x_{i},L)=16\text{dist}(x_{i},F)
, we put
i
in
I_{1}
or
I_{2}
arbitrarily. By (
2.20
), it follows that

\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E_{t})\lesssim\sum_{i\in I_{1}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}+\sum_{i\in I_{2}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}.

We will show that

(2.21)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim_{c,d,p}\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\{x\in F\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,L)>t/2\})

and

(2.22)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{2}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim_{c,d,p}\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\{x\in E\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,F)>t/32\})

from which (
2.17
) follows. The rest of the proof is dedicated to proving (
2.21
) and (
2.22
). Let us begin with (
2.21
). Let

F_{t}=\{x\in F\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,L)>t/2\}

and
\mathscr{B}
be a collection of balls centred on
F,
which cover
F_{t}
, such that

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(F_{t})\sim\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}}r_{B}^{d}.

For
i\in I_{1}
, since
\text{dist}(x_{i},L)>t
(by virtue of that fact that
x_{i}\in E_{t}
), and

\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}=B(x_{i},\text{dist}(x_{i},L)/2)\supseteq B(x_{i},8\text{dist}(x_{i},F)),

we have

(2.24)

\displaystyle F\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\not=\emptyset\quad\text{and}\quad F\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\subseteq F_{t}.

Additionally, since the collection of balls
\mathscr{B}
form a cover of
F_{t}
, there exists at least one
B\in\mathscr{B}
such that
B\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\not=\emptyset.
With this observation, we can partition
I_{1}
as follows. Let

\displaystyle I_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\{i\in I_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{there exists}\ B\in\mathscr{B}\ \text{such that}\ \tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\ \text{and}\ r_{B}\geq r_{B_{i}}\},

\displaystyle I_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\{i\in I_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}r_{B}<r_{B_{i}}\mbox{ for all }B\in\mathscr{B}\mbox{ such that }\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\}.

We first control the sum over
I_{1,1}.
Let
B\in\mathscr{B}
. If there is a ball
B_{i}
satisfying
\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset
and
r_{B}\geq r_{B_{i}}
(which by definition implies
i\in I_{1,1}
), then
2B_{i}\subseteq 4B.
By (
2.19
) we know the
\{2B_{i}\}
are disjoint and satisfy
\text{dist}(x_{i},L)\leq r_{2B_{i}}/2,
so by Lemma
2.14
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{1,1}\\
\frac{1}{2}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\\
r_{B_{i}}\leq r_{B}\end{subarray}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{1,1}\\
2B_{i}\subseteq 4B\end{subarray}}r_{2B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim r_{B}^{d}.

Thus,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1,1}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\leq\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{1,1}\\
\frac{1}{2}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\\
r_{B_{i}}\leq r_{B}\end{subarray}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}}r_{B}^{d}.

We now turn our attention to
I_{1,2}.
For
i\in I_{1,2}
, let
x_{i}^{\prime}
be the point in
F
closest to
x_{i}
and set
B_{i}^{\prime}=B(x_{i}^{\prime},\text{dist}(x_{i},L)/4).
Note that
B_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i},
since for
y\in B_{i}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle|y-x_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{4}\text{dist}(x_{i},L)+|x_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i}|\overset{(i\in I_{1})}{\leq}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{16}\right)\text{dist}(x_{i},L)\leq\frac{1}{2}\text{dist}(x_{i},L).

Since
F\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\subseteq F_{t}
by (
2.24
), and
\mathscr{B}
forms a cover for
F_{t},
the balls

\{B\in\mathscr{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\cap B\cap\frac{1}{2}B_{i}\not=\emptyset\}

form a cover for
F\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}.
Furthermore, if
B\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i}\not=\emptyset
then
B\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{j}=\emptyset
for all
i\not=j
, that is

(2.26)

\displaystyle\#\{i\in I_{1,2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\}\leq 1,

since otherwise
|x_{i}-x_{j}|<4\max\{\delta(x_{i}),\delta(x_{j})\},
contradicting (
2.19
). Since
F
is lower content
d
-regular, we have

\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(F\cap B_{i}^{\prime})\gtrsim r_{B_{i}^{\prime}}^{d}\gtrsim r_{B_{i}}^{d},

which implies

(2.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1,2}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\in I_{1,2}}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(F\cap B_{i}^{\prime})\leq\sum_{i\in I_{1,2}}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(F\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in I_{1,2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in\mathscr{B}\\
F\cap B\cap\frac{1}{2}B_{i}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}r_{B}^{d}\leq\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{1,2}\\
F\cap B\cap\frac{1}{2}B_{i}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}r_{B}^{d}\overset{\eqref{e:1,2}}{\leq}\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}}r_{B}^{d}.

Combining (
2.25
) and (
2.27
), we conclude

(2.28)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}}r_{B}^{d}\overset{\eqref{e:good}}{\lesssim}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(F_{t})

which is (
2.21
).

We turn our attention to proving (
2.22
), the proof of which follows much the same as that for (
2.21
). Let

E_{t}^{\prime}=\{x\in E\cap 2\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{dist}(x,F)>t/32\}

and let
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
be a collection of balls covering
E_{t}^{\prime}
such that each
B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
is centred on
E_{t}^{\prime}
, has
r_{B}\leq 1
and

(2.29)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E_{t}^{\prime})\sim\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}r_{B}^{d}.

As before, we partition
I_{2}
. For
i\in I_{2},
we have
\text{dist}(x_{i},L)>t
(since
x_{i}\in E_{t}
) and

(2.30)

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x_{i},F)\geq\text{dist}(x_{i},L)/16\geq t/16.

Hence,

(2.31)

\displaystyle E\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}=E\cap B(x_{i},\text{dist}(x_{i},F)/2)\subseteq E_{t}^{\prime}.

Thus, for each
B_{i},
since
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
forms a cover for
E_{t}^{\prime},
there exists
B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
such that
B\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}\not=\emptyset.
We partition
I_{2}
by letting

\displaystyle I_{2,1}

\displaystyle=\{i\in I_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{there exists}\ B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ \tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\ \text{and}\ r_{B}\geq r_{B_{i}}\},

\displaystyle I_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\{i\in I_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}r_{B}<r_{B_{i}}\mbox{ for all }B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}\mbox{ such that }\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\}.

If
B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
and
B\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}\not=\emptyset
with
r_{B}\geq r_{B_{i}}
then
2B_{i}\subseteq 4B.
Furthermore, by (
2.19
), we know the
\{2B_{i}\}
are disjoint and satisfy
\text{dist}(x_{i},L)<\text{dist}(x_{i},F)/16=r_{2B_{i}}/2,
so by Lemma
2.14
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{2,1}\\
\frac{1}{32}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\\
r_{B_{i}}\leq r_{B}\end{subarray}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{2,1}\\
2B_{i}\subseteq 4B\end{subarray}}r_{2B_{i}}^{d}\lesssim r_{B}^{d}.

Thus,

(2.32)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{2,1}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\leq\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{2,1}\\
\frac{1}{32}B_{i}\cap B\not=\emptyset\\
r_{B_{i}}\leq r_{B}\end{subarray}}r_{2B_{i}}^{d}\leq\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}r_{B}^{d}.

We now deal with
I_{2,2}.
Since by (
2.31
),
E\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}\subseteq E_{t}^{\prime}
, the balls

\{B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\cap B\cap\frac{1}{32}B_{i}\not=\emptyset\}

form a cover for
E\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}.
As before, if
B\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i}\not=\emptyset
then
B\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{j}=\emptyset
for all
i\not=j
by (
2.19
). By lower regularity of
E
, we know
\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i})\gtrsim r_{B_{i}}^{d},
from which we conclude

(2.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i\in I_{2,2}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}&\lesssim\sum_{i\in I_{2,2}}\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(E\cap\tfrac{1}{32}B_{i})\leq\sum_{i\in I_{2,2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}\\
E\cap B\cap\frac{1}{32}B_{i}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}r_{B}^{d}\\
&=\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{2,2}\\
E\cap B\cap\frac{1}{32}B_{i}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}r_{B}^{d}\lesssim\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}r_{B}^{d}.\end{split}

The proof of (
2.22
) (and hence the proof of the lemma) is completed since

\sum_{i\in I_{2}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}=\sum_{i\in I_{2,1}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}+\sum_{i\in I_{2,2}}r_{4B_{i}}^{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}\eqref{I_F^1}\\
\eqref{I_F^2}\end{subarray}}}{{\lesssim}}\sum_{B\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}}r_{B}^{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:good2}}}{{\lesssim}}\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E_{t}^{\prime}).

∎

Finally, we can use
\beta
-numbers to control the angles between best approximating planes. First, some notation. For two planes
P,P^{\prime}
containing the origin, we define

(2.34)

\displaystyle\angle(P,P^{\prime})=d_{B(0,1)}(P,P^{\prime}).

If
P,P^{\prime}
are general affine planes with
x\in P
and
y\in P^{\prime}
, we define

(2.35)

\displaystyle\angle(P,P^{\prime})=\angle(P-x,P^{\prime}-y).

Lemma 2.16

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 2.18]
)
.

Let
M>1
,
\alpha>0
,
E
a Borel set, and
\mathscr{D}
be the cubes for
E
from Lemma
2.1
and
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}.
Let
P_{Q}
satisfy
\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})=\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q},P_{Q})
. Let
Q,R\subseteq Q_{0}
, and suppose each
T\subseteq Q_{0}
which contains either
Q
or
R
satisfies
\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{T})<\varepsilon
. Then for
\Lambda>0
, if
\emph{dist}(Q,R)\leq\Lambda\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}\leq\Lambda^{2}\min\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\},
then

\displaystyle\angle(P_{Q},P_{R})\lesssim_{M,\Lambda}\varepsilon.

3.
Reifenberg parametrisations in Hilbert space

In this section we discuss the Reifenberg parametrization theorem of David and Toro
[
DT12
]
. This was one of the main tool in
[
AS18
]
, and we need a version to hold in Hilbert space. It turns out most of the arguments of David and Toro make sense in Hilbert space. We will describe the necessary changes and the remaining details will be outlined in the Appendix.

3.1.
CCBP

The main result of
[
DT12
]
is stated in terms of a
coherent collection of balls and planes
(CCBP), defined below.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
P_{0}
be a
d
-plane in
H
. Let
\{x_{j,k}\},\ k\in\mathbb{N},\ j\in J_{k},
be a collection of points in
H
such that

(3.1)

\displaystyle|x_{i,k}-x_{j,k}|\geq 10^{-k}\eqqcolon r_{k}\quad\text{for all}\ i,j\in J_{k}.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N},\ j\in J_{k}
, let
B_{j,k}=B(x_{j,k},r_{k})
and
P_{j,k}
be a
d
-plane such that
x_{j,k}\in P_{j,k}.
The triple
\mathscr{P}=(P_{0},\{B_{j,k}\},\{P_{j,k}\}),\ k\in\mathbb{N},\ j\in J_{k}
, is said to be a
coherent collection of balls and planes
(CCBP) if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in J_{k}
,

(3.2)

\displaystyle x_{j,k}\in V^{2}_{k-1},

where

(3.3)

\displaystyle V_{k}^{\lambda}=\bigcup_{j\in J_{k}}\lambda B_{j,k}.

(2)

For
j\in J_{0}
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{j,0},P_{0})\leq\varepsilon.

(3)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
i,j\in J_{k}
such that
|x_{i,k}-x_{j,k}|\leq 100r_{k},

(3.5)

\displaystyle d_{x_{j,k},100r_{k}}(P_{i,k},P_{j,k})\leq\varepsilon.

(4)

For
j\in J_{0}
and
x\in P_{0}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P_{0})\leq 2,

(3.6)

\displaystyle d_{x_{j,0},100}(P_{j,0},P_{0})\leq\varepsilon.

(5)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in J_{k}
and
j\in J_{k+1}
such that
|x_{i,k}-x_{j,k+1}|\leq 2r_{k},

(3.7)

\displaystyle d_{x_{i,k},20r_{k}}(P_{i,k},P_{j,k+1})\leq\varepsilon.

Remark 3.2
.

In practice, to verify properties (2)-(5), we will show
\varepsilon_{k}(x_{j,k})<\varepsilon
for all
k\geq 0
and
j\in J_{k}
, where

\varepsilon_{k}(x)=\sup\{d_{x_{i,l},10^{4}r_{l}}(P_{j,k},P_{i,l})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\in J_{k},\ \mathinner{\!\left\lvert l-k\right\rvert}\leq 2,\ i\in J_{l},x\in 100B_{j,k}\cap 100B_{i,l}\}.

3.2.
Partition of unity

The main alteration to the construction in
[
DT12
]
is in defining a partition of unity, adapted to the collection of balls in the CCBP. Let us outline the David-Toro construction, see also
[
DT12
, Chapter 3]
. Let
k\geq 0.
David and Toro begin by defining a collection of smooth bump functions
\theta_{j,k}
supported on the balls
10B_{j,k}
,
j\in J_{k}
. They then introduce a collection of maximally separated points
x_{l,k}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus V_{k}^{9}
, a collection of balls
B_{l,k}
centred on
x_{l,k}
, and a collection of function
\theta_{l,k}
supported on
10B_{l,k}
. These are indexed by
l\in L_{k}.
The supports of the function
\theta_{j,k},\ j\in J_{k}\cup L_{k}
have bounded overlap with constant depending on
n
, which allows them to construct a partition of unity for the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Since we are working in infinite dimensional Hilbert space, we have no hope of controlling the overlap of balls centred on an arbitrary collection of separated points. We can however, control the overlap of the balls
10B_{j,k},\ j\in J_{k}
, using Lemma
2.14
, since nearby points must lie close to a
d
-plane. We will use this fact to construct a partition of unity by the following lemma, which is a version of
[
ENV18
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space. Let
\Gamma\geq 1
,
r>0
, and let
\{10B(x_{i},r)\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of balls with bounded overlap, so that

\sum_{i\in I}\mathds{1}_{10B(x_{i},r)}(x)\leq\Gamma

for all
x\in H
. Then there are smooth Lipschitz functions
\phi_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow[0,1]
such that

(1)

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\phi_{i}\subseteq 10B(x_{i},r)
.

(2)

\sum_{i\in I}\phi_{i}=1
on
\bigcup_{i\in I}8B(x_{i},r).

(3)

|D^{m}\phi_{i}(y)|\lesssim_{\Gamma,m}r^{-m}
for all
y\in H
and
m\geq 0.

Here,
D^{m}\phi_{i}(y)
denotes the
m^{\text{th}}
Frechét derivative of the function
\phi_{i}
at the point
y.

Proof.

Let
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a standard
C^{\infty}
bump function with compact support in
B(0,10)
so that
\phi(y)=1
for all
y\in B(0,8)
, and
0\leq\phi(y)\leq 1
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Notice then that
|\phi^{(m)}(y)|\lesssim_{m}1
for all
y\in\mathbb{R},
where
\phi^{(m)}
denotes the
m^{\text{th}}
derivative of
\phi.
For each
i\in I
, define

\tilde{\phi}_{i}(y)=\phi\left(\frac{|y-x_{i}|}{r}\right).

Since
H
is a Hilbert space, it has a smooth norm (see for example see
[
FM02
]
). Using this, along with the estimates for
\phi^{(m)}
, we have

(3.8)

\displaystyle|D^{m}\tilde{\phi}_{i}(y)|\lesssim_{m}r^{-m}

for all
y\in H
and
m\geq 0.
Also,
\tilde{\phi}_{i}
has compact support in
10B(x_{i},r)
and is identically equal to 1 on
8B(x_{i},r)
and so

(3.9)

\displaystyle\Phi(y)\coloneqq\sum_{i\in I}\tilde{\phi}_{i}(y)\geq 1,\mbox{ for all }y\in\bigcup_{i\in I}8B(x_{i},r).

Since the balls
10B(x_{i},r)
have bounded overlap, we have
\Phi(y)\leq\Gamma.
Thus, we can define

\phi_{i}(y)=\tilde{\phi}_{i}(y)/\Phi(y).

In this way,
(1)
follows from the same statement for
\tilde{\phi}_{i}
, and
(2)
follows from (
3.9
) and the definition of
\phi_{i}
. Finally,
(3)
follows from (
3.8
), (
3.9
), and the fact that the balls
10B(x_{i},r)
have bounded overlap.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(P_{0},\{B_{j,k}\},\{P_{j,k}\}),\ k\in\mathbb{N},\ j\in J_{k}
, be a CCBP. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the balls
\{10B_{j,k}\}
have bounded overlap (with constant depending only on
d
), and there exists a collection of smooth Lipschitz function
\{\theta_{j,k}\}_{j\in J_{k}}
such that the following holds.

(1)

For each
j\in J_{k}
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\theta_{j,k}\subseteq 10B_{j,k}.

(2)

For
y\in V_{k}^{8}
we have

(3.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)=1.

(3)

For each
j\in J_{k}
and
y\in H
we have

(3.12)

\displaystyle|D^{m}\theta_{j,k}(y)|\lesssim_{d}r_{k}^{-m}.

Proof.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}.
By Lemma
3.3
, it suffices to show that the collection of balls
\{10B_{j,k},\ j\in J_{k},
have bounded overlap (with constant only dependent on
d
). Let
x\in H
and let
J_{k}^{\prime}=\{j_{0},j_{1},\dots,j_{N}\}\subseteq J_{k}
be such that

x\in\bigcap_{i=0}^{N}B(x_{j_{i},k},10r_{k}).

Each
j_{i}\in J_{k}^{\prime}
satisfies
|x_{j_{i},k}-x_{j_{0},k}|\leq 20r_{k}\leq 100r_{k}
, so by Definition
3.1
(3) (for
\varepsilon
small enough),

\text{dist}(x_{j_{i},k},P_{i_{0},k})\leq 100\varepsilon r_{k}\leq r_{k}/8.

By (
3.1
), the balls
B(x_{j_{i},k},r_{k}/4),\ j_{i}\in J_{k}^{\prime}
are disjoint, and so, satisfy the conditions of Lemma
2.14
with
P=P_{j_{0},k}
and
B=B(x,30r_{k})
. This gives

Nr_{k}\sim\sum_{j_{i}\in J_{k}^{\prime}}r_{\frac{1}{4}B_{j_{i},k}}^{d}\lesssim_{d}r_{k}

and we conclude
N\lesssim_{d}1
.
∎

In
[
DT12
]
, the functions
\theta_{l,k},\ l\in L_{k}
are grouped into a single function
\psi_{k}
, after which there is no mention of the defining functions
\theta_{l,k}.
We shall introduce a similar function, also denoted
\psi_{k}
, which shares the same properties. This may seem artificial, but we hope that it makes comparing the two papers easier. For
k\geq 0
, define a function
\psi_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting

\psi_{k}(y)=1-\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y).

In this way

\psi_{k}(y)+\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)=1\quad\text{for all}\ y\in H.

Additionally, we have the following properties of
\psi_{k}.
Compare these to the corresponding items;
[
DT12
, Equation 3.12, Equation 3.15]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
k\geq 0.
The function
\psi_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
C^{\infty}
and satisfies

\psi_{k}(y)=0\quad\text{for all}\ x\in V_{k}^{8}

and

|D^{m}\psi_{k}(y)|\lesssim_{m}r_{k}^{-m}

for all
y\in H
and
m\geq 0.

Proof.

Since the balls
10B_{j,k},

j\in J_{k},
have bounded overlap and each of the functions
\theta_{j,k}
is
C^{\infty},
it follows that
\psi_{k}
are
C^{\infty}
. The estimates on the derivative follows from bounded overlap with the estimates on the function
\theta_{j,k}.

∎

3.3.
The Reifenberg map

For
k\geq 0,
we define a function
\sigma_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow H
by setting

\sigma_{k}(y)=y+\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)[\pi_{j,k}(y)-y]=\psi_{k}(y)y+\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)\pi_{j,k}(y),

and a function
f_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P_{0}\rightarrow H
by setting

f_{0}(y)=y\quad\text{and}\quad f_{k+1}=\sigma_{k}\circ f_{k}.

Let
y\in H.
Since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\theta_{j,k}\subseteq 10B_{j,k},
if
j\in J_{k}
is such that
\theta_{j,k}(y)\not=0,
then
y\in 10B_{j,k}.
This implies
|y-\pi_{j,k}(y)|\leq 10r_{k}
, which, along with the bounded overlap of
\{10B_{j,k}\}
, implies

|\sigma_{k}(y)-y|\leq 10r_{k}.

It follows that

(3.13)

\displaystyle||f_{k+1}-f_{k}||_{\infty}\leq 10r_{k},

so the functions
f_{k}
converge uniformly to a continuous function
f
on
P_{0}.
We now state the Reifenberg parametrization theorem. We include each of the properties used in
[
AS18
]
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
(P_{0},\{B_{j,k}\},\{P_{j,k}\})
be a CCBP in
H
. Recall the definition,

\varepsilon_{k}(x)=\sup\{d_{x_{i,l},10^{4}r_{l}}(P_{j,k},P_{i,l})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\in J_{k},\ \mathinner{\!\left\lvert l-k\right\rvert}\leq 2,\ i\in J_{k},x\in 100B_{j,k}\cap 100B_{i,l}\}.

There is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
\varepsilon_{k}(x_{j,k})<\varepsilon
for all
k\geq 0
and
j\in J_{k}
, then the map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P_{0}\rightarrow H
(from above) is bijective on its image and the following hold.

(1)

We have

(3.14)

E_{\infty}=\bigcap_{K=1}^{\infty}\overline{\bigcup_{k=K}^{\infty}\{x_{j,k}\}_{j\in J_{k}}}\subseteq\Sigma=f(P_{0}).

(2)

f(x)=x
when
\emph{dist}(z,P_{0})>2.

(3)

There is
0<\tau<1
so that if
x,y\in P_{0}
, and
|x-y|\leq 1
, then

(3.15)

\frac{1}{4}|x-y|^{1+\tau}\leq|f(x)-f(y)|\leq 10|x-y|^{1-\tau}.

(4)

|f(x)-x|\lesssim\varepsilon
for
x\in H.

(5)

For
x\in P_{0},

f(x)=\lim_{k}\sigma_{k}\circ\dots\circ\sigma_{0}.

(6)

For
k\geq 0,

(3.16)

\sigma_{k}(y)=y\ \text{for}\ y\in H\setminus V^{10}_{k}.

(7)

Let
\Sigma_{0}=P_{0}
and

(3.17)

\Sigma_{k}=\sigma_{k}(\Sigma_{k-1}).

There is a Lipschitz function
A_{j,k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P_{j,k}\cap 49B_{j,k}\rightarrow P_{j,k}^{\perp}
such that

(3.18)

\displaystyle|A_{j,k}(x_{j,k})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k},

(3.19)

\displaystyle|DA_{j,k}(y)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\quad\text{for}\ y\in P_{j,k}\cap 49B_{j,k},

and if
\Gamma_{j,k}
is its graph over
P_{j,k}
, then

(3.20)

\Sigma_{k}\cap D(x_{j,k},P_{j,k},49r_{k})=\Gamma_{k}\cap D(x_{j,k},P_{j,k},49r_{k})

where

(3.21)

D(x_{j,k},P_{j,k},49r_{k})=\{z+w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}z\in P\cap B(x,r),\ w\in P^{\perp}\cap B(0,r)\}.

(8)

For
k\geq 0
and
y\in\Sigma_{k}
, there is an affine
d
-plane
P
through
y
and a
C\varepsilon
-Lipschitz and
C^{2}
-function
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\rightarrow P^{\perp}
so that if
\Gamma
is the graph of
A
over
P
, then

(3.22)

\Sigma_{k}\cap B(y,19r_{k})=\Gamma\cap B(y,19r_{k}).

(9)

The surface
\Sigma=f(P_{0})
is
C\varepsilon
-Reifenberg flat in the sense that for all
z\in\Sigma,
and
t\in(0,1),
there is
P=P(z,t)
so that
d_{B(z,t)}(\Sigma,P)\leq\varepsilon.

(10)

For all
y\in\Sigma_{k}
,

(3.23)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{k}(y)-y|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}

and moreover,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\emph{dist}(y,\Sigma)\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k},\ \text{for}\ y\in\Sigma_{k}.

(11)

For
k\geq 0
and
y\in\Sigma_{k}\cap V_{k}^{8},
choose
i\in J_{k}
such that
y\in 10B_{i,k}.
Then

(3.25)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(y)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{k}(y)r_{k}.

(12)

For
x,y\in\Sigma_{k}
,

\angle(T\Sigma_{k}(x),T\Sigma_{k}(y))\lesssim\varepsilon\frac{|x-y|}{r_{k}},

where
T\Sigma_{k}(x)
denotes the tangent space at
x\in\Sigma_{k}.

(13)

For
x\in\Sigma
and
r>0
,

\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\Sigma\cap B(x,r))\gtrsim r^{d}.

It is possible to show that
f
is bi-Lipschitz, when there is better control on the angles between the planes
P_{j,k}.
This is made precise below.

Theorem 3.7

(
[
DT12
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

With the same notation and assumptions as Theorem
3.6
, assume additionally that there exists
M<\infty
such that

\sum_{k\geq 0}\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}(f_{k}(z))^{2}\leq M\ \text{for}\ z\in P_{0}

with

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}^{\prime}(x)=\sup\{\d_{x_{i,l},10^{4}r_{l}}(P_{j,k},P_{i,l})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\in J_{k},\ \mathinner{\!\left\lvert l-k\right\rvert}\leq 2,\ i\in J_{l},x\in 10B_{j,k}\cap 10B_{i,l}\}.

Then
f\colon P_{0}\rightarrow\Sigma
is
C(M,d)
-bi-Lipschitz.

With the modifications outlined above, most of the arguments from
[
DT12
]
give the required result. For the reader’s convenience, we include the key details for the above results in the Appendix.

4.
Estimating curvature for Reifenberg flat sets

In this section we prove Theorem
1.9
. As mentioned in the introduction, it suffices that prove Theorem
1.9
for subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n},
as long as the constants are independent of
n
. The reason we prove Theorem
1.9
for subsets of Euclidean space, rather than working directly in Hilbert space, is so that we can state directly from
[
AS18
]
any result we may need. While most of the preliminary results from
[
AS18
]
remain true in Hilbert space, we wanted to save the reader from having to check this.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose for any
n\geq 2
,
1\leq d<n
,
1\leq p<p(d)
and
C_{0}>1
, that there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that (
1.10
) holds with constants
d,p
, and
C_{0},
for any
(\varepsilon,d,\delta_{0})
-Reifenberg flat surface
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with constants independent of
n
. Then, there exists
C\geq 1
so that (
1.10
) holds with constant
d,p,
and
C_{0}
for any
(C\varepsilon,d,\delta_{0})
-Reifenberg flat surface
\Sigma\subseteq H.

Let
n\geq 2,

1\leq d<n
,
1\leq p<p(d)
and
C_{0}>1
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be a constant to be chosen sufficiently small and
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an
(\varepsilon,d,\delta_{0})
-Reifenberg flat surface. Let
X_{k}
be a sequence of maximally
\rho^{k}
-separated nets (with
\rho
as in Theorem
2.1
) and let
\mathscr{D}
be the Christ-David for
\Sigma
from Theorem
2.1
. Since
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, Theorem
2.1
(4) now holds. Let
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
and assume without loss of generality that
0\in Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
. From now on, when we write
\mathscr{D}
we mean those cubes
Q
in
\mathscr{D}
so that
Q\subseteq Q_{0}.
We may also assume
\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})<\infty
since otherwise the statement is trivial. We are left to show

(4.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{\Sigma}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})

for
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with constant independent of
n
. Since we are now working in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we can state those results in Sections 5,6 and 7
[
AS18
]
whose constants are independent of
n
. The most import of these is the construction of a sequence of approximating surfaces
\Sigma^{N}
(Definition
4.6
) for
\Sigma
and the local Lipschitz description of these approximating surfaces (Lemma
4.9
). The basic idea is to push the
\beta
-number estimates for
\Sigma
onto
\beta
-number estimates for
\Sigma^{N}
using Lemma
2.13
. Because (locally)
\Sigma^{N}
is approximately a Lipschitz graph over the previous surface, we can apply Dorronsoro’s Theorem to control the
\beta
-number on
\Sigma^{N}.
The error term in Lemma
2.13
is controlled by Lemma
4.4
(5).

4.1.
Setup and definitions

Let
M>1
be a constant to be chosen sufficiently large. For each
Q\in\mathscr{D},
let
P_{Q}^{\prime}
be the
d
-plane such that

\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})=\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(MB_{Q},P_{Q}^{\prime})<\varepsilon

and
P_{Q}
be the
d
-plane through
x_{Q}
such that

\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(MB_{Q},P_{Q})\leq 2\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})<2\varepsilon.

Let
P_{0}=P_{Q_{0}}.
Let
\alpha>0.

Definition 4.2

(The stopping-time condition)
.

For
Q\in\mathscr{D}
we construct a stopping-time region
S_{Q}
as follows. First, add
Q
to
S_{Q}.
Then, inductively, we add cubes
R
to
S_{Q}
if:

•

R^{(1)}\in S_{Q}.

•

We have
\angle(P_{T},P_{Q})<\alpha
for all siblings
T
of
R
.

4.2.
Families of cubes

For each non-negative integer
N
, we define collections of cubes Layer(
N
), Stop(
N
), and Up(
N
), and a collection of stopping-time regions
\mathscr{F}^{N}
as follows. Let
\tau\in(0,1)
be a constant to be chosen sufficiently small. First, set

\text{Stop}(-1)=\mathscr{D}_{0}.

Assume that Stop(
N-1
) has been defined for some
N\geq 0.
Let
\text{Layer}(N)
be the collection of cubes from the stopping-time regions defined by those cubes in
\text{Stop}(N-1),
that is,

\displaystyle\text{Layer}(N)=\bigcup\{S_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\text{Stop}(N-1)\}.

Then, define
\text{Stop}(N)
to be

\displaystyle\text{Stop}(N)=\{Q\in\mathscr{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\ \text{max. such that there is}\

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}\in\text{Child}(Q^{(1)})\ \text{with}\ \ell(Q^{\prime})<\tau d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(Q^{\prime})\}.

Remark 4.3
.

Note the non-standard notation. At the minute,
\text{Stop}(N)
is not used to denote the minimal cubes of some stopping-time region but rather a collection of disjoint cubes spread out over
\Sigma
.

The cubes
\text{Up}(N)
will be those
Q\in\mathscr{D}
which contain some cube in
\text{Stop}(N).
Define
\text{Up}(-1)=\emptyset
. Then, assuming Up(
N-1
) has been defined for some
N\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle\text{Up}(N)=\text{Up}(N-1)\cup\{Q\in\mathscr{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\supseteq R\ \text{for some}\ R\in\text{Layer}(N)\cup\text{Stop}(N)\}.

For each
Q\in\text{Stop}(N-1)
define an extension of the stopping time region
S_{Q}
by setting,

S_{Q}^{\prime}=\{R\in\text{Up}(N)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\subseteq Q\}\supseteq S_{Q}.

In particular, we add to
S_{Q}
all those cubes between
\min(S_{Q})
and
\text{Stop}(N).
It is easy to check that
S_{Q}^{\prime}
still defines a stopping-time region in the sense of Definition
2.4
. Finally, for
N\geq 0,
set

\mathscr{F}^{N}=\{S_{Q}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\text{Stop}(N-1)\}.

By construction

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}=\bigcup_{N\geq 0}\mathscr{F}^{N}.

The reason Azzam and Schul work with
S_{Q}^{\prime}
and not just
S_{Q}
is because they want to ensure nearby minimal cubes have comparable sizes. For us, this is important as it allows us to prove minimal cubes have bounded overlap with constant only depending on
d
. If we work with the regular stopping-time regions it, is possible for nearby cubes to vary wildly in size.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
N\geq 0
,
C_{1}>1
and
0<\tau<\min\{\tau_{0},(16+8C_{1})^{-1}\},
where
\tau_{0}=\frac{1}{4(2+\rho^{-1})}
.

(1)

If
Q,R\in\emph{Stop}(N)
are such that
C_{1}B_{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{R}\not=\emptyset
, then
\ell(Q)\sim\ell(R).

(2)

The balls
C_{1}B_{Q},\ Q\in\emph{Stop}(N)
, have bounded overlap in
\Sigma
, with constant depending on
d.

(3)

If
x\in\Sigma
and
d_{\emph{Up}(N)}(x)>0
, then there is
Q\in\emph{Stop}(N)
so that
x\in Q.

(4)

If
Q\in\emph{Stop}(N)
then

\ell(Q)\sim d_{\emph{Up}(N)}(Q).

(5)

We have

\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{Q\in\emph{Stop}(N)}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim_{d,\alpha,\varepsilon}\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}).

Proof.

Items
(1)
and
(4)
can be found in
[
AS18
, Section 5]
, and item
(5)
is
[
AS18
, Proposition 8.1]
. Item
(3)
can also be found in
[
AS18
, Section 5]
, however this relies on
[
AS18
, Corollary 5.8]
, whose proof contains an error. Thus, we shall prove both
(2)
and
(3)
.

Let us start with
(2)
. Fix
x\in\Sigma
and let

\mathscr{D}_{x}=\{Q\in\text{Stop}(N)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in Q\}.

If
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{x}
, by
(1)
, we have
\ell(Q)\sim\ell(Q^{\prime}).
Let
r=\sup_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{x}}\ell(Q).
It follows that
\{c_{0}B_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{x}\}
are pairwise disjoint balls contained in
B=B(x,(c_{0}+C_{1})r).
For
\varepsilon>0
small enough, recalling that
\Sigma
is
(\varepsilon,d)
-Reifenberg flat, we can find a
d
-plane
P
such that

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq\varepsilon(c_{0}+C_{1})r\leq\frac{c_{0}\ell(Q)}{2}

for all
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{x}.
So, by Lemma
2.14
,

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{D}_{x}r^{d}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{x}}r_{c_{0}B_{Q}}^{d}\lesssim_{d}r^{d}.

In particular,
\#\mathscr{D}_{x}\lesssim_{d}1.

Now for
(3)
. We shall prove the contrapositive. Fix
x\in\Sigma
and suppose
x
is not contained in any cube from
\text{Stop}(N).
Let
R_{j}\in\mathscr{D}
be a sequence of cubes such that
x\in R_{j}
and
\ell(R_{j})\downarrow 0.
By assumption, each
R_{j}
is not contained in any cube from
\text{Stop}(N)
, and so
\ell(R_{j})\geq\tau d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(R_{j})
. For each
j
, let
x_{j}\in R_{j}
be so that
d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(x_{j})\leq d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(R_{j})+j^{-1}.
Then

(4.3)

\displaystyle d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(x)

\displaystyle\leq|x-x_{j}|+d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(x_{j})\leq|x-x_{j}|+d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(R_{j})+j^{-1}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\leq(2+\tau^{-1})\ell(R_{j})+j^{-1}\rightarrow 0,

and so
d_{\text{Layer}(N)}(x)=0.
Since
\text{Layer}(N)\subseteq\text{Up}(N),
this implies
d_{\text{Up}(N)}=0
as required. ∎

4.3.
The sequence of approximating surfaces

We can now construct the sequence of approximating surfaces alluded to at the beginning of this section. For each
N\geq 0
we will construct a surface
\Sigma^{N}
using Theorem
3.6
, where we use the centres of the cubes from
\text{Up}(N)
as our nets.

Definition 4.5
.

For
k\geq 0
and integer, let
s(k)
be such that
5\rho^{s(k)}\leq r_{k}\leq 5\rho^{s(k)-1}.
We define
\text{Up}(N)_{k}=\mathscr{D}_{s(k)}\cap\text{Up}(N)
and let
\mathscr{X}_{k}^{N}=\{x_{j,k}\}_{j\in J^{N}_{k}}
be a maximally
r_{k}
separated net for

\mathscr{C}_{k}^{N}\coloneqq\{x_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\text{Up}(N)_{k}\}.

In
[
AS18
]
, it was shown that the points
\mathscr{X}_{k}^{N},\ k\geq 0
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
3.6
for each
N\geq 0.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
P_{0}=P_{Q_{0}}
and for
N\geq 0
, let
\Sigma^{N},\Sigma_{k}^{N},\ \sigma^{N},\ \sigma_{k}^{N}
be the resulting surfaces and functions from Theorem
3.6
, respectively.

Lemma 4.7

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 6.2]
)
.

If
d_{\emph{Up}(N)}(x)=0
then
x\in\Sigma\cap\Sigma^{N}.

Lemma 4.8

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 6.7]
)
.

Let
x\in\Sigma,
then

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,\Sigma^{N})\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau}d_{\emph{Up}(N)}(x).

The following is a local Lipschitz description of the surfaces
\Sigma^{N}.

Lemma 4.9

(
[
AS18
, Theorem 6.10]
)
.

Let
C_{2}>1
. There exists
M\geq 1
large enough and
\tau
small enough so that the following holds. Let
Q\in S\in\mathscr{F}^{N}
and
P
be a
d
-plane such that
d_{B_{Q}}(P,P_{Q})<\theta.
If
\alpha,\theta
and
\varepsilon
are small enough (depending on
C_{2}
), then there is a
C(\alpha+\theta)
-Lipschitz map
A_{P,Q}^{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\rightarrow P^{\perp}
that is zero outside
P\cap\pi(B(x_{Q},2C_{2}\ell(Q)))
such that if
\Gamma^{N}_{P,Q}
is the graph of
A_{P,Q}^{N}
along
P
, then

(4.6)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{N}\cap D(x_{Q},P,C_{2}\ell(Q))=\Gamma_{P,Q}^{N}\cap D(\pi(x_{Q}),P,C_{2}\ell(Q)).

If
Q=Q(S),
we will set
A_{S}=A_{P,Q(S)}^{N}
and
\Gamma_{S}=\Gamma_{P,Q(S)}^{N}.

4.4.
Dorronsoro’s Theorem

We describe the Dorronsoro-type theorem we will use. This is due to Hytönen, Li and Naor
[
HLN16
]
. For a Lipschitz function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert f\right\rVert}_{\text{Lip}}
denote its Lipschitz constant, and for
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
1<p<\infty
, set

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Omega_{f,p}(B)=\inf_{A}\left(\fint_{B}\left(\frac{|f(y)-A(y)|}{r_{B}}\right)^{p}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

where the infimum is taken over all affine maps
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Additionally, for
p=\infty
, define

\Omega_{f,\infty}(B)=\inf_{A}\sup_{y\in B}\frac{|f(y)-A(y)|}{r_{B}}.

As a consequence of
[
HLN16
, Theorem 40]
, we get the following.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty
and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Lipschitz function with bounded support. Then

(4.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Omega_{f,p}(B(x,r))^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}dx\lesssim_{d,p}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}f)^{d}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert f\right\rVert}^{2}_{\emph{Lip}}

We can discretize the above theorem as follows. The proof is standard.

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty
and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Lipschitz function with bounded support. Let
\mathscr{I}
denote the collection of dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for each
I\in\mathscr{I},
let
B_{I}=B(x_{I},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)),
where
x_{I}
denotes the centre of
I
. Then

(4.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathscr{I}}\Omega_{f,p}(3B_{I})^{p}\ell(I)^{d}\lesssim_{d,p}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}f)^{d}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert f\right\rVert}^{2}_{\emph{Lip}}.

Later on, it will be useful for us to bound
\Omega_{f,\infty}
in terms of
\Omega_{f,p},
in analogy with Lemma
2.9
. This will be possible if
f
is bi-Lipschitz on its image.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty
and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a function which is bi-Lipschitz onto its image, with bi-Lipschitz constant
L
. Then, for
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},

\Omega_{f,\infty}(\tfrac{1}{2}B)\lesssim_{L}\Omega_{f,1}(B)^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\lesssim_{p}\Omega_{f,p}(B)^{\frac{1}{d+1}}.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
r_{B}=1.
Let us begin with the left-most inequality. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow H
be an affine function so that

\fint_{B}|f(y)-A(y)|\,dx\leq\Omega_{f,1}(B)+\varepsilon.

Let
y\in\tfrac{1}{2}B
be the point so that
\tau\coloneqq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(f(y),A(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is maximal. By the triangle inequality,
B(f(y),\tau/2)\subseteq\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,A(\mathbb{R}^{d}))>t\}
for all
0<t<\tau/2.
Also, using that
f
is bi-Lipschitz, for each
z\in B(y,\tau/2L)\subseteq B
we know
f(z)\in B(f(y),\tau/2).
Hence,

B(y,\tau/2L)\subseteq\{x\in B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(f(x),A(\mathbb{R}^{d}))>t\}\subseteq\{x\in B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|f(y)-A(y)|>t\}

for all
0<t<\tau/2
, and so,

(4.10)

\displaystyle 1\lesssim_{L}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{d}(B(y,\tau/2L))}{\tau^{d}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\tau^{d+1}}\int_{0}^{\frac{\tau}{2}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\{x\in B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|f(y)-A(y)|>t\})\,dt

(4.11)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\tau^{d+1}}\int_{B}|f(y)-A(y)|\,dx\leq\frac{1}{\tau^{d+1}}\left[\Omega_{f,1}(B)+\varepsilon\right].

Taking
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
and rearranging gives the first inequality since

\Omega_{f,\infty}(\tfrac{1}{2}B)=\tau\lesssim\Omega_{f,1}(B)^{\frac{1}{d+1}}.

We now prove the second inequality. The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
2.10
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an affine function so that

\left(\fint_{B}|f(y)-A(y)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\Omega_{f,p}(B)+\varepsilon.

The function
x\mapsto x^{p}
is convex, so, by Jensen’s inequality,

(4.12)

\displaystyle\Omega_{f,1}(B)\leq\fint_{B}|f(y)-A(y)|\,dx\lesssim\left(\fint_{B}|f(y)-A(y)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\Omega_{f,p}(B)+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, this finishes the proof.
∎

If
\Sigma
is a bi-Lipschitz image of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can give an upper bound for the
\beta
-numbers of
\Sigma
by a corresponding
\Omega
-number.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty,

f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an
L
-bi-Lipschitz function and
\Sigma=f(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
Let
x\in\Sigma,\ r>0,
and let
B
be a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that contains
f^{-1}(\Sigma\cap B(x,r)).
Then

(4.13)

\displaystyle\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(B(x,r))\lesssim\left(\frac{\mathscr{H}^{d}(I)}{r^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Omega_{f,p}(B).

Proof.

Let
A
be an affine function which infimises (
4.7
) for
f
,
p
and
B
. Since
f
is bi-Lipschitz,
\Sigma
is Ahlfors
d
-regular (with constant depending on
L
) and so
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E)\sim_{L}\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E)
for all
E\subseteq\Sigma.
Hence,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\beta^{d,p}_{\Sigma}(B(x,r))^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\{y\in\Sigma\cap B(x,r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,A(\mathbb{R}^{d})>tr\})t^{p-1}\,dt

(4.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\{y\in\Sigma\cap B(x,r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,A(\mathbb{R}^{d})>tr\})t^{p-1}\,dt

(4.16)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{\Sigma\cap B(x,r)}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,A(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}{r}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}(y)

(4.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{f^{-1}(\Sigma\cap B(x,r))}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(f(z),A(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}{r}\right)^{p}J_{f}(z)\,dz

(4.18)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{L}\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{B}\left(\frac{|f(z)-A(z)|}{r}\right)^{p}\,dz

(4.19)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathscr{H}^{d}(B)}{r^{d}}\Omega_{f,p}(B)^{p}.

∎

We now have all the ingredients to finish the proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

We use the collected facts to prove (
4.1
). Notice, by (
4.2
), we have

(4.20)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}=\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}^{N}}\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

We begin by bounding the sum over cubes in a particular stopping-time region. Let
N\geq 0
and
S\in\mathscr{F}^{N}.
For
Q\in S,
let
x_{Q}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{N}
be the closest point to
x_{Q}
and let
B^{\prime}_{Q}=B(x_{Q}^{\prime},\ell(Q)).
By Lemma
4.8
,
C_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq 2C_{0}B_{Q}^{\prime}
, so Lemma
2.13
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{\Sigma^{N}}^{d,p}(2C_{0}B_{Q}^{\prime})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\hskip-40.00006pt+\sum_{Q\in S}\left(\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d}}\int_{\Sigma\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}}\left(\frac{\text{dist}(y,\Sigma^{N})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(y)\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}.

The proof now splits into two claims.

Claim 1:

(4.21)

\displaystyle I_{2}\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q(S))^{d}.

Proof of Claim 1.

By Lemma
2.7
, we may assume that
p\geq 2.
Let
y\in\Sigma.
Assume first that there exists
R\in\text{Stop}(N)
such that
y\in R.
By Lemma
4.8
, and using that fact that
R\in\text{Up}(N)
(since
\text{Stop}(N)\subseteq\text{Up}(N)
), we have

(4.22)

\displaystyle\text{dist}(y,\Sigma^{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:dSS^N}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon d_{\text{Up}(N)}(y)\leq\varepsilon\left(\ell(R)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,R)\right)=\varepsilon\ell(R).

On the other hand, if there is no
R\in\text{Stop}(N)
such that
y\in R,
then by Lemma
4.4
(3) we have
d_{\text{Up}(N)}(y)=0.
In particular,
\text{dist}(y,\Sigma^{N})=0
by Lemma
4.7
. Using the above, the bounded overlap of the cubes in
\text{Stop}(N)
(Lemma
4.4
(2)), and Lemma
2.6
, we get

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Q\in S}\left(\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d}}\int_{\Sigma\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}_{R}(y)\left(\frac{\text{dist}(y,\Sigma^{N})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(y)\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{Q\in S}\left(\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\int_{R}\left(\frac{\text{dist}(y,\Sigma^{N})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(y)\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:deR}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon\sum_{Q\in S}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d+p}}{\ell(Q)^{d+p}}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{Q\in S}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d\frac{2}{p}+2}}{\ell(Q)^{d(\frac{2}{p}-1)+2}},

where the final inequality follows from the fact that we have assumed
p\geq 2.

Our next step is to swap the order of summation. First, for fixed
R\in\text{Stop}(N)
and
k\in\mathbb{Z},
we show

(4.23)

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{D}_{R,k}=\#\{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\cap S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\}\lesssim 1.

Indeed, let
R\in\text{Stop}(N)
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}.
For any
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{R,k}
we have
Q\in\text{Up}(N)
since
Q\in S\in\mathscr{F}^{N}
. Combining this with Lemma
4.4
(4), and the fact that
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset
, we get

(4.24)

\displaystyle\ell(R)\sim d_{\text{Up}(N)}(R)\leq\ell(Q)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q,R)\leq 3C_{0}\ell(Q).

Thus, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
c_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq B(x_{R},C\ell(Q))\coloneqq B
. Since
\Sigma
is
(\varepsilon,d)
-Reifenberg flat, we can find a
d
-plane
P
through
x_{B}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,P)\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q)
for all
y\in\Sigma\cap B.
In particular,

\text{dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq\tfrac{c_{0}\ell(Q)}{2}

for
\varepsilon
small enough. Since the balls
\{c_{0}B_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{R,k}}
are disjoint, the above implies that they satisfy the conditions of Lemma
2.14
, which gives

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{D}_{R,k}(5\rho^{k})^{d}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{R,k}}r_{c_{0}B_{Q}}^{d}\lesssim r_{B}^{d}\lesssim(5\rho^{k})^{d}

and proves (
4.23
).

We return to estimating
I_{2}.
By our restrictions on
p
we have
p<2d/(d-2),
or equivalently,
d(2/p-1)+2>0.
Combining this, (
4.23
), and (
4.24
), we can swap the order of integration and sum over a geometric series to get,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:RleqQ}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
5\rho^{k}\gtrsim\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\cap S\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d\frac{2}{p}+2}}{\ell(Q)^{d(\frac{2}{p}-1)+2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{vol1}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d\frac{2}{p}+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
5\rho^{k}\gtrsim\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\rho^{-k[d(\frac{2}{p}-1)+2]}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}.

Now, suppose
R\in\text{Stop}(N)
is such that
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset.
Let
x^{\prime}_{R}
be the point in
\Sigma^{N}
closest to
x_{R},
and let
P_{S}=P_{Q(S)}
(recall the definition of
P_{Q}
from the beginning of Section
4
). Since

\displaystyle|x_{R}-x_{R}^{\prime}|\lesssim\varepsilon d_{\text{Up}(N)}(x_{R})\leq\varepsilon\ell(R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:RleqQ}}}{{\lesssim}}C_{0}\varepsilon\ell(Q(S)),

taking
C_{2}
(the constant appearing in the statement of Lemma
4.9
) larger than
4C_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
small enough, we have

B(x_{R}^{\prime},\ell(R))\subseteq D(x_{Q(S)},P_{S},C_{2}\ell(Q(S))).

Let
\Gamma_{S}
be the Lipschitz graph from Lemma
4.9
. Since
\Gamma_{S}
is Ahlfors regular and the balls
\{C_{1}B_{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\}
have bounded overlap by Lemma
4.4
(2), we get

\displaystyle\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(B(x_{R}^{\prime},\ell(R))\cap\Gamma_{S})

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(N)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(D(x_{Q(S)},P_{S},C_{2}\ell(Q(S)))\cap\Gamma_{S})

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q(S))^{d}.

This proves Claim 1.
∎

We turn our attention to estimating
I_{1}.

Claim 2:

(4.25)

\displaystyle I_{1}\lesssim\alpha^{2}\ell(Q(S))^{d}.

Proof of Claim 2.

Let
P_{S}
be as above. Let
\mathscr{D}_{P_{S}}
be the usual dyadic grid for
P_{S}
, and let
\pi_{S}
denote the orthogonal projection onto
P_{S}.
For each
Q\in S,
let
I_{Q}
be the minimal cube from
\mathscr{D}_{P_{S}}
which contains
\pi_{S}(x_{Q})
and satisfies
\ell(I_{Q})\geq 8C_{0}\ell(Q).
In particular, this implies

(4.26)

\displaystyle\ell(Q)\sim\ell(I_{Q}).

Recall that
x_{Q}^{\prime}
was defined to be the closest point to
x_{Q}
in
\Sigma^{N}.
Since the orthogonal projection onto a
d
-planes are 1-Lipschitz, we can use this, Lemma
4.8
, and the fact that
Q\in\text{Up}(N)
to get

|\pi_{S}(x_{Q})-\pi_{S}(x_{Q}^{\prime})|\leq|x_{Q}-x_{Q}^{\prime}|\lesssim\varepsilon d_{\text{Up}(N)}(x_{Q})\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q).

So, for
\varepsilon
small enough, we have

\pi_{S}(2C_{0}B_{Q}^{\prime})\subseteq 3I_{Q}\subseteq 3B_{I_{Q}}.

Using this with Lemma
4.13
and the fact that for
C_{2}\geq 4C_{0}
we have
2C_{0}B_{Q}^{\prime}\subseteq C_{2}B_{Q(S)}
for all
Q\in S,
we obtain

(4.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{\Sigma^{N}}^{d,p}(2C_{0}B_{Q}^{\prime})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{Q\in S}\Omega_{A_{S},p}(3B_{I_{Q}})^{2}\ell(I_{Q})^{d}

(4.28)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{P_{S}}}\Omega_{A_{S},p}(3B_{I})^{2}\ell(I)^{d}\#\{Q\in S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{Q}=I\}.

Next, we show

(4.29)

\displaystyle\#\{Q\in S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{Q}=I\}\lesssim_{d}1.

Indeed, fix
I\in\mathscr{D}_{P_{S}}
and suppose
Q,R\in S
are such that
I_{Q}=I_{R}=I.
By (
4.26
) we have
\ell(Q)\sim\ell(R).
Using that
\pi_{S}
is bi-Lipschitz on
D(x_{Q(S)},P_{S},C_{2}\ell(Q))
(see Lemma
4.9
), along with Lemma
4.8
, and the fact that
\pi_{S}(x_{Q}),\pi_{S}(x_{R})\in I
, we have

(4.30)

\displaystyle|x_{Q}-x_{R}|

\displaystyle\leq|x_{Q}^{\prime}-x_{R}^{\prime}|+\text{dist}(x_{Q},\Sigma^{N})+\text{dist}(x_{R},\Sigma^{N})

(4.31)

\displaystyle\lesssim|\pi_{S}(x_{Q}^{\prime})-\pi_{S}(x_{R}^{\prime})|+\text{dist}(x_{Q},\Sigma^{N})+\text{dist}(x_{R},\Sigma^{N})

(4.32)

\displaystyle\lesssim|\pi_{S}(x_{Q})-\pi_{S}(x_{R})|+2\text{dist}(x_{Q},\Sigma^{N})+2\text{dist}(x_{R},\Sigma^{N})

(4.33)
