\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(I)+\varepsilon d_{\text{Up}(N)}(Q)+\varepsilon d_{\text{Up}(N)}(R)

(4.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(I).

Thus, there exists some
p\in\Sigma
and
C>0
such that
x_{Q}\in B(p,C\ell(I))
for all
Q\in S
such that
I_{Q}=I.
Since
\Sigma
is
(\varepsilon,d)
-Reifenberg flat, there is some
d
-plane
P
through
p
such that

\text{dist}(x_{Q},P)\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(I)

for all cubes in
\{Q\in S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{Q}=I\}
. Since these cubes have comparable sizes and the balls
c_{0}B_{Q}
are disjoint, another application of Lemma
2.14
gives (
4.29
).

Now, continuing on from (
4.27
), we use (
4.29
), Corollary
4.11
and the fact that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}(A_{S})\subseteq P_{S}\cap C_{2}B_{Q(S)}
, to get

(4.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{\Sigma^{N}}^{d,p}(2C_{0}B_{Q}^{\prime})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{P_{S}}}\Omega_{A_{S},p}(3B_{I})^{2}\ell(I)^{d}\lesssim\mathinner{\!\left\lVert A_{S}\right\rVert}^{2}_{\text{Lip}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}\lesssim\alpha^{2}\ell(Q(S))^{d},

which completes the proof of Claim 2.
∎

We combine Claim 1 and Claim 2 to get

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\subseteq Q_{0}}\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{F}^{N}\\
Q(S)\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{\Sigma}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\varepsilon+\alpha^{2})\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{F}^{N}\\
Q(S)\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon+\alpha^{2})\left(\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{Q\in\text{Stop}(N)}\ell(Q)^{d}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}),

where in the last inequality we used Lemma
4.4
(5) and the lower regularity of
\Sigma.
This finishes the proof of (
4.1
), and hence, the proof of Theorem
1.9
.

∎

5.
Estimating curvature for general sets

In this section we prove the Theorem
1.6
. We start by making some reductions, which are proven in the appendix. The first allows us to assume
A>6C_{0}.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose that for any
\tilde{C}_{0}>1
and
\tilde{A}>\max\{6\tilde{C}_{0},10^{5}\}
there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that (
1.8
) holds with constant
\tilde{C}_{0},\tilde{A}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}.
Then, for any
C_{0}>1
and
A>10^{5}
there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that (
1.8
) holds with constant
C_{0},A
and
\varepsilon.

The second observation allows us to prove Theorem
1.6
for subsets of Euclidean space, as long as the constants independent of the ambient dimension.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose for any
n\geq 2
,
1\leq d<n,

1\leq p<p(d),

C_{0}>1
, and
A>\max\{6C_{0},10^{5}\}
, that there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that (
1.8
) holds with constants
d,p,C_{0},A,
and
\varepsilon
, for any lower content
d
-regular set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with constants independent of
n
. Then, there exists
C\geq 1
so that (
1.8
) holds with constant
d,p,C_{0},2A
, and
C\varepsilon,
for all lower content
d
-regular sets
E\subseteq H.

Let
n\geq 2,

1\leq d<n,\ 1\leq p<p(d),\ C_{0}>1
and
A>\max\{6C_{0},10^{5}\}.
Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be lower content
d
-regular with regularity constant
c>0
. Let
X_{k}
be a sequence of maximally
\rho^{k}
-separated nets and let
\mathscr{D}
be the Christ-David for
E
from Theorem
2.1
. Let
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
and assume without loss of generality that
0\in Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
. By the previous two lemmas, it remains to find
\varepsilon>0
so that

(5.1)

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\text{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)

with constant independent of
n
(and
E
). This is our goal for the remainder of the section. First, we may assume

\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)<\infty.

Second, since
E
is lower content
d
-regular, we have
1\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}),
so we are left to bound the second term. The rough idea for this is as follows. We begin by running a stopping-time argument where we stop when the set
E
fails to be flat at some particular scale. In each of these stopping-time regions
S,
we shall produce a surface
\Sigma^{Q(S)}
by Theorem
3.6
that is Reifenberg flat, and well-approximates
E
inside
S
. By Lemma
2.13
, we push the
\beta
-numbers estimate for
E
onto a
\beta
-number estimate for
\Sigma^{Q(S)}
, which we control by Theorem
1.9
. The error term is controlled by
\text{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

Let us begin. Let
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{D}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon),
i.e. those cubes
Q\in\mathscr{D}
for which
E
is approximately flat at the scale of
Q
. In particular, if for a ball
B
, we define

(5.2)

\displaystyle{b\beta}_{E}^{d}(B)=\inf\{d_{B}(E,P)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\mbox{ is a $d$-plane }\},

then

\mathscr{G}=\{Q\in\mathscr{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{b\beta}_{E}^{d}(AB_{Q})<\varepsilon\}.

Definition 5.3
.

For
Q\in\mathscr{G}
, we define a stopping-time region
S_{Q}
as follows. First, add
Q
to
S_{Q}.
Then, inductively, add cubes
R
to
S_{Q}
if

(1)

R^{(1)}\in S_{Q}
,

(2)

{b\beta}_{E}^{d}(AB_{R^{\prime}})<\varepsilon
for each sibling
R^{\prime}
of
R
.

We start by stating some results from
[
AS18
]
. The following is a combination of
[
AS18
, Lemma 10.1]
and the discussion before.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
A>10^{5}
and
Q\in\mathscr{G}
. For
k\geq 0
, set
s(k)
denote the integer such that

5\rho^{s(k)}\leq r_{k}\leq 5\rho^{s(k)-1},

and

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{k}^{Q}=\{x_{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\in S_{Q}\cap\mathscr{D}_{s(k)}\}.

Let
\{x_{j,k}\}_{j\in J_{k}^{Q}}
be a maximally
r_{k}
-separated net in
\mathscr{C}_{k}^{Q}
. For
j\in J_{k}^{Q}
, let
R_{j}\in S_{Q}\cap\mathscr{D}_{s(k)}
be the cube so that
x_{j,k}=x_{R_{j}},
then let
B_{j,k}=B(x_{R_{j}},r_{k})
and
P_{j,k}=P_{R_{j}}.
Then, the triple
(P_{Q},\{B_{j,k}\},\{P_{j,k}\}),\ k\geq 0,\ j\in J_{k}^{Q},
defines a CCBP.

Remark 5.5
.

For
j\in J_{k}^{Q}
let
R_{j}
be as above. It is not difficult to see that
\rho AB_{j,k}\subseteq AB_{R_{j}},
and so
{b\beta}_{E}^{d}(\rho AB_{j,k})\lesssim\varepsilon.

For
Q\in\mathscr{G},
let
\sigma_{k}^{Q},\Sigma_{k}^{Q},\sigma^{Q}
and
\Sigma^{Q}
be the functions and surfaces obtained by Theorem
3.6
. Additionally, for
x\in H
and
R\in\mathscr{D}
, let

d_{Q}(x)=d_{S_{Q}}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad d_{Q}(R)=d_{S_{Q}}(R).

Lemma 5.6

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 10.2]
)
.

Let
Q\in\mathscr{G}.
If
d_{Q}(x)=0,
then
x\in E\cap\Sigma^{Q}
.

Lemma 5.7

(
[
AS18
, Lemma 10.3]
)
.

Suppose
A\geq C_{1}
. For
x\in\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q}
, let
k=k_{Q}(x)
be the maximal
k
such that
x\in V_{k-1}^{11}.
Then

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,E)\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}\sim\varepsilon d_{Q}(x)

and there is a
C\varepsilon
-Lipschitz graph
\Gamma_{x}^{Q}
over a
d
-plane
P_{x}^{Q}
such that

(5.5)

\displaystyle B(x,r_{k})\cap\Sigma^{Q}=B(x,r_{k})\cap\Sigma^{Q}_{k}=B(x,r_{k})\cap\Gamma_{x}^{Q}.

As in Section
4
, and unlike what is done in
[
AS18
]
, we will need to perform a smoothing procedure to extend each of these stopping-time regions. We choose a constant

0<\tau\leq\tau_{0}=\frac{1}{4(1+\rho^{-1})}

to be fixed small enough. Then, for each
Q\in\mathscr{G},
define

\text{Stop}(Q)=\{R\in\mathscr{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\ \text{is maximal so that there exists $\tilde{R}\in\text{Child}(R^{(1)})$ with }\ \ell(\tilde{R})<\tau d_{Q}(\tilde{R})\}.

We also define

S_{Q}^{\prime}=\{R\subseteq Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\not\subset T\mbox{ for any }T\in\text{Stop}(Q)\}\supseteq S_{Q}.

Because
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q)
is defined in terms of siblings, each
S_{Q}^{\prime}
defines a stopping-time region in the sense of Definition
2.4
. Let us state and prove some facts about
\text{Stop}(Q)
and
S_{Q}^{\prime}.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
Q\in\mathscr{G}
. Let
C_{1}>1
,
\tau_{1}=\min\{\tau_{0},(8+4C_{1})^{-1}\}
and suppose
0<\tau<\tau_{1}.
If
Q,R\in\emph{Stop}(Q)
are such that
C_{1}B_{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{R}\not=\emptyset
then

(5.6)

\displaystyle\ell(Q)\sim\ell(R).

Furthermore, the collection of balls
\{C_{1}B_{R}\}_{R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q)}
has bounded overlap with constant independent of
n
.

Remark 5.9
.

The proof of Lemma
5.8
is essentially the same as the proof of Lemma
4.4
(2). We omit the details.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
Q\in\mathscr{G}
and
R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q).
Then
\ell(R)\sim\tau d_{Q}(R)
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{R}\in S
be the sibling of
R
which satisfies
\ell(\tilde{R})<\tau d_{Q}(\tilde{R})
. Then, by Lemma
2.3
, the fact that
R,R^{\prime}\subseteq B(x_{R^{(1)}},\ell(R)/\rho),
and our assumption on
\tau,
we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle\ell(R)

\displaystyle=\ell(\tilde{R})<\tau d_{Q}(\tilde{R})\leq\tau\left(\ell(\tilde{R})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\tilde{R},R)+\ell(R)+d_{Q}(R)\right)

(5.8)

\displaystyle\leq\tau\left((2+2\rho^{-1})\ell(R)+d_{Q}(R)\right)

(5.9)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\ell(R)+\tau d_{Q}(R).

Rearranging implies
\ell(R)\leq 2\tau d_{Q}(R).
Now for the reverse direction. By maximality, we know
\ell(R^{(1)})\geq\tau d_{Q}(R^{(1)}).
Hence, by Lemma
2.3
and our choice of
\tau,

(5.10)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho}\ell(R)

\displaystyle=\ell(R^{(1)})\geq\tau d_{S}(R^{(1)})\overset{\eqref{Tr}}{\geq}\tau\left(d_{S}(R)-2\ell(R)-2\ell(R^{(1)})\right)

(5.11)

\displaystyle=\tau d_{S}(R)-2(1+\rho^{-1})\tau\ell(R)\geq\tau d_{S}(R)-\frac{1}{\rho}\ell(R).

Rearranging implies
\ell(R)\geq\tfrac{\rho}{2}\tau d_{Q}(R).

∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
Q\in\mathscr{G}.
For each
R\in S_{Q}^{\prime}
there exists
T\in S_{Q}
such that

(5.12)

\displaystyle\tau\ell(T)\lesssim\ell(R)\leq\ell(T)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,T)\lesssim\ell(R).

Proof.

If
R\in S_{Q}
then the lemma is trivial, so suppose
R\in S_{Q}^{\prime}\setminus S_{Q}.
We first claim that we can find
\tilde{R}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q)
so that
\tilde{R}\subseteq R
and
x_{R}\in\tilde{R}
. To see this, let
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
be so that
x_{R}\in R^{\prime}
and
\ell(R^{\prime})<\tfrac{\tau c_{0}}{2}\ell(R),
and let
R^{\prime\prime}
be a sibling of
R^{\prime}
(possibly
R^{\prime}
itself). For any
y\in R^{\prime\prime}
(assuming
\tau
to be small enough), we have

(5.13)

\displaystyle|y-x_{R}|

\displaystyle\leq|y-x_{R^{\prime\prime}}|+|x_{R^{\prime\prime}}-x_{R^{\prime}}|+|x_{R^{\prime}}-x_{R}|\leq\ell(R^{\prime\prime})+\rho^{-1}\ell(R^{\prime})+\ell(R^{\prime})

(5.14)

\displaystyle=(2+\rho^{-1})\ell(R^{\prime})\leq\frac{c_{0}}{2}\tau(2+\rho^{-1})\ell(R)\leq\frac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(R)

and so
R^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\tfrac{c_{0}}{2}B_{R}.
This implies that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R^{\prime\prime},T)\geq\tfrac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(R)
for all
T\in S_{Q}
with
\ell(T)\leq\ell(R).
Thus,

\tau d_{Q}(R^{\prime\prime})\geq\frac{\tau c_{0}}{2}\ell(R)>\ell(R^{\prime\prime}).

Hence, by maximality, we can find
\tilde{R}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q)
so that
R^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{R}\subseteq R
and
x_{R}\in\tilde{R}
as required.

Let us continue with the estimates. Let
\tilde{R}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q)
be the cube from above and let
\tilde{T}\in S_{Q}
be so that
d_{Q}(\tilde{R})\sim\ell(\tilde{T})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\tilde{R},\tilde{T})
. By Lemma
5.10
,

\ell(R)\geq\ell(\tilde{R})\sim\tau d_{Q}(\tilde{R})\sim\tau\left(\ell(\tilde{T})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\tilde{R},\tilde{T})\right)\geq\tau\left(\ell(\tilde{T})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,\tilde{T})\right),

so the first and third inequalities are satisfied by
\tilde{T}.
If
\ell(R)\leq\ell(\tilde{T})
, we set
T=\tilde{T}.
Otherwise, we set
T
to be the ancestor of
\tilde{T}
so that
\ell(T)=\ell(R).

∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
Q\in\mathscr{G}.
If
x\in E
is such that
x
is not contained in any cube
R
from
\emph{Stop}(Q)
, then
d_{Q}(x)=0.

Proof.

Let
R_{j}\in\mathscr{D}
be a sequence of cubes such that
R_{j}\ni x
and
\ell(R_{j})\downarrow 0.
Since
x
is not contained in any cube from
\text{Stop}(Q)
, each
R_{j}
is not contained in any cube from
\text{Stop}(Q).
It follows that
\ell(R_{j})\geq\tau d_{Q}(R_{j})
otherwise
R_{j}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q).
For each
j
, let
x_{j}\in R_{j}
be so that
d_{Q}(x_{j})\leq d_{Q}(R_{j})+j^{-1}.
Then

(5.15)

\displaystyle d_{Q}(x)\leq|x-x_{j}|+d_{Q}(x_{j})\leq|x-x_{j}|+d_{Q}(R_{j})+j^{-1}\leq(2+\tau^{-1})\ell(R_{j})+j^{-1}\rightarrow 0,

and so
d_{Q}(x)=0.

∎

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
Q\in\mathscr{G}
and
R\in S_{Q}^{\prime}.
Then
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{R},\Sigma^{Q})\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau}\ell(R).

Proof.

By Lemma
5.11
, we can find
T\in S_{Q}
such that

\tau\ell(T)\lesssim\ell(R)\leq\ell(T)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\text{dist}(R,T)\lesssim\ell(T).

Recall the definitions of
s(k)
,
\mathscr{C}_{k}^{Q}
,
J_{k}^{Q}
,
B_{j,k}
and
P_{j,k},
from Lemma
5.4
. Let
k
be such that
T\in\mathscr{D}_{s(k)}.
This means

\ell(T)\sim r_{k}.

Since
\{x_{j,k}\}_{j\in J_{k}^{Q}}
is a maximal net in
\mathscr{C}_{k}^{Q}
, there exists
j\in J_{k}^{Q}
such that
x_{T}\in B_{j,k}.
It follows that

(5.16)

\displaystyle|x_{R}-x_{j,k}|

\displaystyle\leq|x_{R}-x_{T}|+|x_{T}-x_{j,k}|\leq\ell(R)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,T)+\ell(T)+|x_{T}-x_{j,k}|

(5.17)

\displaystyle\lesssim(2+C\tau^{-1})\ell(T)+r_{k}\lesssim r_{k},

so taking
A
large enough implies
x_{R}\in\rho AB_{j,k}
. Using this with the fact that
{b\beta}_{E}^{d}(\rho AB_{j,k})\lesssim\varepsilon
(see Remark
5.5
) gives

(5.18)

\displaystyle|x_{R}-\pi_{j,k}(x_{R})|=\text{dist}(x_{R},P_{j,k})\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

By Lemma
3.6
(11), there exists
y\in\Sigma_{k}^{Q}
such that
|\pi_{j,k}(x_{R})-y|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}
. Moreover, this satisfies
\text{dist}(y,\Sigma^{Q})\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}
, by Lemma
3.6
(10). Putting everything together, we have

(5.19)

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x_{R},\Sigma^{Q})\leq|x_{R}-\pi_{j,k}(x_{R})|+|\pi_{j,k}(x_{R})-y|+\text{dist}(y,\Sigma^{Q})\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau}\ell(R).

∎

The following is similar to
[
AS18
, Lemma 10.4]
. In
[
AS18
]
, the sum on the right-hand side of (
5.20
) is taken over the cubes in
\min(S_{Q})
, but their proof contains a mistake. We were not able to prove the estimate stated in
[
AS18
]
, but the following is sufficient. Additionally, we now have constants independent of
n
.

Lemma 5.14
.

For
Q\in\mathscr{G},

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q})\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\emph{Stop}(Q)\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}+\mathscr{H}^{d}(\{x\in\overline{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{Q}(x)=0\}).

Proof.

Let
k_{Q}(x)
be as in the statement of Lemma
5.7
. Let
\delta>0
and set

\displaystyle E_{1}=\{x\in\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{Q}(x)>0\ \text{and}\ \text{dist}(x,Q)<\delta r_{k_{Q}(x)}\}.

Our first goal is to bound
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{1}).
Let
\{x_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a maximal net in
E_{1}
such that

|x_{i}-x_{j}|\geq\max\{r_{k_{Q}(x_{i})},r_{k_{Q}(x_{j})}\}

and let
B_{j}=B(x_{j},2r_{k_{Q}(x_{j})})
for
j\in J.
In this way, the collection of balls
\tfrac{1}{4}B_{j},\ j\in J,
are disjoint and

\displaystyle E_{1}\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{j}.

Let
j\in J,

k_{j}=k_{Q}(x_{j})
, and let
z_{j}
be the point in
Q
closest to
x_{j}.
We claim that there exists
Q_{j}\in\text{Stop}(Q)
so that
Q_{j}\subseteq Q,

z_{j}\in Q_{j}
, and

(5.21)

\displaystyle\ell(Q_{j})\sim r_{k_{j}}.

Let us see why this is true. By definition, we have

(5.22)

\displaystyle|x_{j}-z_{j}|=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{j},Q)<\delta r_{k_{j}}.

Then, by (
5.4
) and the fact that
d_{Q}
is 1-Lipschitz, we have

r_{k_{j}}\sim d_{Q}(x_{j})\leq d_{Q}(z_{j})+|x_{j}-z_{j}|<d_{Q}(z_{j})+\delta r_{k_{j}},

so for
\delta>0
small enough

(5.23)

\displaystyle d_{Q}(z_{j})\gtrsim r_{k_{j}}.

Now, Lemma
5.12
implies the existence of a cube
Q_{j}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q)
so that
z_{j}\in Q_{j}
. Notice that
Q_{j}\subseteq Q
since
z_{j}\in Q\cap Q_{j}
. We are left to show (
5.21
). Let
T_{j}\in S_{Q}
be the cube from Lemma
5.11
for
Q_{j}
. By (
5.23
), and the fact that
z_{j}\in Q_{j},
we have

(5.24)

\displaystyle r_{k_{j}}\lesssim d_{Q}(z_{j})\leq\ell(T_{j})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(z_{j},T_{j})\leq\ell(T_{j})+2\ell(Q_{j})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q_{j},T_{j})\lesssim\ell(Q_{j}).

For the reverse direction, using Lemma
5.10
, (
5.21
), (
5.22
), and the fact that
z_{j}\in Q_{j}
, we get

(5.25)

\displaystyle\ell(Q_{j})\lesssim d_{Q}(Q_{j})\leq d_{Q}(z_{j})\leq|x_{j}-z_{j}|+d_{Q}(x_{j})\lesssim r_{k_{j}}.

Now that we have defined
Q_{j}
, we control how often
Q_{j}=R
for some fixed cube
R
. Let
R\in\text{Stop}(Q)
and let

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{R}=\{j\in J\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q_{j}=R\}.

By (
5.22
) and (
5.21
), for
i,j\in\mathscr{C}_{R}
and
\delta>0
small enough, we have

|x_{j}-x_{i}|\leq|x_{j}-z_{j}|+|z_{j}-z_{i}|+|z_{i}-x_{i}|\leq\delta r_{k_{j}}+2\ell(R)+\delta r_{k_{i}}\leq 3\ell(R).

Pick some
j^{*}\in\mathscr{C}_{R},
and set
p=x_{j^{*}}
. Then
p\in\Sigma^{Q}
and the above inequality implies
x_{j}\in B(p,3\ell(R))
for all
j\in\mathscr{C}_{R}
. Using this with (
5.21
) and the fact that
\Sigma^{Q}
is
C\varepsilon
-Reifenberg flat, there exists a
d
-plane
P
so that

\text{dist}(x_{j},P)\leq 3\varepsilon\ell(R)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}r_{B_{j}/4}.

Hence, we can apply Lemma
2.14
to the disjoint balls
\tfrac{1}{4}B_{j},\ j\in J
, and get

(5.26)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathscr{C}_{R}}r_{k_{j}}^{d}\sim\sum_{j\in\mathscr{C}_{R}}r_{B_{j}/4}^{d}\lesssim\ell(R)^{d}.

Combining this, with the local Lipschitz description of
\Sigma^{Q}
inside
B_{j}
from Lemma
5.7
, we have

(5.27)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in J}\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{1}\cap B_{j})\leq\sum_{j\in J}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{Q}\cap B_{j})\lesssim\sum_{j\in J}r_{k_{j}}^{d}

(5.28)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(Q)\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\sum_{j\in\mathscr{C}_{R}}r_{k_{j}}^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(Q)\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}.

Now, set

\displaystyle E_{2}=\{x\in\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{Q}(x)>0\ \text{and}\ \text{dist}(x,Q)\geq\delta r_{k_{Q}(x)}\}.

We will control
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{2}).
Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a maximal net in
E_{2}
such that

(5.29)

\displaystyle|x_{i}-x_{j}|\geq\max\{r_{k_{Q}(x_{i})},r_{k_{Q}(x_{j})}\}

and define
B_{i}=B(x_{i},r_{k_{Q}(x_{i})}).
Let
Q_{j}\in S_{Q}
be a cube such that

(5.30)

\displaystyle\ell(Q_{j})+\text{dist}(x_{j},Q_{j})<2d_{Q}(x_{j}),

and let
R_{j}\in S_{Q}
be the maximal ancestor of
Q_{j}
for which

\ell(R_{j})<2d_{Q}(x_{j}).

By (
5.30
), we have
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{j},Q_{j})<2d_{Q}(x_{j}),
which, since
Q_{j}\subseteq R_{j},
implies

(5.31)

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x_{j},R_{j})<2d_{Q}(x_{j}).

Additionally,

(5.32)

\displaystyle\ell(R_{j})\sim d_{Q}(x_{j})\sim r_{k_{j}}.

Indeed, the second is true by (
5.4
). For the first equivalence, the forward inequality is clear by definition. If
R_{j}\not=Q
then the backwards inequality follows from maximality, if
R_{j}=Q
then the backwards inequality follows from the fact that
x_{j}\in C_{1}B_{Q}
, since

d_{Q}(x_{j})\leq\ell(Q)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{j},Q)\lesssim\ell(Q).

Now, for each
i\in I
, let

B_{i}^{\prime}=\tfrac{c_{0}}{8}B_{R_{i}}.

We will show that the balls
\{B_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
have bounded overlap in
\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q}.
Indeed, let
x\in\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q}
and define

(5.33)

\displaystyle I(x)=\{i\in I\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in B_{i}^{\prime}\}.

Suppose
i,j\in I(x).
We begin by showing
r_{B_{j}^{\prime}}\sim r_{B_{i}^{\prime}}
. Assume without loss of generality that
\ell(R_{i})\leq\ell(R_{j}).
We are left to show
\ell(R_{j})\lesssim\ell(R_{i}).
Let
x_{i}^{\prime}
be the point in
E
closest to
x_{i}.
Recalling that
x_{i}\in Q\subseteq\Sigma_{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q}
, we have

(5.34)

\displaystyle|x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime}|=\text{dist}(x_{i},E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{SigmaQ}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon r_{k_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:maxan}}}{{\sim}}\varepsilon\ell(R_{i}).

So, for
\varepsilon>0
small enough,

(5.35)

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x_{i}^{\prime},Q)\geq\text{dist}(x_{i},Q)-|x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime}|\geq\delta r_{k_{i}}-C\varepsilon r_{k_{i}}\gtrsim\delta r_{k_{i}}>0.

Let
x^{\prime}
be the point in
E
closest to
x
. Since
x\in\Sigma^{Q}\cap B_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q},

(5.36)

\displaystyle|x^{\prime}-x|=\text{dist}(x,E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{SigmaQ}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon d_{Q}(x)\leq\varepsilon(|x-x_{i}|+d_{Q}(x_{i}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:maxan}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon\ell(R_{i}).

The above estimate also holds true for the index
j
. Taking
\varepsilon
small enough, and using (
5.36
) for
j,
we have

|x^{\prime}-x_{R_{j}}|\leq|x^{\prime}-x|+|x-x_{R_{j}}|\leq C\varepsilon\ell(R_{j})+\frac{c_{0}}{8}\ell(R_{j})\leq\frac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(R_{j})

which implies

x^{\prime}\in E\cap\frac{c_{0}}{2}B_{R_{j}}\subseteq Q.

Using this, the fact that
x_{i}^{\prime}\in E\setminus Q
(by (
5.35
)), (
5.34
) and (
5.36
), we get

(5.37)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(R_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\text{dist}(x_{i}^{\prime},E\cap\tfrac{c_{0}}{2}B_{R_{j}})\leq|x_{i}^{\prime}-x^{\prime}|\leq|x_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i}|+|x_{i}-x|+|x-x^{\prime}|

(5.38)

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(R_{i}).

This proves
r_{B_{i}^{\prime}}\sim r_{B_{j}^{\prime}}.

We will now apply Lemma
2.14
. Let
R=\sup_{i\in I(x)}r_{k_{i}}.
Since the balls
\tfrac{1}{4}B_{i}^{\prime}
,
i\in I(x),
have comparable radii, we can find
i^{*}\in I(x)
and a constant
C>0
such that
\tfrac{1}{4}B_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq B(x_{i^{*}},CR)
for all
i\in I(x)
. Furthermore, since
\Sigma^{Q}
is Reifenberg flat, there exists a plane
P
through
x_{i^{*}}
such that
\text{dist}(x_{B_{i}^{\prime}},P)\lesssim r_{B_{i}^{\prime}}/2
for all
i\in I(x).
Finally, since the balls
\tfrac{1}{4}B_{i}^{\prime}
are disjoint, we can apply Lemma
2.14
to get

(5.39)

\displaystyle I(x)\lesssim_{d}1

as required.

Combining (
5.32
) with (
5.39
), and the fact that
\Sigma^{Q}
is lower regular (since it is Reifenberg flat, see for example the proof of Lemma
A.19
), we obtain

(5.40)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}r_{k_{i}}^{d}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I}\ell(R_{i})^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{Q}\cap B_{i}^{\prime})\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}\left(\Sigma^{Q}\cap\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{i}^{\prime}\right).

Suppose
x\in\Sigma^{Q}\cap B_{i}^{\prime}
for some
i\in I.
Recall by (
5.36
) that

(5.41)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,Q)=\text{dist}(x,E)\lesssim\varepsilon d_{Q}(x)\sim\varepsilon r_{k_{Q}(x)}.

This and the fact that
E\cap B_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq R_{i}\subseteq Q
imply, for
\varepsilon
small enough, that
x
belongs to either
E_{1}
or
E_{0}
, where

E_{0}=\{x\in E\cap C_{1}B_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{Q}(x)=0\}\subseteq\{x\in\overline{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{Q}(x)=0\}.

We conclude the proof by noticing

(5.42)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in I}\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B_{i})\leq\sum_{i\in I}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{Q}\cap B_{i})\lesssim\sum_{i\in I}r_{k_{i}}^{d}

(5.43)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}\left(\Sigma^{Q}\cap\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{i}^{\prime}\right)\leq\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{0}\cup E_{1})

(5.44)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\text{Stop}(Q)\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}+\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{0}).

∎

Let
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{0}
be the maximal collection of cubes from
\mathscr{G}
contained in
Q_{0}.
Then, supposing
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k}
has been defined for some
k\geq 0,
define
{\min}_{k}
to be the cubes
R
so that
R\in\min(S_{Q})
for some
Q\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k}.
We let
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}_{k}
be the cubes
R
so that
R\subseteq Q
and
R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q)
for some
Q\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k}
, and let
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k+1}
be the maximal collection of cubes from
\mathscr{G}
contained in those cubes from
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}_{k}.
Finally, we define

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k},\quad\min=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}{\min}_{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}_{k}.

Lemma 5.15
.

We have

\sum_{Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)

and

\sum_{Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{Q}\cap C_{1}B_{Q})\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

Before proving Lemma
5.15
, we need some preliminary results.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
K
be the set of points in
Q_{0}
which are contained in infinitely many cubes
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
so that
Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon).
Then
\mathscr{H}^{d}(K)=0.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{C}_{1}
be the maximal collection of cubes from
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
contained in
Q_{0}.
Then, assuming
\mathscr{C}_{k}
has been defined for some
k\geq 1,
let
\mathscr{C}_{k+1}
be the maximal collection of cubes in
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
which are strictly contained in the cubes from
\mathscr{C}_{k}.
By maximality each
\mathscr{C}_{k}
forms a cover for
K
. By construction, any
Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
is contained in
\mathscr{C}_{k}
for at most one
k
, and if
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{k}
, then
\ell(Q)\leq 5\rho^{k}.
Thus, since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)<\infty
(recall this from the beginning of the section), it follows that

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{k}}\ell(Q)^{d}=0.

Hence,

\mathscr{H}^{d}(K)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathscr{H}^{d}_{5\rho^{k}}(K)\leq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{k}}\ell(Q)^{d}=0.

∎

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
k\geq 0.
Then

\sum_{Q\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}_{k}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in{\min}_{k+1}}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{Q\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k+1}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{Q}(x)=0\}).

Proof.

Let
Q\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}_{k}.
By Lemma
5.16
, the cubes
\{R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}_{k+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\subseteq Q\}
cover
Q
up to a set of
\mathscr{H}^{d}
-measure zero. Using this with the fact that
E
is lower content
d
-regular, we have

\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(Q)\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k+1}\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}.

Let
R\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k+1}
be so that
R\subseteq Q
and let
x\in E\cap c_{0}B_{R}.
One of two things can happen, either
x\in T
for some
T\in\min(S_{R})
(which by definition means that
T\in\min_{k+1}
), or
d_{R}(x)=0.
Again, since
E
is lower content
d
-regular, this implies

(5.45)

\displaystyle\ell(R)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E\cap c_{0}B_{R})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in{\min}_{k+1}\\
T\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\ell(T)^{d}+\mathscr{H}_{\infty}^{d}(\{x\in R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{R}(x)=0\})

(5.46)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in{\min}_{k+1}\\
T\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\ell(T)^{d}+\mathscr{H}^{d}(\{x\in R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{R}(x)=0\}).

Using the above and taking the sum over all
R\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k+1}
so that
R\subseteq Q
, we have

\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\min}_{k+1}\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k+1}\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\{x\in R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{R}(x)=0\}).

Since the cubes in
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}_{k}
are disjoint, taking the sum over
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}_{k}
finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

For
Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}
define

d(Q)=\{x\in\overline{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}d_{Q}(x)=0\}.

If
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}
are such that
Q\not=Q^{\prime},
then
\mathscr{H}^{d}(d(Q)\cap d(Q^{\prime}))=0.

Proof.

If
Q,Q^{\prime}\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k}
for some
k
then by construction
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\emptyset
and so
d(Q)\cap d(Q^{\prime})\subseteq\partial Q,
where
\partial Q=\{x\in\overline{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B(x,r)\cap E\setminus Q\not=\emptyset\ \text{for all}\ r>0\}
. By
[
AS18
, Lemma 10.5]
we have
\mathscr{H}^{d}(\partial Q)=0
which finishes the proof in this case. Suppose then that
Q\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{k}
and
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}_{m}
, and assume without loss of generality that
k>m.
It follows that
Q\subseteq R
for some
R\in\min(S_{Q^{\prime}}).
Hence,

d(Q)\cap Q^{o}\subseteq R^{o}\subseteq Q^{\prime}\setminus d(Q^{\prime}).

This implies
d(Q)\cap d(Q^{\prime})\subseteq\partial Q
and the lemma follows again by
[
AS18
, Lemma 10.5]
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.15
.

The second inequality is an easy consequence of the first inequality and Lemma
5.14
. Let us prove the first inequality. Since each
Q\in\min
is the minimal cube of some stopping-time region, it has a child, which we denote by
Q^{\prime}
, so that
Q^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon).
Using Lemma
5.17
, Lemma
5.18
, and taking the sum over all
k\geq 0,
we get

(5.47)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\min}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(d(Q))\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\min}\ell(Q^{\prime})^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(d(Q))

(5.48)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)+\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}),

as required.
∎

Let

\mathscr{F}=\{S_{Q}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Top}\}.

For
S\in\mathscr{F},
set

\Sigma^{S}=\Sigma^{Q(S)},\ \mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(Q(S))\ \text{and}\ d_{S}=d_{Q(S)}.

The following finishes the proof of (
5.1
).

Proposition 5.19
.

We have

\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)

Proof.

By construction, for each
Q\in\mathscr{G},
there exists
S\in\mathscr{F}
so that
Q\in S.
Hence

\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})\ell(Q)^{d}+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

It remains to bound the first term. First, by Lemma
2.13
, we have

(5.49)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{\Sigma^{S}}^{d,p}(2C_{0}B_{Q})\ell(Q)^{d}

(5.50)

\displaystyle\hskip-50.00008pt+\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{Q\in S}\left(\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d}}\int_{E\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}}\left(\frac{\text{dist}(x,\Sigma^{S})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(x)\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}

(5.51)

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}.

The proof now split into two claims.

Claim 1:

(5.52)

\displaystyle I_{2}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

Proof of Claim 1.

Let
S\in\mathscr{F}.
By Lemma
2.7
, we may assume
p>2
(as in the proof of Theorem
1.9
), and we take
\tau
small enough so that Lemma
5.13
and Lemma
5.8
hold. By Lemma
5.12
, if
x\in E\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}
is not contained in any cubes from
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)
then
d_{S}(x)=0.
This implies
\text{dist}(x,\Sigma^{S})=0,
by Lemma
5.6
. Suppose instead that
x\in R
for some
R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S).
Then, by Lemma
5.13
,

(5.53)

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x,\Sigma^{S})\leq|x-x_{R}|+\text{dist}(x_{R},\Sigma^{S})\lesssim 2\ell(R).

Using Lemma
2.6
and using our assumption that
p\geq 2
we may write

(5.54)

\displaystyle I_{2}\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{Q\in S}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d+p}}{\ell(Q)^{d+p}}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{Q\in S}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d\frac{2}{p}+2}}{\ell(Q)^{d(\frac{2}{p}-1)+2}}.

For fixed
R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)
, we will show

(5.55)

\displaystyle\{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\cap S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\}\lesssim 1.

First, by Lemma
5.13
(2), if we choose
\varepsilon
small enough there exists a point
x_{R}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{S}
such that

|x_{R}-x_{R}^{\prime}|\leq\ell(R).

Suppose
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\cap S
is such that
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset.
Since

\ell(R)<\tau d_{S}(R)\leq\tau(\ell(Q)+\text{dist}(Q,R))\lesssim\tau\ell(Q),

we get

|x_{Q}-x_{R}^{\prime}|\leq|x_{Q}-x_{R}|+|x_{R}-x_{R}^{\prime}|\leq\ell(R)+2C_{0}\ell(Q)+\ell(R)\leq C\ell(Q).

In particular, this implies that
x_{Q}\in B(x_{R}^{\prime},C\ell(Q)).
Since
\Sigma_{S}
is
(\varepsilon,d)
-Reifenberg flat, we can find a plane
P
through
x_{R}^{\prime}
such that

d_{x_{R}^{\prime},C\ell(Q)}(P,\Sigma_{S})\lesssim\varepsilon.

Let
x_{Q}^{\prime}
be the point in
\Sigma_{S}
which is closest to
x_{Q}
. Then

\displaystyle\text{dist}(x_{Q},P)\lesssim|x_{Q}-x_{Q}^{\prime}|+\text{dist}(x_{Q}^{\prime},P)\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q).

So, for
\varepsilon
small enough, we have
\text{dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq c_{0}\ell(Q)/2.
Then, (
5.55
) follows from Lemma
2.14
by the standard argument.

We return to estimating
I_{2}.
By assumption
p\leq 2d/(d-2),
which is equivalent to
d(2/p-1)+2>0.
Combining this, with the fact that (
5.55
), we can swap the order of integration and sum over a geometric series to get

(5.56)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in S\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d\frac{2}{p}+2}}{\ell(Q)^{d(\frac{2}{p}-1)+2}}\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}

(5.57)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{Stop}(S)\\
R\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q(S)}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{S}\cap c_{0}B_{R})\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{S}\cap C_{1}B_{Q(S)})

(5.58)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

This completes the proof of Claim 1.
∎

Claim 2:

I_{1}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

Proof of Claim 2.

Fix
S\in\mathscr{F}
and let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
Q(S)\in\mathscr{D}_{k}.
Recall, this means that

\ell(Q(S))=5\rho^{k}.

For
n\geq k
let
\mathscr{X}_{n}
be a maximal
\rho^{n}
-separated net for
\Sigma^{S}
, and let
\mathscr{D}^{S}
be the Christ-David cubes from Theorem
2.1
for
\Sigma^{S}
with respect to the nets
\mathscr{X}_{n}.
For
Q\in S
, let
x_{Q}^{\prime}
be the point in
\Sigma^{S}
closest to
x_{Q}
and let
\tilde{Q}\in\mathscr{D}^{S}
be the cube so that
x_{Q}^{\prime}\in\tilde{Q}
and
\ell(Q)=\ell(\tilde{Q}).
By Lemma
5.13
, for any
y\in 2C_{0}B_{Q},
we have

(5.59)

\displaystyle|y-x_{\tilde{Q}}|

\displaystyle\leq|y-x_{Q}|+|x_{Q}-x_{Q}^{\prime}|+|x_{Q}^{\prime}-x_{\tilde{Q}}|

(5.60)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{0}\ell(Q)+C\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau}\ell(Q)+\ell(\tilde{Q})

(5.61)

\displaystyle\leq 3C_{0}\ell(\tilde{Q})

for
\varepsilon
small enough with respect to
\tau.
Hence,
2C_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq 3C_{0}B_{\tilde{Q}}.
Since points in
\{x_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in S\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}\}
are maximally separated and lie close to
\Sigma_{S}
(which is Reifenberg flat), Lemma
2.14
implies the map
Q\rightarrow\tilde{Q}
is at most
C
-to-
1
. Let
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the cubes
R\in\mathscr{D}^{S}
which contains some
\tilde{Q}
obtained above and so that
\ell(Q)=\ell(Q(S))
. The centres of these cubes are all maximally separated and contained in the ball
C_{1}B_{Q(S)}
, so
\#I\lesssim 1
by Lemma
2.14
. Using this with Lemma
2.11
and Theorem
1.9
gives

(5.62)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in S}\beta^{d,p}_{\Sigma^{S}}(2C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)\lesssim\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}^{S}\\
Q\subseteq R_{i}\end{subarray}}\beta_{\Sigma_{S}}^{d,p}(3C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I}\mathscr{H}^{d}(R_{i})\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{S}\cap C_{1}B_{Q(S)}).

Finally, we can apply Lemma
5.15
to get

(5.63)

\displaystyle I_{1}\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{F}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma^{S}\cap C_{1}B_{Q(S)})\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

This completes the proof of Claim 2.
∎

Now, Claim 1 and Claim 2 finish the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.
Estimating measure

In this section we prove Theorem
1.7
. Let
1\leq d<\infty,\ 1\leq p<\infty
,
\ C_{0}>2\rho^{-1}
,
A>1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
E\subseteq H
be lower content
d
-regular, with regularity constant
c
. Let
X_{k}
be a sequence of maximally
\rho^{k}
-separated nets and let
\mathscr{D}
be the Christ-David cubes for
E
from Theorem
2.1
. Let
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
and assume without loss of generality that
0\in Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
. Since we cannot assume
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E)
to be finite, we do not know that a maximally separated net in
E
has a finite number of points. Thus, we cannot reduce to the Euclidean case, as we did in the previous sections. Although, now that we have Theorem
3.6
for subsets of Hilbert space, the proof of (
1.8
) is almost identical to that written in
[
AS18
, Sections 11-12]
. We note that we must give a direct proof of Lemma
6.8
, which for Euclidean space is a corollary of a result of David and Semmes. This requires some work. Since the rest of the construction will be useful for the proof of Theorem
1.4
, we fill in some details.

Recall that we are trying to show

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

By the following (which is proven in the appendix), it suffices to prove the same inequality holds with
C_{0}
replaced by some larger constant
M
and setting
p=1.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
A\geq 1
and
M\geq C_{0}
, and suppose

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Then, (
6.1
) holds with constant
\varepsilon,A
and
C_{0}
.

Remark 6.2
.

From here well shall set
M=\max\{50A,C_{0}\}.

Our goal for the rest of the section is to prove (
6.2
) for
\varepsilon,A
and
M
. From here onwards, when we write
\mathscr{D}
we mean those cubes
Q\in\mathscr{D}
so that
Q\subseteq Q_{0}.
Our first task is to split
\mathscr{D}
into stopping-time regions. For each
Q\in\mathscr{D}
such that
E\cap C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset
, we define a stopping-time region
S_{Q}
as follows.

Let
\lambda>0.
Begin by adding
Q
to
S_{Q}
and inductively on scales, add cubes
R
to
S_{Q}
if each of the following holds,

(1)

R^{(1)}\in S_{Q}
.

(2)

Every sibling
R^{\prime}
of
R
satisfies

\sum_{R^{\prime}\subseteq T\subseteq Q}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{T})^{2}<\lambda^{2}.

Remark 6.3
.

If
\beta_{F}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})\geq\lambda
then
S_{Q}=\{Q\}.

We partition
\{Q\in\mathscr{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\cap MB_{Q}\not=\emptyset\}
into a collection of stopping-time regions as follows. First, add
S_{Q_{0}}
to
\mathscr{S}.
Then, if
S
has been added to
\mathscr{S},
and if
Q\in\text{Child}(R)
for some
R\in\min(S)
such that
E\cap MB_{Q}\not=\emptyset,
add
S_{Q}
to
\mathscr{S}.
Let
\mathscr{S}
be the collection of stopping-time regions obtained by repeating this process indefinitely. Note that

\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}=\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\sum_{Q\in S}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

For each
S\in\mathscr{S}
which is not a singleton (i.e.
S\not=\{Q\}
) we construct a bi-Lipschitz surface which well approximates
E
inside
S
. The following is essentially
[
AS18
, Lemma 11.3]
, however, the precise statement below is
[
Hyd20
, Lemma 3.7]
.

Lemma 6.4
.

There exists
\lambda>0
small enough so that for each
S\in\mathscr{S}
which is not a singleton, there is a surface
\Sigma_{S}
satisfying the follow. First,
\Sigma_{S}=f_{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap MB_{Q(S)}
, where
f_{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow H
is
(1+C\lambda)
-bi-Lipschitz map onto its image. Second, for each
R\in S
and
y\in F\cap(M^{\frac{1}{3}}/4)B_{R}
,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\emph{dist}(y,\Sigma_{S})\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(R).

Finally, for each ball
B
centred on
\Sigma_{S}
and contained in
MB_{Q(S)}
, we have

(6.4)

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{d}}{2}r_{B}^{d}\leq\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma_{S}\cap B)\lesssim r_{B}^{d}.

The following lemma is
[
AS18
, Lemma 11.4]
. The same proof works now that we have Theorem
3.6
for subsets of
H.

Lemma 6.5
.

We have

\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Let

G=\left\{x\in Q_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(MB_{Q})\mathds{1}_{Q}(x)<\infty\right\}.

This is the collection of points in
Q_{0}
which are only contained in a finite number of stopping-time regions. Thus, for each
x\in G
there exists a minimal stopping-time region
S
so that
x\in Q(S)
. This implies
x
is contained in arbitrarily small cubes from
S
, hence
x\in\Sigma_{S}
by (
6.3
). In particular, one has

G\subseteq\bigcup_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\Sigma_{S}.

Let
E_{0}=Q_{0}\setminus G
(the collection of points in
Q_{0}
which are contained in an infinite number of stopping-time regions). The following gives the required bound for
\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})
in (
6.2
).

Lemma 6.6
.

We have
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{0})=0
and

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E_{0})=0
follows from
[
AS18
, Corollary 11.7]
. Since
\Sigma_{S}
is contained in the ball
MB_{Q(S)}
, (
6.4
) and Lemma
6.5
imply

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}\left(\bigcup_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\Sigma_{S}\right)\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

∎

To finish the proof of (
6.2
), we are left to show

(6.7)

\displaystyle\text{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Let us introduce some notation. Recall the definition of
\beta_{E,\infty}^{d}
. Now, for a ball
B
centred on
E
and a
d
-plane
L
, define the similar quantity

\eta_{E,\infty}^{d}(B,L)=\frac{1}{r_{B}}\sup_{x\in L\cap B}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,E)

and

\eta_{E,\infty}^{d}(B)=\inf\{\eta_{E,\infty}^{d}(B,L)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\mbox{ is a $d$-plane }\}.

Recall that if
Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
then
{b\beta}_{E}^{d}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon
, with
{b\beta}_{E}^{d}
as in (
5.2
). For such a
Q
,
[
AS18
, Lemma 12.6]
implies there exists a constant
C
(independent of
Q
) so that either
\beta^{d}_{E,\infty}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C
or
\eta_{E,\infty}^{d}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C.
Hence,

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathscr{D},\ \beta^{d}_{E,\infty}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C\}

(6.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathscr{D},\ \eta^{d}_{E,\infty}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C\}

By Lemma
2.9
, we have the following for the first sum;

(6.10)

\displaystyle\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathscr{D},\ \beta^{d}_{E,\infty}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathscr{D},\ \beta^{d,1}_{E}(2AB_{Q})\geq c\varepsilon^{d+1}\}

(6.11)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(c\varepsilon^{d+1})^{2}}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(2AB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

(6.12)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d},

where the final inequality follows from Lemma
2.11
and Remark
6.2
, since
2AB_{Q}\subseteq MB_{Q}.
The second sum is dealt with below.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
S\in\mathscr{S}.
Then

\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathscr{D},\ \eta^{d}_{E,\infty}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C\}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

To prove Lemma
6.7
, will need some preliminary lemmas. In
[
AS18
]
, the following is stated as a corollary of a more general result for UR sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This more general result is exactly Theorem
1.9
(for subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) and so we need a direct proof.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\delta>0
and
S\in\mathscr{S}
. Then

\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in S,\ {b\beta}^{d}_{\Sigma_{S}}(AB_{Q})\geq\delta\}\lesssim_{\delta}\ell(Q(S))^{d}.

The proof of Lemma
6.8
will be split into several lemmas. Fix
S\in\mathscr{S}.
Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{S}
and let
f=f_{S}
be the
(1+C\lambda)
-bi-Lipschitz from Lemma
6.4
so that
\Sigma=f(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap MB_{Q(S)}.
Let
\mathscr{I}
be the usual dyadic grid for
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
For
I\in\mathscr{I},
let
x_{I}
be its centre, and define
B_{I}=B(x_{I},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)).
Now, for each
Q\in S
, let
x_{Q}^{\prime}
be the point in
\Sigma
which is closest to
x_{Q}
and let
B_{Q}^{\prime}=B(x_{Q}^{\prime},\ell(Q)).
By (
6.3
), for
\lambda
small enough, we have

4AB_{Q}\subseteq 5AB_{Q}^{\prime}.

Finally, define
I_{Q}
to be the cube in
\mathscr{I}
so that
f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime})\in I_{Q}
and
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q})<6A\ell(Q)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q}).

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
Q\in S.
Then

f^{-1}(\Sigma\cap 4AB_{Q})\subseteq f^{-1}(\Sigma\cap 5AB_{Q}^{\prime})\subseteq 4B_{I_{Q}}.

Proof.

The first inclusion follows from the fact that
4AB_{Q}\subseteq 5AB_{Q}^{\prime}.
Now for the second inclusion. Take
z\in f^{-1}(\Sigma\cap 5AB_{Q}^{\prime})
and let
y\in\Sigma\cap 5AB_{Q}^{\prime}
so that
z=f^{-1}(y).
By the definition of
I_{Q}
, and the fact that
f
is
(1+C\lambda)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have

(6.13)

\displaystyle|z-x_{I}|

\displaystyle\leq|z-f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime})|+|f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime})-x_{I}|\leq(1+C\varepsilon)|y-x_{Q}^{\prime}|+|f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime})-x_{I}|

(6.14)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\lambda)5A\ell(Q)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q})\leq 2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q})\leq 4\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q}).

∎

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
I\in\mathscr{I}.
Then

(6.15)

\displaystyle S_{I}=\{Q\in S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{Q}=I\}\lesssim_{d}1.

Proof.

First, since
\ell(I)\sim\ell(Q)
for each
Q\in S_{I},
there are a bounded number of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
for which
S_{I}\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}\not=\emptyset.
Fix
k
for which this intersection is non-empty. For any
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in S_{I}\cap\mathscr{D}_{k},
since
c_{0}B_{Q_{1}}\cap c_{0}B_{Q_{2}}=\emptyset,
we know by (
6.3
) that
|x_{Q_{1}}^{\prime}-x_{Q_{2}}^{\prime}|\geq c_{0}\ell(Q_{1})/2\sim\ell(I).
Then, since
f
is
(1+C\lambda)
-bi-Lipschitz, this implies the preimages
f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime}),\ Q\in S_{I}\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}
, are a collection of approximately
\ell(I)
separated points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which are contained in a ball of radius approximately
\ell(I)
(by definition of
I
). A standard volume argument in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
implies
\#(S_{I}\cap\mathscr{D}_{k})\lesssim_{d}1,
which finishes the proof.

∎

Let
0<\alpha\ll\delta.
Let
S_{\geq}
be those cubes
Q\in S
so that
\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I_{Q}})\geq\alpha
and let
S_{\leq}=S\setminus S_{\geq}.
The definition of
\Omega_{f,2}
can be found at the beginning of Section
4.4
. Then, for each
Q\in S_{\leq},
choose an affine map
\mathcal{A}_{Q}
so that
\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I_{Q}},\mathcal{A}_{Q})\leq 2\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I_{Q}}).

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
Q\in S_{\leq}.
For
y\in 4B_{I_{Q}}
we have

(6.16)

\displaystyle|f(y)-\mathcal{A}_{Q}(y)|\lesssim\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I).

Additionally, for
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

(6.17)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{A}_{Q}(y)-\mathcal{A}_{Q}(z)|\geq(1+C\lambda)^{-1}|y-z|-\frac{C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}}{\gamma}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I),

where
\gamma=\gamma_{y,z}=\min\{1,4\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)/|y-z|\}
.

Proof.

For brevity, let us write
I=I_{Q}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{Q}.
The first statement is a consequence of Lemma
4.12
, since for any
y\in 4B_{I}
we have

(6.18)

\displaystyle|f(y)-\mathcal{A}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\Omega_{f,\infty}(4B_{I},\mathcal{A})\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)\lesssim\Omega_{f,\infty}(4B_{I})\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)

(6.19)

\displaystyle\lesssim\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I})^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)\lesssim\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I).

Let us focus on the second statement. We start with the points
y^{\prime},z^{\prime}\in 4B_{I}.
Notice, for these points, we have

(6.20)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{A}(y^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}(z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\geq|f(y^{\prime})-f(z^{\prime})|-|f(y^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}(y^{\prime})|-|f(z^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}(z^{\prime})|

(6.21)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\lambda)^{-1}|y^{\prime}-z^{\prime}|-C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I).

Now consider
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to be any arbitrary points. Let
\gamma=\gamma_{y,z}=\min\{1,4\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)/|y-z|\}
and let
\mathcal{L}
denote the linear part of
\mathcal{A}
. Then

(6.22)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{A}(y)-\mathcal{A}(z)|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{L}(y-z)|=\frac{1}{\gamma}|\mathcal{L}(\gamma\cdot(y-z)+x_{I})-\mathcal{L}(x_{I}))|=\frac{1}{\gamma}|\mathcal{A}(\gamma\cdot(y-z)+x_{I})-\mathcal{A}(x_{I})|

(6.23)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\gamma}\left((1+C\lambda)^{-1}|\gamma\cdot(y-z)|-C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)\right)

(6.24)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\lambda)^{-1}|y-z|-\frac{C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}}{\gamma}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I),

where the penultimate inequality follows from (
6.20
) since
x_{I},x_{I}+\gamma\cdot(y-z)\in 4B_{I}.

∎

Lemma 6.12
.

There exists
\alpha>0
small enough so that for any
Q\in S_{\leq}
we have

(6.25)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\circ f(4B_{I_{Q}})\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{Q}(3B_{I_{Q}})\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap AB_{Q},

where
\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{Q}}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the
d
-plane
\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Proof.

We start with the second inclusion. Again, let us write
I=I_{Q}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{Q}.
Let
y_{0}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap AB_{Q}
and let
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be such that
y_{0}=\mathcal{A}(z_{0}).
We will be done with this part once we show
z_{0}\in 3B_{I_{Q}}.
By (
6.16
), (
6.17
), and recalling
f
is
(1+C\lambda)
-bi-Lipschitz and
f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime})\in I,
we have

(6.26)

\displaystyle|z_{0}-x_{I}|

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\lambda)\left[|y_{0}-\mathcal{A}(x_{I})|+\frac{C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}}{\gamma}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)\right]

(6.27)

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\leq(1+C\lambda)\left[|y_{0}-x_{Q}^{\prime}|+|f(f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime}))-f(x_{I})|+|f(x_{I})-\mathcal{A}(x_{I})|+\frac{C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}}{\gamma}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)\right]

(6.28)

\displaystyle\leq 2A\ell(Q)+(1+C\lambda)\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)+C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)+\frac{C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}}{\gamma}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)

(6.29)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)+\frac{C\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}}{\gamma}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I).

Suppose, first of all, that
z_{0}\not\in 4B_{I}.
In this case,
\gamma=4\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)/|z_{0}-x_{I}|
, and (
6.26
) implies
|z_{0}-x_{I}|\leq 2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)+\tfrac{1}{3}|z_{0}-x_{I}|
for
\alpha
small enough. Rearranging, we get
|z_{0}-x_{I}|\leq 3\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)
which is a contradiction. It follows that
z_{0}\in 4B_{I}
and
\gamma=1.
Then, for
\alpha
small enough, (
6.26
) implies
|z_{0}-x_{I}|\leq 3\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)
as required for the second inclusion.

We turn our attention to the first inclusion, which requires some topological consideration. Let
y_{1}\in\mathcal{A}(3B_{I})
and let
z_{1}\in 3B_{I}
be so that
y_{1}=\mathcal{A}(z_{1}).
Notice, since
f
is bi-Lipschitz and
\mathcal{A}
well approximates
f,
then
\text{Rank}(\mathcal{A})=d.
In particular,
\mathcal{A}
is invertible. Now, define
g=\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\circ f
and for
z\in\partial=\partial(4B_{I_{Q}})
consider the map

(6.30)

\displaystyle z\mapsto\frac{\mathcal{A}^{-1}(g(z))-\mathcal{A}^{-1}(y_{1})}{||\mathcal{A}^{-1}(g(z))-\mathcal{A}^{-1}(y_{1})||}=\frac{\mathcal{A}^{-1}(g(z))-z_{1}}{||\mathcal{A}^{-1}(g(z))-z_{1}||}.

Let us see why the above map is well-defined. Let
z\in\partial.
By definition,
g(z)
is the point in
\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is closest to
f(z).
Using this, with Lemma
4.12
, (
6.16
), and the fact that
f
is
(1+C\lambda)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have

(6.31)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q})

\displaystyle\leq|z-z_{1}|\leq(1+C\lambda)|f(z)-f(z_{1})|\lesssim|f(z)-g(z)|+|g(z)-y_{1}|+|y_{1}-f(z_{1})|

(6.32)

\displaystyle\leq|f(z)-\mathcal{A}(z)|+|g(z)-y_{1}|+|\mathcal{A}(z_{1})-f(z_{1})|\lesssim|g(z)-y_{1}|+\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q}).

Taking
\alpha
small enough and rearranging implies
|g(z)-y_{1}|\gtrsim\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q})
and so the map is well-defined. Additionally, since

|g(z)-\mathcal{A}(z)|\leq|g(z)-f(z)|+|f(z)-\mathcal{A}(z)|\leq 2|f(z)-\mathcal{A}(z)|\lesssim\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I_{Q}),

we see that the segment
[g(z),\mathcal{A}(z)]
does not contain the point
y_{1}
for
\alpha
small enough. Hence, the map in (
6.30
) is homotopic to the map defined by

(6.33)

\displaystyle z\mapsto\frac{\mathcal{A}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}(z))-z_{1}}{||\mathcal{A}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}(z))-z_{1}||}=\frac{z-z_{1}}{||z-z_{1}||}.

Moving
z_{1}
to
x_{I}
along the segment
[z_{1},x_{I}]
(observing this does not meet
\partial
), this map is homotopic to

z\mapsto\frac{z-x_{I}}{||z-x_{I}||}

which has degree equal to 1. Thus, the map defined in (
6.30
) has degree equal to 1. This implies
y_{1}\in\pi\circ f(4B_{I_{Q}})
since otherwise we could define a homotopy from (
6.30
) to a constant by setting

h(z,t)=\frac{\mathcal{A}^{-1}(g(tz+(1-t)x))-\mathcal{A}^{-1}(y_{1})}{||A^{-1}(g(tz+(1-t)x))-\mathcal{A}^{-1}(y_{1})||}

for
z\in\partial,\ 0\leq t\leq 1,
which is impossible.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

For each
Q\in S,
we have

f(4B_{I_{Q}})\subseteq\Sigma.

Proof.

Let
Q\in S
and
y\in f(4B_{I_{Q}}).
Clearly
f^{-1}(y)\in 4B_{I_{Q}}.
Then, by (
6.3
), and the fact that
f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime})\in I_{Q}\subseteq 4B_{I_{Q}}
and
f
is
(1+C\lambda)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have

(6.34)

\displaystyle|y-x_{Q}|

\displaystyle\leq|y-x_{Q}^{\prime}|+|x_{Q}^{\prime}-x_{Q}|=|f(f^{-1}(y))-f(f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime}))|+|x_{Q}^{\prime}-x_{Q}|

(6.35)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\lambda)|f^{-1}(y)-f^{-1}(x_{Q}^{\prime})|+|x_{Q}^{\prime}-x_{Q}|

(6.36)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\lambda)\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(4I_{Q})+C\lambda^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(Q)

(6.37)

\displaystyle\leq 50A\ell(Q)\leq M\ell(Q).

This implies that

f(4B_{I_{Q}})\subseteq f(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap MB_{Q}\subseteq f(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap MB_{Q(S)}=\Sigma

and completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.8
.

Starting with
S_{\geq},
by (
6.15
) and Corollary
4.11
, we know

(6.38)

\displaystyle\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in S_{\geq},\ {b\beta}_{\Sigma}^{d}(AB_{Q})\geq\delta\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\{\ell(I_{Q})^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in S_{\geq}\}

(6.39)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\sum\{\ell(I)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in\mathscr{I},\ \Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I})\geq\alpha\}

(6.40)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathscr{I}\\
I\subseteq I_{Q(S)}\end{subarray}}\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I})^{2}\ell(I)^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q(S))^{d}.

We are left to bound sum over cubes in
S_{\leq}.
For
Q\in S_{\leq}
, by Lemma
2.9
, Lemma
6.9
, and Lemma
4.13
, we know

\beta_{\Sigma,\infty}^{d}(2AB_{Q},\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\lesssim\beta^{d,2}_{\Sigma}(4AB_{Q},\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\lesssim\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I_{Q}})^{\frac{1}{d+1}}.

We wish to prove a similar bound for
\eta^{d}_{\Sigma_{S},\infty}.
Let
y\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap AB_{Q}
. By Lemma
6.12
and Lemma
6.13
, we can find
z^{\prime}\in 4B_{I_{Q}}
and
z\in f(4B_{I_{Q}})\subseteq\Sigma
so that
z=f(z^{\prime})
and
y=\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(z)
. Since
y
is the point in
\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is closest to
z,
and
z^{\prime}\in 4B_{I_{Q}}
, we have

|y-z|\leq|\mathcal{A}_{Q}(z^{\prime})-f(z^{\prime})|\lesssim\alpha^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(I)

and so
z\in\Sigma\cap 2AB_{Q}
for
\alpha
small enough. We also have

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,\Sigma)\leq|y-z|=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(z,\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

which after taking supremum and using the fact that
z\in\Sigma\cap 2AB_{Q}
, implies

\eta_{\Sigma,\infty}^{d}(AB_{Q},\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\leq\beta_{\Sigma,\infty}^{d}(2AB_{Q},\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

Combining the two, we get

{b\beta}^{d}_{\Sigma}(AB_{Q})\leq{b\beta}_{\Sigma}^{d}(AB_{Q},\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\lesssim\beta^{d}_{\Sigma,\infty}(2AB_{Q},\mathcal{A}_{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\lesssim\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I_{Q}})^{\frac{1}{d+1}},

hence,

(6.41)

\displaystyle\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in S_{\leq},\ {b\beta}_{\Sigma}^{d}(AB_{Q})\geq\delta\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\{\ell(Q)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in S_{\leq},\ \Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I_{Q}})\geq c\delta^{d+1}\}

(6.42)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\sum\{\ell(I)^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in\mathscr{I},\ \Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I_{Q}})\geq c\delta^{d+1}\}

(6.43)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\delta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathscr{I}\\
I\subseteq I_{Q(S)}\end{subarray}}\Omega_{f,2}(8B_{I})^{2}\ell(I)^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q(S))^{d}.

This finishes the proof.

∎

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

For
S\in\mathscr{S}
let
S_{\varepsilon}
be the set of cubes in
S
so that
\eta_{E,\infty}^{d}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C
but
\eta_{\Sigma_{S},\infty}^{d}(AB_{Q})<\varepsilon/3C.
By
[
AS18
, Proposition 12.7]
,

\sum_{Q\in S_{\varepsilon}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q(S))^{d}.

Combining this with Lemma
6.5
and Lemma
6.8
(with
\delta=\varepsilon/3C
), we have

(6.44)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
\eta^{d}_{E,\infty}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/C\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\left[\sum_{Q\in S_{\varepsilon}}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in S\\
\eta_{\Sigma_{S},\infty}^{d}(AB_{Q})\geq\varepsilon/3C\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{d}\right]\lesssim\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}

(6.45)

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

This finishes to proof of Lemma
6.7
and hence the proof of (
6.2
).
∎

7.
Bilateral Weak Geometric Lemma

In this section we prove Theorem
1.4
. We start with the following direction, the proof of which is based on the arguments in
[
DS91
, Section 16]
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose
E\subseteq H
is Ahlfors
d
-regular and satisfies the
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}
(Definition
1.3
), then
E
is
UR
(Definition
1.2
).

The main part of the proof of Proposition
7.1
is to show the following.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0.
Then there exists
L\geq 1
which depends on
\varepsilon
so that the following holds. Let
x\in E
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E).
Then there exists a set
F\subseteq E\cap B(x,r)
so that
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\setminus F)\leq\varepsilon r^{d}
and an
L
-bi-Lipschitz map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Before proving Lemma
7.2
, let us see why it implies Proposition
7.1
.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

Let
A
denote the Ahlfors regularity constant for
E
, set
\varepsilon=(2A)^{-1}
and let
L=L(\varepsilon)
be the constant so that Lemma
7.2
holds. Let
x\in E
,
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)
, and let
F
and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the set and
L
-bi-Lipschitz map, respectively, from Lemma
7.2
for
x
and
r.
Additionally, define the function
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
by setting
h(y)=\tfrac{f(y)-f(x)}{L}.
In this way,
h
is
L^{2}
-bi-Lipschitz and
h(F)\subseteq B_{d}(0,r).
Since
h
is
L^{2}
-bi-Lipschitz, it is invertible with
L^{2}
-bi-Lipschitz inverse
h^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}h(F)\rightarrow H.
By the Kirszbraun extension theorem, we can extend
h^{-1}
to a Lipschitz function from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
H
(whose Lipschitz constant depends only on
L
). Then,

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap h^{-1}(B_{d}(0,r)))

\displaystyle\geq\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap F)

(7.2)

\displaystyle\geq\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r))-\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\setminus F)

(7.3)

\displaystyle\geq(2A)^{-1}r^{d}.

Since
A
and
L
are independent of
x
and
r,
this finishes the proof.
∎

Let us now focus on proving Lemma
7.2
. Let
\varepsilon>0
,
x\in E
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E).
Our first task is to define
F,
which will be chosen by running a certain stopping-time argument, then choosing parts of
E\cap B(x,r)
for which we do not stop too many times, and do not lie too near the boundaries of certain cubes.

Let
C_{0}>1,

\mathscr{D}
be the Christ-David cubes for
E
,
k
be such that
5\rho^{k+1}<r\leq 5\rho^{k},
and
\{Q_{0,i}\}_{i\in I}
be the cubes in
\mathscr{D}_{k}
that intersect
B(x,r).
For each
i\in I,
using the stopping-time construction for the cubes contained in
Q_{0,i}
, outlined at the beginning of Section
6
(with
M=C_{0}
), we find a collection of stopping-time regions
\mathscr{S}_{i}
so that the following holds. First,

(7.4)

\displaystyle\{Q\in\mathscr{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\subseteq Q_{0,i}\}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathscr{S}_{i}}S.

Second, by Lemma
6.5
,

\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}_{i}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0,i})^{d}+\sum_{Q\subseteq Q_{0,i}}\beta^{d,1}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0,i})\lesssim\ell(Q_{0,i})^{d},

where the penultimate inequality follows from Theorem
1.6
and the fact that
E
satisfies the BWGL, and the final inequality follows from Ahlfors regularity. Finally, for each
S\in\mathscr{S}_{i}
which is not a singleton, there is a surface
\Sigma_{S}
satisfying the properties of Lemma
6.4
.

Let
R_{0}
be the union of the cubes
Q_{0,i}
and

\mathscr{S}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\in\mathscr{S}_{i}\}.

Using the same terminology as
[
DS91
]
, we call a cube
Q\subseteq R_{0}
a
transition cube
if it is the top cube, or a minimal cube of some
S\in\mathscr{S}
. Let
T
denote the set of transition cubes. For
Q\in\mathscr{D},
let
N(Q)
denote the number of transition cubes containing
Q
(this is the quantity
\ell(Q)
in
[
DS91
]
). Let
\eta>0
(which will be chosen small momentarily) and define

\sigma(Q)=\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,E\setminus Q)\leq\eta\rho^{k}\}.

Let
N\geq 1
(which will be chosen large) and define
F\subseteq E\cap B(x,r)
by setting

F=E\cap B(x,r)\cap\left(\bigcup_{Q\in T}\sigma(Q)\right)^{c}\cap\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in T\\
N(Q)\geq N\end{subarray}}Q\right)^{c}.

Now that we have defined
F
, let us show that it takes up a large portion of
E\cap B(x,r).
We first need the following.

Lemma 7.3
.

We have

(7.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in T}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim r^{d}.

Proof.

Since
E
is doubling (by virtue of being Ahlfors
d
-regular), there are a bounded number of cubes in
R_{0}
, with constant depending only on
d
and the Ahlfors regularity constant. In other words

\#I\lesssim 1.

Using this with (
7.4
), and the fact that
r\sim\ell(Q_{0,i})
for each
i\in I
, we have

(7.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}_{i}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}\lesssim\sum_{i\in I}\ell(Q_{0,i})^{d}\lesssim r^{d}.

Since
T
is the collection of all top cubes and minimal cubes from the stopping-time regions in
\mathscr{S}
, and every minimal cube has a child which is a top cube, (
7.5
) follows from (
7.6
).
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

For
\eta
small enough and
N
large enough,
\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\setminus F)\leq\varepsilon r^{d}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1
(4) and (
7.5
), for
\eta>0
small enough, we have

\sum_{Q\in T}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\sigma(Q))\leq C\eta^{\frac{1}{C}}\sum_{Q\in T}\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q)\leq C\eta^{\frac{1}{C}}\sum_{Q\in T}\ell(Q)^{d}\leq C\eta^{\frac{1}{C}}r^{d}\leq\varepsilon r^{d}/2.

Similarly, for
L
large enough, if

A=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in T\\
N(Q)\geq N\end{subarray}}Q

then

\mathscr{H}^{d}(A)\leq N^{-1}\sum_{Q\in T}\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q)\leq CN^{-1}\sum_{Q\in T}\ell(Q)^{d}\leq CN^{-1}r^{d}\leq\varepsilon r^{d}/2.

Combing the above estimates, we have

(7.7)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\setminus F)\leq\varepsilon r^{d}.

∎

The next step in the proof of Lemma
7.2
is to construct the bi-Lipschitz map from
F
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which will be done by stitching together the maps associated to each stopping-time region
S\in\mathscr{S}
. The amount of stitching is controlled by the constant
N
. We begin by defining a preliminary map
g
on the set of cubes
Q
contained in
R_{0}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

There exists a map
g
from the set of transition cubes
T
so that for each such
Q
,
g(Q)
is a cube in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(g(Q))=C_{3}^{-N(Q)}\ell(Q)
for some large constant
C_{3}>1
, and for any
Q,Q^{\prime}\in T
satisfying
Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}
then
g(Q)\subseteq\tfrac{1}{2}g(Q^{\prime}).

Proof.

We define
g
inductively, starting with the cubes
Q_{0,i}.
Since
\#I\lesssim 1,
we can find
C>1
large enough so that we can set
\{g(Q_{0,i})\}_{i\in I}
to be a collection of cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with diameters
5\rho^{k}
and mutual distances between
5\rho^{k}
and
5C\rho^{k}
(with
k
as defined after the statement of Proposition
7.1
). Since
N(Q_{i,0})=0
for each
i\in I,
the cubes
g(Q_{i,0})
have the correct diameter.

We now define
g(Q)
for the remaining transition cubes. Suppose
g(Q)
has been defined for some
Q\in T_{m}=T\cap\mathscr{D}_{m}
with
m\geq k.
Assume first of all that
Q
is a minimal cube of a stopping-time region. We will define
g(R)
for each child
R
of
Q
. By definition
N(R)=N(Q)+1
for each child
R
of
Q
. Since
E
is doubling,
Q
has a bounded number of children (with constant depending only on
d
and the Ahlfors regularity constant). Thus, for
C_{3}>1
large enough, we can choose
g(R)
to be a collection of cubes with diameters
C_{3}^{-N(R)}\ell(R)
contained in
\tfrac{1}{2}g(Q)
and so that

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R),g(R^{\prime}))\geq C_{3}^{-N(R)}\ell(R).

Assume now that
Q
is the top cube of a stopping-time region
S\in\mathscr{S}
, which is not a singleton. We want to define
g(R)
for the minimal cubes of
S
. Here, we deviate slightly from that written in
[
DS91
]
. Let
h=h_{S}
be the bi-Lipschitz map from Lemma
6.4
and let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{S}=h(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap MB_{Q}.
Since
h
is bi-Lipschitz with constant close to 1,
h^{-1}(\Sigma\cap B_{Q})
is contained in some ball of radius
2\ell(Q).
Thus, since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(g(Q))=C_{3}^{-N(Q)}\ell(Q),
we can find an affine map
\phi=\phi_{Q}
so that

(7.9)

\displaystyle\phi(h^{-1}(\Sigma\cap B_{Q}))\subseteq\frac{1}{3}g(Q)

and

(7.10)

\displaystyle C_{4}^{-1}C_{3}^{-N(Q)}|p-q|\leq|\phi(p)-\phi(q)|\leq C_{3}^{-N(Q)}|p-q|

for some constant
C_{4}\geq 1
and each
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

For
R\in\min(S)
, let
z_{R}\in\Sigma
be the point closest to
x_{R}
(the centre of
R
). Define
g(R)
to be the cube centred at
\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R}))
of size
C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}\ell(R)=C_{3}^{-N(R)}\ell(R).
Clearly then
g(R)\subseteq\tfrac{1}{2}g(Q).

∎

Continuing with the assumption that
Q
is the top cube of a stopping-time region
S
, which is not a singleton, similarly to (
7.8
), we will need to estimate the relative distance between
g(R),g(R^{\prime})
for
R,R^{\prime}\in\min(S)
. This will facilitate our forthcoming estimates on the function
f
(still to be defined).

Lemma 7.6
.

For
R,R^{\prime}\in\min(S)
there is
C_{3}>1
large enough so that

(7.11)

\displaystyle C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}[\ell(R)+\ell(R^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,R^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R),g(R^{\prime}))

(7.12)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq 3C_{3}^{-N(Q)}[\ell(R)+\ell(R^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,R^{\prime})].

Proof.

Let
R_{1},R_{2}\in\min(S).
Since
x_{R_{i}}\in c_{0}B_{R_{i}}
for each
i=1,2,
and
c_{0}B_{R_{1}}\cap c_{0}B_{R_{2}}=\emptyset
, we can find a constant
C_{5}
so that

(7.13)

\displaystyle C_{5}^{-1}[\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2})]\leq|x_{R_{1}}-x_{R_{2}}|\leq\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2}).

Recalling the definition of
z_{R}
(just below (
7.10
)), by Lemma
6.4
we know
|x_{R_{i}}-z_{R_{i}}|\leq C_{6}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(R_{i})
for some constant
C_{6}
and for each
i=1,2.
Recall also that,
h
is
(1+C_{7}\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz for some constant
C_{7}.
Choosing
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\leq\min\{(2C_{5}C_{6})^{-1},C_{7}^{-1}\},
we have

(7.14)

\displaystyle(2C_{5})^{-1}[\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2})]

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}^{-1}[\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2})]

(7.15)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt-C_{6}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(R_{1})-C_{6}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(R_{2})

(7.16)

\displaystyle\leq|x_{R_{1}}-x_{R_{2}}|-C_{6}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(R_{1})-C_{6}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d+1}}\ell(R_{2})

(7.17)

\displaystyle\leq|z_{R_{1}}-z_{R_{2}}|\leq(1+C_{7}\varepsilon)|h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}})-h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}})|

(7.18)

\displaystyle\leq 2|h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}})-h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}})|.

After rearranging, this gives

(7.19)

\displaystyle|h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}})-h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}})|\geq(4C_{5})^{-1}[\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2})].

Similarly, one can also show

(7.20)

\displaystyle|h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}})-h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}})|\leq 2[\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2})].

Let
y_{R_{1}}\in g(R_{1})
and
y_{R_{2}}\in g(R_{2})
be points so that
|y_{R_{1}}-y_{R_{2}}|=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{1}),g(R_{2})).
Recalling that
g(R_{i})
is centred at
\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{i}}))
and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(g(R_{i}))=C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}\ell(R_{i})
, choosing
C_{3}\geq 8C_{4}C_{5}
, and using (
7.10
) and (
7.19
), we have

(7.21)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{1}),g(R_{2}))

\displaystyle=|y_{R_{1}}-y_{R_{2}}|

(7.22)

\displaystyle\geq|\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}}))-\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}}))|-|y_{R_{1}}-\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}}))|-|y_{R_{2}}-\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}}))|

(7.23)

\displaystyle\geq(4C_{4}C_{5})^{-1}C_{3}^{-N(Q)}[\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2})]

(7.24)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt-C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}\ell(R_{1})-C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}\ell(R_{2})

(7.25)

\displaystyle\geq C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}[\ell(R_{1})+\ell(R_{2})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{1},R_{2})],

The reverse inequality follows similarly;

(7.26)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{1}),g(R_{2}))

\displaystyle=|y_{R_{1}}-y_{R_{2}}|

(7.27)

\displaystyle\leq|\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}}))-\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}}))|+|y_{R_{1}}-\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{1}}))|+|y_{R_{2}}-\phi(h^{-1}(z_{R_{2}}))|

(7.28)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{3}^{-N(Q)}[\ell(R)+\ell(R^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,R^{\prime})]+C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}\ell(R)+C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}\ell(R^{\prime})

(7.29)

\displaystyle\leq 3C_{3}^{-N(Q)}[\ell(R)+\ell(R^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,R^{\prime})].

∎

Now that we have defined
g,
we can use it to define a function
f
. By definition, for each
x\in F
there is a minimal transition cube, which we denote by
Q(x),
so that
x\in Q(x).
It must be that
Q(x)
is top cube of a stopping-time region
S(x)
(which is not a singleton), otherwise
x
would be contained in a smaller transition cube. Furthermore,
x
is contained in arbitrarily small cubes from
S(x)
and so
x\in F\cap\Sigma_{S(x)}
by (
6.3
). Since
x\in\Sigma_{S(x)},

h_{Q(x)}^{-1}(x)
makes sense, and we can define

f(x)=\phi_{Q(x)}(h^{-1}_{Q(x)}(x)).

Notice, by (
7.9
),
f(x)\in g(Q(x)).
The proof of Lemma
7.2
is finished once we show the following.

Lemma 7.7
.

The map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz, where
L
depends on
\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in F
be distinct points. Let
Q_{1}
be the maximal cube contained in
R_{0}
so that
x\in Q_{1}
but
y\not\in Q_{1}.
Assume to begin with that
Q_{1}
is contained in some stopping-time region
S
, but that
Q_{1}
is not the top cube of
S.
By maximality, any cube containing
Q_{1}
contains both
x
and
y
, in particular, this holds true for
Q(S).
Assume further that there are cubes
R_{x},R_{y}\in\min(S)
so that
x\in R_{x}
and
y\in R_{y}.
This implies, first, that

(7.30)

\displaystyle|x-y|\leq\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y}).

Secondly, since
x\not\in\sigma(R_{x})
and
y\not\in\sigma(R_{y})
(by the definition of
F
), we also have

(7.31)

\displaystyle|x-y|\gtrsim\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y}).

The estimates on
f
then follow from the fact that
f(x)\in g(R_{x}),

f(y)\in g(R_{y}),

N(Q)\leq N
and the estimates (
7.11
), (
7.30
), and (
7.31
), since

(7.32)

\displaystyle|x-y|

\displaystyle\leq\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y})\lesssim_{L}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{x}),g(R_{y}))\leq|f(x)-f(y)|

(7.33)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{x}),g(R_{y}))+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(R_{x})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(R_{y})\lesssim_{N}\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y})

(7.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim|x-y|.

If it is the case that
x
is not contained in any cube from
\min(S)
, then
f(x)=\phi_{Q(S)}(h^{-1}_{Q(S)}(x))
. By similar arguments to (
7.11
), it can be shown that

C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}[\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,R_{y})]\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,g(R_{y}))\leq 3C_{3}^{-N(Q)}[\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,R_{y})],

and the estimates follow in the same way as above. Swapping the roles of
x
and
y
, the same is true if
y
is not contained in any cube from
\min(S).
If neither
x
nor
y
are contained in minimal cubes, the estimates follow directly from an argument similar to that which established (
7.11
).

Now we are left with the case that
Q_{1}
is the top of a stopping-time region. Let
Q_{2}
be the parent of
Q_{1}.
It must be that
Q_{2}
is a transition cube, and by maximality,
x,y\in Q_{2}.
Since
x,y\in Q_{2}
,
x\in Q_{1}\setminus\sigma(Q_{1})
and
y\not\in Q_{1}
we have
|x-y|\sim\ell(Q_{1}).

To estimate the distance between
f(x)
and
f(y)
, first note that since
x,y\in Q_{2},
we have
f(x),f(y)\in g(Q_{2})
and so

|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(g(Q_{2}))\lesssim\ell(Q_{2})\sim\ell(Q_{1})\sim|x-y|.

Next, let
Q_{1}^{\prime}
be the sibling of
Q_{1}
so that
y\in Q_{1}^{\prime}.
By (
7.11
) we have

|f(x)-f(y)|\geq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(Q_{1}),g(Q_{1}^{\prime}))\gtrsim\ell(Q_{1})\sim|x-y|

and this finishes the proof of the bi-Lipschitz estimates.

∎

Now for the converse to Proposition
7.1
.

Proposition 7.8
.

Suppose
E
is
UR
, then
E
satisfies the
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}.

We will need the following two lemmas.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\rightarrow H
be an
L
-bi-Lipschitz function. Then there exists constant
m
and
M
(depending only on
d
and
L
), and an extension
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\tilde{H}\coloneqq H\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
of
f
which is
M
-bi-Lipschitz.

Remark 7.10
.

The above lemma is essentially
[
DS91
, Proposition 17.4]
. The proof is identical up to notational changes (swapping
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
H
). Additionally, the constant
M
in
[
DS91
, Proposition 17.4]
is actually independent of
n
.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
E\subseteq H
and suppose there are constants
C
and
\theta>0
so that for each
x\in E
and
r\in(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}E),
there exists an Ahlfors
d
-regular set
\tilde{E}
with regularity constant at most
C
, so that

\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap\tilde{E})\geq\theta r^{d},

and so that
\tilde{E}
satisfies the
(2,2)
-geometric lemma, meaning

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}^{\tilde{E}}\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\beta_{\tilde{E}}^{d,2}(C_{0}B_{R})^{2}\ell(R)^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q)^{d}

for all
C_{0}>1
and
Q\in\mathscr{D}^{\tilde{E}}.
Then
E
also satisfies the
(2,2)
-geometric lemma.

Remark 7.12
.

The above is a consequence of
[
DS93
, Theorem IV.1.3]
, whose proof is found in
[
DS93
, Section IV.1.4]
and remains valid for subsets of
H
. For the definition of the general
(p,q)
-geometric lemma, see
[
DS93
, Definition IV.1.2]
. This is stated in terms of a Carleson measure condition, however, the above formulation is equivalent and is similar to how we formulated the BWGL, see after Definition
1.3
.

Proof of Proposition
7.8
.

Let
A\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
. We need to show
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q,A,\varepsilon)<C(A,\varepsilon)\ell(Q)^{d}
for all
Q\in\mathscr{D}.
Let
C_{0}\geq 2\rho^{-1}.
By Theorem
1.6
, with
p=2,
we know

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q,A,\varepsilon)\lesssim_{A,\varepsilon}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,2}_{E}(C_{0}B_{R})^{2}\ell(R)^{d}

for all
Q\in\mathscr{D}.
Thus, we are done if we can show that

(7.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,2}_{E}(C_{0}B_{R})^{2}\ell(R)^{d}\lesssim\ell(Q)^{d}

for all
Q\in\mathscr{D}.
This will follow from the fact that
E
satisfies the
(2,2)
-geometric lemma, which we verify by Lemma
7.11
. First, by
[
Sch09
, Corollary 1.2]
,
E
has
big pieces of bi-Lipschitz images of

\mathbb{R}^{d},
meaning there are constants
L,\theta>0
such that for all
x\in E
and
r\in(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}E),
there is a compact subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and an
L
-bi-Lipschitz map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\rightarrow H
such that

(7.36)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap f(A))\geq\theta r^{d}.

Let
x\in E
,
r\in(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}E)
, and let
A=A_{x,r}
and
f=f_{x,r}
be as above. By Lemma
7.9
(and identifying
\tilde{H}
with
H
), we can extend
f
to a bi-Lipschitz function
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow H
, and we set
\tilde{E}=g(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
Clearly

\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap\tilde{E})\geq\theta r^{d}.

Additionally,
\tilde{E}
satisfies the
(2,2)
-geometric lemma. To see this, notice that
\tilde{E}
satisfies the BWGL by Lemma
6.8
, and so, by Theorem
1.6
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}^{\tilde{E}}\\
R\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\beta_{\tilde{E}}^{d,2}(C_{0}B_{R})^{2}\ell(R)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q)\lesssim\ell(Q)^{d}

for all
C_{0}>1
and
Q\in\mathscr{D}^{\tilde{E}}.
Equation (
7.35
) and hence the proposition now follows by Lemma
7.11
.
∎

Appendix A
Parameterisation in Hilbert space

In this section, we prove some of the main results in Theorem
3.6
. The main objective is to prove the local Lipschitz description of the surfaces
\Sigma_{k}
– Theorem
3.6
(7). We will prove this and all the results necessary preliminary results, which include Theorem
3.6
(10) and (11). In addition, we will prove Theorem
3.6
(13). The results Theorem
3.6
(2) and (4)-(6) are immediate from the definition in Section
3
. For the remaining points of Theorem
3.6
((1),(3),(8),(9) and (12)), we direct the reader to the proofs contained in
[
DT12
]
, which are self-contained given the results we prove in this section. We shall on comment on this after we are finished in (7).

Preliminaries
