We collect a series of preliminary lemmas, to aid the proof of Theorem
3.6
(7). Included here is the proof of Theorem
3.6
(11).

Lemma A.1
.

Let
P,P^{\prime}
be
d
-planes in
H
,
x\in P
and
B
a ball centred on
x.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and suppose

(A.1)

\displaystyle d_{B}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon.

Let
P^{\prime\prime}
be the plane parallel to
P^{\prime}
such that
x\in P^{\prime\prime}.
Then for any
A>0
we have

(A.2)

\displaystyle d_{AB}(P,P^{\prime\prime})\lesssim\varepsilon

and

(A.3)

\displaystyle d_{AB}(P,P^{\prime})\lesssim\varepsilon(1+1/A).

Proof.

Let
A>0
and
P^{\prime\prime}
be as above. Since
P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime\prime}
are parallel,
x\in P^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon r_{B}
, we have

(A.4)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\leq\varepsilon r_{B}.

So, for each
y\in P^{\prime}\cap AB,
we can find
z\in P^{\prime\prime}\cap AB
such that
|y-z|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{B}.
Similarly, for each
z\in P^{\prime\prime}\cap AB
, we can find
y\in P^{\prime}\cap AB
such that
|y-z|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{B}.
This implies

(A.5)

\displaystyle r_{AB}d_{AB}(P,P^{\prime})\lesssim\varepsilon r_{B}+r_{AB}d_{AB}(P,P^{\prime\prime})

and so (
A.3
) will follow once we show (
A.2
). Furthermore, since
x\in P\cap P^{\prime\prime}
we have

d_{\frac{1}{2}B}(P,P^{\prime\prime})=d_{AB}(P,P^{\prime\prime}),

so it suffices to show

d_{\frac{1}{2}B}(P,P^{\prime\prime})\lesssim\varepsilon.

This will be the goal for the rest of the proof. Let
z\in P^{\prime\prime}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B.
By (
A.4
) there exists
z^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}
such that

(A.6)

\displaystyle|z-z^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon r_{B}.

It follows that
z^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}\cap(\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)B
which by (
A.1
). implies the existence of a point
y^{\prime}\in P\cap B
such that

(A.7)

\displaystyle|z^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon r_{B}.

By (
A.7
), since
z^{\prime}\in(\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)B,
it actually follows that
y^{\prime}\in P\cap(\tfrac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon)B
. Since
B
is centred on
x
and
x\in P
there exists a point
y\in P\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
such that

(A.8)

\displaystyle|y^{\prime}-y|\leq 2\varepsilon r_{B}.

Combing the above, we have

(A.9)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(z,P\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B)\leq|z-y|\leq|z-z^{\prime}|+|z^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|+|y^{\prime}-y|\leq 4\varepsilon r_{B}.

Since
z
was an arbitrary point in
P^{\prime\prime}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
, this gives

\sup_{y\in P^{\prime\prime}\cap\frac{1}{2}B}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,P\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B)\leq 4\varepsilon r_{B}.

Swapping the roles of
P
and
P^{\prime\prime}
we also get

\sup_{y\in P\cap\frac{1}{2}B}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,P^{\prime\prime}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B)\leq 4\varepsilon r_{B}

which finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose
P,P^{\prime}
are two
d
-planes in
H
which contain the origin and satisfy
d_{B(0,1)}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon.
Then for
y\in P^{\prime}
, we have

(A.10)

\displaystyle|\pi^{\perp}_{P}(y)|\lesssim\varepsilon|y|,

where
\pi_{P}^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal projection on
P^{\perp},
the linear space orthogonal to
P

Proof.

Let
B=B(0,1).
Since
0\in P\cap P^{\prime}
and
d_{B}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon,
we have
d_{AB}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon
for all
A>0.
So, taking
A=|y|,
we get

(A.11)

\displaystyle|\pi^{\perp}_{P}(y)|=|y-\pi_{P}(y)|=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,P)\leq|y|d_{B(0,|y|)}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon|y|.

∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
B
be a ball and suppose
P,P^{\prime}
are
d
-planes in
X
. For each
\alpha>0
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that if

d_{B}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon

then

P\cap(1-\alpha)B\subseteq\pi_{P}(P^{\prime}\cap B).

The proof of Lemma
A.3
is similar to the topological argument used to prove Lemma
6.12
, we shall omit the details. The following is Lemma
3.6
(11).

Lemma A.4
.

\emph{(11)}
holds.

Proof.

Let
y\in\Sigma_{k}\cap V_{k}^{8}
. By Lemma
3.4
this mean that

\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)=1.

Then, if
i\in J_{k}
is such that
y\in 10B_{i,k}
, we have

(A.12)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(y)|

\displaystyle=\big|y+\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)[\pi_{j,k}(y)-y]-\pi_{i,k}(y)\big|

(A.13)

\displaystyle=\big|\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)[\pi_{j,k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(y)]\big|\leq\sum_{j\in J_{k}}|\theta_{j,k}(y)||\pi_{j,k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(y)|.

Since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}\theta_{j,k}\subseteq 10B_{j,k}
, if
j\in J_{k}
is such that
\theta_{j,k}(y)\not=0
, then
y\in 10B_{j,k}
. In this case
|x_{j,k}-x_{i,k}|\leq 20r_{k}.
This, along with the definition of
\varepsilon_{k}(y)
, gives

(A.14)

\displaystyle d_{x_{i,k},100r_{k}}(P_{i,k},P_{j,k})\leq\varepsilon_{k}(y)

which implies

(A.15)

\displaystyle|\pi_{j,k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}(y)r_{k}.

As shown in the proof of Lemma
3.4
, the balls
10B_{j,k},\ j\in J_{k}
have bounded overlap, hence

|\sigma_{k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(y)|\leq\sum_{j\in J_{k}}|\theta_{j,k}(y)|C\varepsilon_{k}(y)r_{k}\leq C\varepsilon_{k}(y)r_{k}

as required.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Let
k\geq 0
,
y,z\in\Sigma_{k}\cap V_{k}^{8}
and
i\in J_{k}
so that
y,z\in 10B_{i,k},
then

|(\sigma_{k}(y)-\sigma_{k}(z))-(\pi_{i,k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(z))|\lesssim\varepsilon_{k}(y)|y-z|

Proof.

Using the fact that
y,z\in V_{k}^{8}
and so
\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\theta_{j,k}(y)=1
, with the analogous quantity holding for
z,
we have

(A.16)

\displaystyle|(\sigma_{k}(y)-\sigma_{k}(z))-(\pi_{i,k}(y)-\pi_{i,k}(z))|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in J_{k}}|\theta_{j,k}(y)||\pi_{j,k}(y-z)-\pi_{i,k}(y-z)|

(A.17)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{j\in J_{k}}|\theta_{j,k}(y)-\theta_{j,k}(z)||\pi_{j,k}(z)-\pi_{i,k}(z)|

(A.18)

\displaystyle=S_{1}+S_{2}.

We start by estimating
S_{1}.
By (
A.14
), for each
j\in J_{k}
such that
\theta_{j,k}(y)\not=0,
we have

d_{x_{i,k},100r_{k}}(P_{i,k},P_{j,k})\leq\varepsilon_{k}(y).

Then, Lemma
A.2
implies

(A.19)

\displaystyle|\pi_{j,k}(y-z)-\pi_{i,k}(y-z)|

\displaystyle\leq|\pi_{j,k}(y-z)-\pi_{i,k}(\pi_{j,k}(y-z))|+|\pi_{i,k}(\pi_{j,k}(y-z))-\pi_{i,k}(y-z)|

(A.20)

\displaystyle=|\pi_{i,k}^{\perp}(\pi_{j,k}(y-z))|+|\pi_{i,k}(\pi_{j,k}^{\perp}(y-z))|

(A.21)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{k}(y)|y-z|.

Since the ball
10B_{j,k},\ j\in J_{k},
have bounded overlap, we conclude that
S_{1}\lesssim\varepsilon_{k}(y)|y-z|.
We also see that
S_{2}\lesssim\varepsilon_{k}(y)|y-z|
by the bounded overlap of the balls
10B_{j,k},
(
A.15
), and Lemma
3.3
, since

(A.22)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j,k}(y)-\theta_{j,k}(z)||\pi_{j,k}(z)-\pi_{i,k}(z)|\lesssim r_{k}^{-1}|y-z|\varepsilon_{k}(z)r_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon_{k}(z)|y-z|.

∎

Local Lipschitz description

We can now begin the proof of Theorem
3.6
(7), which follows from the proposition below. This is the analogue of
[
DT12
, Proposition 5.1]
.

Proposition A.6
.

There are constant
\{C_{i}\}_{i=1}^{7}
such that the following holds for all
k\geq 0
and
j\in J_{k}.
First, there is a function
A_{j,k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P_{j,k}\cap 49B_{j,k}\rightarrow P_{j,k}^{\perp}
such that

(A.23)

\displaystyle|A_{j,k}(x_{j,k})|\leq C_{1}\varepsilon r_{k}.,

(A.24)

\displaystyle|DA_{j,k}(z)|\leq C_{2}\varepsilon,\quad\text{for all}\ z\in P_{j,k}\cap 49B_{j,k}

and if
\Gamma_{A_{j,k}}
is the graph of
A_{j,k}
over
P_{j,k}
then

(A.25)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{k}\cap D(x_{j,k},P_{j,k},49r_{k})=\Gamma_{A_{j,k}}\cap D(x_{j,k},P_{j,k},49r_{k}).

Next, there is a function
F_{j,k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P_{j,k}\cap 40B_{j,k}\rightarrow P_{j,k}^{\perp}
such that

(A.26)

\displaystyle|F_{j,k}(x_{j,k})|\leq C_{3}\varepsilon r_{k},

(A.27)

\displaystyle|DF_{j,k}(z)|\leq C_{4}\varepsilon\quad\text{for}\ z\in P_{j,k}\cap 40B_{j,k},

(A.28)

\displaystyle|DF_{j,k}(z)|\leq C_{5}\varepsilon\quad\text{for}\ z\in P_{j,k}\cap 7B_{j,k},

and if
\Gamma_{F_{j,k}}
is the graph of
F_{j,k}
over
P_{j,k}
then

(A.29)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{k+1}\cap D(x_{j,k},P_{j,k},40r_{k})=\Gamma_{F_{j,k}}\cap D(x_{j,k},P_{j,k},40r_{k}).

Remark A.7
.

For a given plane
P
, a ball
B
, and a differentiable function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\rightarrow H
, it is not difficult to show that
|Df(z)|\leq C
for all
z\in P\cap B
if and only if
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq C|x-y|
for all
x,y\in P\cap B.
In the following proof, we will make use of the second characterization.

We prove Proposition
A.6
by induction. Since
\Sigma_{0}=P_{0}
it is clear that (
A.23
)-(
A.25
) hold for
k=0
, which establishes the base case. The induction will be carried out in two stages.

Stage 1

In this section, we show the following.

Lemma A.8
.

If (
A.26
)-(
A.29
) hold for
k
, then (
A.23
)-(
A.25
) hold for
k+1.

Let
j\in J_{k+1}.
Our goal is to define a map
A_{j,k+1}
so that (
A.23
)-(
A.25
) hold. We need some preliminary results. First, by Definition
3.1
(1) there is
i\in J_{k}
such that

(A.30)

\displaystyle x_{j,k+1}\in 2B_{i,k}.

By translating we may assume
x_{i,k}=0,
so that
P_{i,k}
is a linear subspace. To simplify notation we shall write
V=P_{i,k}
and
W=P_{j,k+1}
. By Definition
3.1
(5), we have

(A.31)

\displaystyle d_{0,20r_{k}}(V,W)\leq\varepsilon,

so we know that there exists
p\in W\cap B(0,20r_{k})
satisfying

(A.32)

\displaystyle|p|\leq 20\varepsilon r_{k}.

Let
W^{\prime}
denote the linear subspace parallel to
W
. By Lemma
A.1
we have for any
A>0
,

(A.33)

\displaystyle d_{0,20Ar_{k}}(V,W^{\prime})\lesssim\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad d_{0,20Ar_{k}}(V,W)\lesssim\varepsilon(1+1/A).

Let
\pi
denote the orthogonal projection onto
V
,
\pi_{W}
denote the orthogonal projection on
W
,
\pi_{W}^{\perp}
denote the orthogonal projection on
W^{\perp}
(the linear subspace orthogonal to
W^{\prime}
) and
\pi_{W^{\prime}}
denote the orthogonal projection onto
W^{\prime}
. In this way,

(A.34)

\displaystyle\pi_{W}(x)=\pi_{W}^{\perp}(p)+\pi_{W^{\prime}}(x).

Since
W^{\perp}
and
W^{\prime}
are linear subspaces, and orthogonal projections on linear subspaces are bounded linear operator with norm at most 1, we have

(A.35)

\displaystyle|\pi_{W}(y)|\leq|\pi_{W}^{\perp}(p)|+|\pi_{W^{\prime}}(y)|\leq|p|+|y|\leq 20\varepsilon r_{k}+|y|.

Let
F=F_{i,k}
be the map satisfying (
A.26
)-(
A.29
) for
k
and
i\in J_{k}
and define a map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W\cap 39B_{i,k}\rightarrow W
by setting

f(y)=\pi_{W}(\pi(y)+F(\pi(y))).

See Figure
1
. We have the following.

Figure 1.
Definition of
f
.

Lemma A.9
.

The map
f
is well-defined and has a Lipschitz inverse
f^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W\supseteq U\rightarrow W\cap 39B_{i,k}
such that

(A.36)

\displaystyle\emph{Lip}(f^{-1})+||f^{-1}||\leq 3.

Proof.

We start by considering the action of
\pi
on elements of
W\cap 39B_{i,k}
. Along the way, we show
f
is well-defined. Let
y\in W\cap 39B_{i,k}
. Recall that since
x_{i,k}=0
we have
|y|\leq 39r_{k}.
By (
A.33
) (with
A=|y|/20r_{k}
) we have

(A.37)

\displaystyle|\pi(y)-y|

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,V)\leq|y|d_{B(0,|y|)}(V,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:dVW1}}}{{\leq}}C\varepsilon|y|(1+20r_{k}/|y|)

(A.38)

\displaystyle=C\varepsilon(|y|+20r_{k})\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

Hence, if
\varepsilon
is small enough
\pi(y)\in V\cap 40B_{i,k}=\text{Dom}(F)
for all
y\in W\cap 39B_{i,k}
, and so
f
is well-defined. Next, we establish some bi-Lipschitz estimates for
\pi
. Let
y,z\in W.
Since
\pi
is linear and
y-z\in W^{\prime}
, by (
A.33
) and Lemma
A.2
, we have

(A.39)

\displaystyle|(\pi(y)-\pi(z))-(y-z)|=|\pi(y-z)-(y-z)|\leq C\varepsilon|y-z|.

Now that we have information for
\pi,
we can prove estimates similar to (
A.37
) and (
A.39
) for
f
. Notice, if
y,z\in W\cap 39B_{i,k}
then

(A.40)

\displaystyle|f(y)-y|

\displaystyle=|\pi_{W}(\pi(y)-y+F(\pi(y)))|

(A.41)

\displaystyle\kern-9.26393pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{e:pi_W}}}20\varepsilon r_{k}+|\pi(y)-y+F(\pi(y))|

(A.42)

\displaystyle\leq 20\varepsilon r_{k}+|\pi(y)-y|+|F(\pi(y))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:pi-y}\atop\eqref{e:Fnorm}}}{{\leq}}(C+C_{3})\varepsilon r_{k}

and

(A.43)

\displaystyle|f(y)-y-(f(z)-z)|

\displaystyle=|\pi_{W^{\prime}}(\pi(y)-\pi(z)-(y-z)+F(\pi(y))-F(\pi(z)))|

(A.44)

\displaystyle\leq|\pi(y)-\pi(z)-(y-z)|+|F(\pi(y))-F(\pi(z))|

(A.45)

\displaystyle\kern-7.83685pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{e:pi-yz}\atop\eqref{e:Flip}}}(C+C_{4})\varepsilon|y-z|.

By (
A.43
), choosing
\varepsilon
small enough, we see that
f
is bijection from
W\cap 39B_{i,k}
onto
U=f(W\cap 39B_{i,k})\subseteq W
. Thus, it has an inverse
f^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U\rightarrow W\cap 39B_{i,k}
. It is not difficult to show that (
A.40
) and (
A.43
) imply (
A.36
) for
\varepsilon
small enough.
∎

Now, we can define the function
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U\rightarrow W^{\perp}
by setting

A(y)=\pi^{\perp}_{W}(\pi(f^{-1}(y))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(y))))-\pi_{W}^{\perp}(p).

See Figure
2
.

Figure 2.
Definition of
A
.

By the definition of
f
(at the point
f^{-1}(y))
, we have

y=\pi_{W}(\pi(f^{-1}(y))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(y)))).

Using this, and (
A.34
), for any
y\in U
we have

(A.46)

\displaystyle\begin{split}y+A(y)&=\pi_{W}(\pi(f^{-1}(y))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(y))))+\pi^{\perp}_{W}(\pi(f^{-1}(y))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(y))))-\pi_{W}^{\perp}(p)\\
&=\pi_{W^{\prime}}(\pi(f^{-1}(y))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(y))))+\pi^{\perp}_{W}(\pi(f^{-1}(y))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(y))))\\
&=\pi(f^{-1}(y))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(y))).\end{split}

Now that we have defined
A
, we will establish (
A.23
)-(
A.25
). We start with (
A.25
), which is the following.

Lemma A.10
.

We have

\Gamma_{A}\cap D(x_{j,k+1},W,49r_{k+1})=\Sigma_{k+1}\cap D(x_{j,k+1},W,49r_{k+1}).

Before we can show this, we need the following.

Lemma A.11
.

We have

(A.47)

\displaystyle D(x_{j,k+1},W,49r_{k+1})\subseteq D(0,V,35r_{k}).

Proof.

Let
y\in D(x_{j,k+1},W,49r_{k+1}).
We want to show
|\pi(y)|\leq 35r_{k}
and
|\pi(y)-y|\leq 35r_{k}
. We start by showing
|\pi(y)-\pi_{W}(y)|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}
as follows. First,

\displaystyle|\pi(y)-\pi_{W}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq|\pi(y)-\pi_{W^{\prime}}(y)|+|\pi_{W}^{\perp}(p)|

\displaystyle\kern-1.47917pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{e:p}}}|\pi(y)-\pi(\pi_{W^{\prime}}(y))|+|\pi(\pi_{W^{\prime}}(y))-\pi_{W^{\prime}}(y)|+20\varepsilon r_{k}

\displaystyle=|\pi(\pi_{W}^{\perp}(y))|+|\pi^{\perp}(\pi_{W^{\prime}}(y))|+20\varepsilon r_{k}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(|\pi_{W}^{\perp}(y)|+|\pi_{W}(y)|+r_{k}),

where the last inequality follows from (
A.33
) and Lemma
A.2
. Continuing from this, since
y,p\in W
and
W^{\perp}
is orthogonal to
W,
we must have
\pi_{W}^{\perp}(y)=\pi_{W}^{\perp}(p).
So,

\displaystyle|\pi(y)-\pi_{W}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(|\pi_{W}^{\perp}(p)|+|\pi_{W}(y)|+r_{k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:pi(y)}}}{{\leq}}C\varepsilon(|p|+49r_{k+1}+r_{k})

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon r_{k}.

Our required bounds for
|\pi(y)|
and
|\pi(y)-y|
now follow for
\epsilon
small enough since

(A.48)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\pi(y)|&\leq|\pi(y)-\pi_{W}(y)|+|\pi_{W}(y)-x_{j,k+1}|+|x_{j,k+1}|\\
&\kern-9.9167pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{e:2B_ik}}}|\pi(y)-\pi_{W}(y)|+49r_{k+1}+2r_{k}\\
&\leq|\pi(y)-\pi_{W}(y)|+7r_{k}\leq 8r_{k}\leq 35r_{k}\end{split}

and

(A.49)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\pi(y)-y|&\leq|\pi(y)-\pi_{W}(y)|+|\pi_{W}(y)-y|\\
&\leq C\varepsilon r_{k}+49r_{k+1}\leq 5r_{k}\leq 35r_{k}.\end{split}

∎

Proof of Lemma
A.10
.

Since
\Gamma_{A}\subseteq\Sigma_{k+1}
by (
A.46
), the forward inclusion is clear. Our goal now is to show the backward inclusion. Let
p\in\Sigma_{k+1}\cap D(x_{j,k+1},W,49r_{k+1}),
we must show
p\in\Gamma_{A}.
By (
A.47
) we have
p\in D(0,V,35r_{k}),
which, since
\Sigma_{k+1}
is the graph of
F
over
D(0,V,38r_{k})
, means we can find a point
y\in V\cap 35B_{i,k}
such that

p=y+F(y).

For
\varepsilon
small enough we have
V\cap 35B_{i,k}\subseteq\pi(W\cap 38B_{i,k})
by Lemma
A.3
, so we can find a point
z\in W\cap 38B_{i,k}
such that
y=\pi(z).
Then,

p=\pi(z)+F(\pi(z)).

Let
w=f(z)\in U.
Since
f
is bijective we can write

p=\pi(f^{-1}(w))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(w)))

and so (
A.46
) implies

p=w+A(w)\in\Gamma_{A}

as required.
∎

To finish the proof of Lemma
A.8
and hence Stage 1, we are left to show the following.

Lemma A.12
.

We have
|A(x_{j,k+1})|\leq C_{1}\varepsilon r_{k}
and
|DA(z)|\leq C_{2}\varepsilon
for all
z\in W\cap 49B_{j,k+1}.

Proof.

We start with by estimating
|A(x_{j,k+1})|.
To simplify notation we will write
x=x_{j,k+1}.
Notice, in the definition of
A
, we can replace
p
with any point in
W.
Choosing the point
f^{-1}(x)\in W,
we have

\displaystyle|A(x)|

\displaystyle=|\pi^{\perp}_{W}(\pi(f^{-1}(x))+F(\pi(f^{-1}(x))))-\pi_{W}^{\perp}(f^{-1}(x))|

\displaystyle=|\pi^{\perp}_{W}(\pi(f^{-1}(x))-f^{-1}(x)+F(\pi(f^{-1}(x))))|

\displaystyle\leq|\pi(f^{-1}(x))-f^{-1}(x)|+|F(\pi(f^{-1}(x)))|

\displaystyle\kern-8.7328pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{e:pi-y}\atop\eqref{e:Fnorm}}}(C+C_{3})\varepsilon|f^{-1}(x)|\leq C(1+C_{3})\varepsilon|x|

\displaystyle\kern-9.9167pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{e:2B_ik}}}C(1+C_{3})\varepsilon r_{k+1}.

The required estimate holds as long as we choose

C(1+C_{3})\leq C_{1}.

Now we estimate
|DA(z)|,
recall Remark
A.7
. Let
y,z\in W\cap 49B_{j,k+1}.
By (
A.40
), we know that
f^{-1}(y),f^{-1}(z)\in W\cap 49.5B_{j,k+1}
, which by (
A.37
), implies

\pi(f^{-1}(y)),\pi(f^{-1}(z))\in V\cap 50B_{j,k+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:2B_ik}}}{{\subseteq}}V\cap 7B_{i,k}.

Then

\displaystyle|A(y)-A(z)|

\displaystyle\leq|\pi(f^{-1}(y)-f^{-1}(z))|+|F(\pi(f^{-1}(y))-F(\pi(f^{-1}(z)))|

\displaystyle\kern-8.31601pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{e:Flip1}}}(1+C_{5})|\pi(f^{-1}(y))-\pi(f^{-1}(z))|

\displaystyle\leq C(1+C_{5})\varepsilon|f^{-1}(y)-f^{-1}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq C(1+C_{5})\varepsilon|y-z|,

where the third inequality follows from (
A.33
) since
f^{-1}(y)-f^{-1}(z)\in W^{\prime}.
So, if

C(1+C_{5})\leq C_{2}

then (
A.23
) holds.
∎

Stage 2

In this section we show the following.

Lemma A.13
.

If (
A.23
)-(
A.25
) hold for
k
then (
A.26
)-(
A.29
) also hold for
k
.

Let
j\in J_{k}
. Our ultimate goal is to define a map
F=F_{j,k}
so that (
A.26
)-(
A.29
) hold. We start with some preliminaries. By translation, we may assume
x_{j,k}=0.
Let
P_{j,k},B_{j,k}
and
A_{j,k}
satisfy (
A.23
)-(
A.25
). For ease of notation we denote

P=P_{j,k},\ B=B_{j,k}\ \text{and}\ A=A_{j,k}.

Also, we define
\pi
to be the orthogonal projection onto the plane
P
. Before we can define
F
, we need to prove some preliminary estimates and define an auxiliary function. The first of these estimates is Theorem
3.6
(10).

Lemma A.14
.

Let
y\in\Sigma_{k}.
Then

(A.50)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{k}(y)-y|\leq C(C_{1}+1)\varepsilon r_{k}.

Proof.

If
y\not\in V_{k}^{10}
then by definition
\sigma_{k}(y)=y
and (
A.50
) is trivial. Assume instead that
y\in V_{k}^{10}.
Let
j\in J_{k}
such that
y\in 10B_{j,k}
Let
A_{j,k}
be the map which satisfies (
A.23
)-(
A.25
). If
i\in J_{k}
also satisfies
y\in 10B_{i,k}
then by the Definition
3.1
(3) we have

d_{x_{j,k},100r_{k}}(P_{j,k},P_{i,k})\leq\varepsilon

and so

(A.51)

\displaystyle|\pi_{i,k}(y)-y|

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,P_{i,k})\leq|\pi_{j,k}(y)-y|+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\pi_{j,k}(y),P_{i,k})

(A.52)

\displaystyle\leq|A_{j,k}(\pi_{j,k}(y))|+100\varepsilon r_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:Anorm}}}{{\leq}}(C_{1}+C)\varepsilon r_{k}.

Since the functions
\theta_{j,k}
are supported on
10B_{j,k}
, which have bounded overlap (by the arguments in the proof of Lemma
3.4
), we can use this and the above to get

(A.53)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{k}(y)-y|\leq\sum_{i\in J_{k}}|\theta_{i,k}(y)||\pi_{i,k}(y)-y|\leq C(C_{1}+1)\varepsilon r_{k},

which finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.15
.

Let
y,z\in\Sigma_{k}\cap 45B.
Then,

(A.54)

\displaystyle|(\sigma_{k}(y)-y)-(\sigma_{k}(z)-z)|\leq C(C_{1}+C_{2}+1)\varepsilon|y-z|.

Proof.

First, we write

(A.55)

\displaystyle|(\sigma_{k}(y)-y)-(\sigma_{k}(z)-z)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in J_{k}}|\theta_{i,k}(y)-\theta_{i,k}(z)||\pi_{i,k}(y)-y|

(A.56)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i\in J_{k}}|\theta_{i,k}(y)||\pi_{i,k}^{\perp}(y-z)|

(A.57)

\displaystyle=S_{1}+S_{2}.

Since
y,z\in 45B,
if for some
i\in J_{k}
we have either
\theta_{i,k}(y)\not=0
or
\theta_{i,k}(z)\not=0
then
|x_{i,k}|\leq 100r_{k}.
By the Definition
3.1
(3), we get

(A.58)

\displaystyle d_{0,100r_{k}}(P,P_{i,k})\leq\varepsilon.

By exactly the same estimate as in (
A.51
), this implies

(A.59)

\displaystyle|\pi_{i,k}(y)-y|\leq(C_{1}+C)\varepsilon r_{k}.

Then, since the balls
10B_{i,k}
have bounded overlap and
|\theta_{i,k}(y)-\theta_{i,k}(z)|\lesssim r_{k}^{-1}
by Lemma
3.3
(3), it follows that

(A.60)

\displaystyle S_{1}\leq C(C_{1}+1)\varepsilon|y-z|.

We now control
S_{2}.
Recall that
\Sigma_{k}\cap 45B
is contained in the graph of
A
over
P\cap 49B
. In particular,
y=\pi(y)+A(\pi(y)),
with a similar expression holding for
z
. Again, if
\theta_{i,k}(y)\not=0
then (
A.58
) holds, in particular, this implies
d_{0,100r_{k}}(P,P_{i,k}^{\prime})\lesssim\varepsilon
by Lemma
A.1
, where
P_{i,k}^{\prime}
is the linear space orthogonal to
P_{i,k}
. We use these facts, with Lemma
A.2
and the fact that the balls
10B_{i,k}
have bounded overlap, to control
S_{2}
as follows,

(A.61)

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in J_{k}}|\theta_{i,k}(y)|\left|\pi_{i,k}^{\perp}\left[\pi(y)+A(\pi(y))-(\pi(z)+A(\pi(z))\right]\right|

(A.62)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in J_{k}}|\theta_{i,k}(y)|\left(|\pi_{i,k}^{\perp}(\pi(y)-\pi(z))|+\left|A(\pi(y))-A(\pi(z))\right|\right)

(A.63)

\displaystyle\kern-11.27089pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\begin{subarray}{c}\eqref{e:Anorm}\\
\eqref{e:proj}\end{subarray}}}\sum_{i\in J_{k}}|\theta_{i,k}(y)|(C+C_{2})\varepsilon|\pi(y)-\pi(z)|

(A.64)

\displaystyle\leq C(C_{2}+1)\varepsilon|y-z|.

This completes the proof.

∎

We are almost ready to define
F_{j,k}.
Let us start with an auxiliary function. For
x\in P\cap 40B,
write

h(x)=x+\pi(\sigma_{k}(x+A(x))-(x+A(x))).

See Figure
3
.

Figure 3.
Definition of
h(x)
.

We have

(A.65)

\displaystyle|h(y)-y|\leq|\sigma_{k}(y+A(y))-(y+A(y))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:sig-y}}}{{\leq}}C\varepsilon r_{k}

and

(A.66)

\displaystyle|(h(y)-h(z))-(y-z)|

\displaystyle\leq|\sigma_{k}(y+A(y))-\sigma_{k}(z+A(z))-\left(y+A(y)-(z+A(z))\right)|

(A.67)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:siglip}}}{{\leq}}C\varepsilon|y+A(y)-(z+A(z))|

(A.68)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon|y-z|.

As in Stage 1 this implies that
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\cap 41B\rightarrow U
is bijective, where
U=h(P\cap 41B).
Denote its inverse by
h^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U\rightarrow P\cap 41B.
By (
A.65
) and (
A.66
),
h^{-1}
satisfies

(A.69)

\displaystyle|h^{-1}(x)-x|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k},\quad\text{Lip}(h^{-1})\leq 2.

We don’t include the details here, but a degree theory argument similar to that in the proof of Lemma
6.8
(see also
[
DT12
]
) implies

U\supset P\cap 40B.

Define

F(y)=\pi^{\perp}(\sigma_{k}(h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y)))).

See Figure
4
. We will now verify (
A.26
)-(
A.29
) for
F
. Let us start with (
A.29
), which is the following.

Figure 4.
Definition of
F_{j,k}
.

Lemma A.16
.

We have

\Sigma_{k+1}\cap D(0,P,40B)\subseteq\Gamma_{F}\cap D(0,P,40B).

Proof.

Since
\Gamma_{F}\subseteq\Sigma_{k+1}
by definition, the only non-trivial direction is to show

\Sigma_{k+1}\cap D(0,P,40B)\subseteq\Gamma_{F}\cap D(0,P,40B).

Let
p\in\Sigma_{k+1}\cap D(0,P,40B)
and let
z\in\Sigma_{k}
such that
p=\sigma_{k}(z).
Since
|z-p|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}
by (
A.50
), we see that
\pi(z)\in P\cap 41B.
This implies that
z
is contained in the graph of the function
A
over
P\cap D(0,P,49B)
, hence

p=\sigma_{k}(z)=\sigma_{k}(\pi(z)+A(\pi(z))).

Since
h^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U\rightarrow 41B
is bijective there exists
y\in U
such that
h^{-1}(y)=\pi(z).
This gives

(A.70)

\displaystyle p=\sigma_{k}(h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y)))

\displaystyle=\pi(\sigma_{k}(h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y))))

(A.71)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\pi^{\perp}(\sigma_{k}(h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y))))

(A.72)

\displaystyle=y+\pi^{\perp}(\sigma_{k}(h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y)))

(A.73)

\displaystyle=y+F(y),

where the penultimate equality follows from the definition of
h
. This finishes the proof.
∎

We now focus on proving (
A.26
)-(
A.28
) for
F.
Let us start with (
A.27
).

Lemma A.17
.

For
y,z\in P\cap 40B
we have
|F(y)-F(z)|\leq C_{4}\varepsilon|y-z|.

Proof.

Let
y,z\in P\cap 40B.
To simplify notation we will write

\tilde{y}=h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y)),

with
\tilde{z}
defined analogously. Then

\displaystyle|F(y)-F(z)|

\displaystyle=|\pi^{\perp}(\sigma_{k}(\tilde{y}))-\pi^{\perp}(\sigma_{k}(\tilde{z}))|

\displaystyle\leq|\pi^{\perp}((\sigma_{k}(\tilde{y})-\tilde{y})-(\sigma_{k}(\tilde{z})-\tilde{z}))+|\pi^{\perp}(\tilde{y}-\tilde{z})|

\displaystyle=|\pi^{\perp}((\sigma_{k}(\tilde{y})-\tilde{y})-(\sigma_{k}(\tilde{z})-\tilde{z}))+|A(h^{-1}(y))-A(h^{-1}(z)|

\displaystyle\kern-8.45213pt\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\begin{subarray}{c}\eqref{e:Alip}\\
\eqref{e:siglip}\end{subarray}}}C(C_{1}+C_{2}+1)\varepsilon|\tilde{y}-\tilde{z}|+C_{2}\varepsilon|y-z|

\displaystyle\leq C(C_{1}+C_{2}+1)\varepsilon|y-z|.

If
C_{4}
is chosen so that

C_{4}\geq C(C_{1}+C_{2}+1)

then (
A.27
) follows.
∎

Finally, we prove (
A.26
) and (
A.28
). Both of the constants
C_{3}
and
C_{5}
will be chosen independent of all other constants and this closes the induction.

Lemma A.18
.

We have
|F(x_{j,k})|\leq C_{3}\varepsilon r_{k}
and
|F(y)-F(z)|\leq C_{5}\varepsilon|y-z|
for all
y,z\in P\cap 7B.

Proof.

Let us start with the first statement, it will be useful to prove the same bound for any point in
P\cap 7B.
Let
y\in P\cap 7B
and let
w=h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y)).
By (
A.50
) and (
A.69
) we have

(A.74)

\displaystyle|\sigma_{k}(w)-y|

\displaystyle\leq|\sigma_{k}(w)-w|+|w-y|

(A.75)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon r_{k}+|h^{-1}(y)-y|+|A(h^{-1}(y))|

(A.76)

\displaystyle\leq C(1+C_{1})\varepsilon r_{k}

so that
\sigma_{k}(w)\in 8B
for
\varepsilon
small enough. By Lemma
A.4
, this implies

(A.77)

\displaystyle|F(y)|=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\sigma_{k}(w),P)\leq|\sigma_{k}(w)-\pi(w)|\leq C\varepsilon r_{k}.

Taking
y=x_{j,k}
proves (
A.26
) if we take
C_{3}
large enough.

We prove the second statement. Let
y,z\in 7B
and let
w_{y}=h^{-1}(y)+A(h^{-1}(y))
and
w_{z}=h^{-1}(z)+A(h^{-1}(z)).
Examining (
A.74
), we see
w_{y},w_{z}\in 8B
for
\varepsilon
small enough. By Lemma
A.5
, (
A.66
), and (
A.24
), we have

\displaystyle|F(y)-F(z)|

\displaystyle=|\pi^{\perp}(\sigma_{k}(w_{y})-\sigma_{k}(w_{z}))|

\displaystyle=|\pi^{\perp}(\sigma_{k}(w_{y})-\sigma_{k}(w_{z})-\pi(w_{y})-\pi(w_{z}))|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon|w_{y}-w_{z}|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(|h^{-1}(y)-h^{-1}(z)|+|A(y)-A(z)|)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(|y-z|+C_{2}\varepsilon|y-z|)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon|y-z|.

This completes the proof of (
A.27
) if we take
C_{5}
large enough.
∎

Finally, we show Theorem
3.6
(13), which is the following.

Remaining results

First, let us proof Theorem
3.6
(13), which is the following.

Lemma A.19
.

Let
\Sigma
be the surface produced by Theorem
3.6
. Then, for
x\in\Sigma
and
r>0
,

\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma\cap B(x,r))\gtrsim r^{d}.

Proof.

Consider the map
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow H
given by
A(y)=\tfrac{y-x}{r},
and the set
\tilde{\Sigma}=A(\Sigma).
Then,
0\in\tilde{\Sigma}
, and since
\Sigma
is
C\varepsilon
-Reifenberg flat, the same is true of
\tilde{\Sigma}
. Let
P
be a
d
-plane through
0
so that

d_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{\Sigma},P)\leq C\varepsilon.

By
[
ENV18
, Proposition 2.9]
, for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, there exists a bi-Hölder map
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P\rightarrow H
which is the identity outside of
B(0,3/2)
, satisfies
|g(y)-y|\lesssim\varepsilon
for
y\in P
, and so that
\tilde{\Sigma}\cap B(0,1)=g(P)\cap B(0,1)
. Let
\pi
denote the orthogonal projection onto
P
, and define
h=\pi\circ g.
By definition,
h
is continuous and is the identity outside
B(0,3/2),
so, it is surjective. Additionally, for
y\in P,

|h(y)-y|=|\pi(g(y))-\pi(y)|\leq|g(y)-y|\lesssim\varepsilon.

Let
z\in P\cap B(0,1/2)
. Since
h
is surjective, we can find
w\in P
so that
z=h(w).
For
\varepsilon
small enough, it follows from the inequality above that
w\in B(0,3/4)
and so

|g(w)|\leq|g(w)-w|+|w|\leq C\varepsilon+\frac{3}{4}\leq 1.

In particular,
g(w)\in\tilde{\Sigma}\cap B(0,1)
and
z=\pi(g(w)).
We have just show that
P\cap B(0,1/2)\subseteq\pi(\tilde{\Sigma}\cap B(0,1)).
This gives,

\mathscr{H}^{d}(\tilde{\Sigma}\cap B(0,1))\geq\mathscr{H}^{d}(\pi(\tilde{\Sigma}\cap B(0,1)))\geq\mathscr{H}^{d}(P\cap B(0,\tfrac{1}{2}))\gtrsim 1.

The lemma follow from this since

\mathscr{H}^{d}(\Sigma\cap B(x,r))=\mathscr{H}^{d}(A^{-1}(\tilde{\Sigma}\cap B(0,1)))=r^{d}\mathscr{H}^{d}(\tilde{\Sigma}\cap B(0,1))\gtrsim r^{d}.

∎

As mentioned, we direct the reader to the proofs of the remaining results in Theorem
3.6
. For (1),(3),(8) and (9), see
[
DT12
, Equation 6.2, Proposition 8.1, Lemma 6.2 and Lemma 6.3]
, respectively. For (12), see
[
DT12
, Lemma 7.1]
. This proof relies on
[
DT12
, Equation 5.12]
(which we do not prove), but the same estimate can be obtained from (
A.54
).

Appendix B
Constants

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose that for any
\tilde{C}_{0}>1
and
\tilde{A}>\max\{6\tilde{C}_{0},10^{5}\}
there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that (
5.1
) holds with constant
\tilde{C}_{0},\tilde{A}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}.
Then, for any
C_{0}>1
and
A>10^{5}
there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that (
5.1
) holds with constant
C_{0},A
and
\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
C_{0}>1
,
A>10^{5}
, and
E\subseteq H
be
(c,d)
-lower content
d
-regular. If
A>6C_{0}
then there is nothing to do, so let us assume
A\leq 6C_{0}.
Choose
\kappa\sim_{A,C_{0}}1
so that
A>6\kappa C_{0}>A/2
and choose
K=K(A,C_{0})
to be the smallest integer so that
C_{0}\rho^{K}+1\leq\kappa C_{0}.
Choose
\varepsilon>0
so that (
5.1
) holds with constants
A,\kappa C_{0}
and
\varepsilon.
First, we separate

(B.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{k=K}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\\
Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

(B.2)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{k=K}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\\
Q\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

(B.3)

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

The term
I_{2}
is bounded by
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)
be definition. Additionally, since
E
is lower regular and the balls
c_{0}B_{Q},\ Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}
, are disjoint, we have

I_{1}\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap c_{0}B_{Q})\leq K\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}).

We are left to bound
I_{3}.
Let
\mathscr{C}
to be the collection of cubes
Q\in\bigcup_{k=K}^{\infty}\mathscr{D}_{k}
so that
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
and
Q\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon).
Let
\mathscr{C}_{1}
be the set of cubes
Q\in\mathscr{C}
so that
Q^{(K)}\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
. Also, set
\mathscr{C}_{2}
to be the set of cubes
Q\in\mathscr{C}
so that
Q^{(K)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
. Notice that
Q^{(K)}\subseteq Q_{0}
in either case. Then

I_{3}=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}=I_{3,1}+I_{3,2}.

We start by estimating
I_{3,1}.
For
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1},
set
Q^{*}=Q^{(K)}.
We begin by showing that the map
Q\mapsto Q^{*}
is at most
C
-to-
1
for some universal constant
C.
Indeed, let
R\in\mathscr{D}
and consider
\mathscr{C}_{1}(R)=\{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q^{*}=R\}.
Since
R\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon),
there exists a
d
-plane
P
so that
d_{AB_{R}}(E,P)\leq\varepsilon.
So, for
\varepsilon
small enough,
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq\varepsilon A\ell(R)\leq\tfrac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(Q)
and
c_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq B_{R}=B(x_{R},\rho^{-K}\ell(Q))
for all
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}(R).
By Lemma
2.14
, this implies
\#\mathscr{C}_{1}(R)\lesssim 1
as required. Now, by our choice of
K
,
C_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq\kappa C_{0}B_{Q^{*}}
. So, by our assumption in the statement of Lemma
B.1
, Lemma
2.11
, and the above, we have

(B.4)

\displaystyle I_{3,1}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(\kappa C_{0}B_{Q*})^{2}\ell(Q^{*})^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(\kappa C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

Now for
I_{3,2}
. For
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}
, define
Q^{*}
to be the smallest cube so that
Q\subseteq Q^{*}
and
Q^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon).
Such a cube exists since
Q^{(K)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
by definition. Again, we show the map
Q\mapsto Q^{*}
is at most
C
-to-
1
for some universal constant
C.
Let
R\in\mathscr{D}
for which the set
\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)=\{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q^{*}=R\}
is non-empty. Note, there are at most
K
scales for which
\#\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}\not=\emptyset.
Choose
k
to be one of these scales and let
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}.
Since
R
is the smallest cube containing
Q
so that
R\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon),
there exists a child
R^{\prime}
of
R
so that
R^{\prime}\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon).
As above, since
B_{R}\subseteq AB_{R^{\prime}}
(because
A>10^{5}
), we can find a
d
-plane
P
so that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq\frac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(Q)
and
c_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq B_{R}\subseteq B(x_{R},\rho^{-K}\ell(Q)).
Then, by Lemma
2.14
,
\#\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}\lesssim 1
, and so
\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\lesssim 1.
Hence,

(B.5)

\displaystyle I_{3,2}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}}\ell(Q^{*})^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq Q_{0}\\
Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{d}=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
A\geq 1
and
M\geq C_{0}
, and suppose

(B.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Then, (
6.1
) holds with constant
\varepsilon,A
and
C_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof will be similar to the proof of Lemma
B.1
. First, by Lemma
2.10
,

(B.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

The remainder of the proof will be focussed controlling the right-hand side of (
B.7
) in terms of the same quantity but with
C_{0}
in place of
M
. Let
K=K(C_{0},M)
be the smallest integer so that
1+M\rho^{K}\leq C_{0}
and let
\delta>0
be a constant to be chosen small. Let
\mathscr{C}
be the set of cubes
Q\in\mathscr{D}
so that
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
and
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})\leq\delta.
Notice that

(B.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\setminus\mathscr{C}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim_{\delta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d},

so we only need to control the sum over cubes in
\mathscr{C}.
Let
\mathscr{C}_{1}
be the set of cubes
Q\in\mathscr{C}\cap\bigcup_{k=K}^{\infty}\mathscr{D}_{k}
so that
Q^{(K)}
satisfies
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q^{(K)}})\leq\delta
and let
\mathscr{C}_{2}
be the set of cubes
Q\in\mathscr{C}\cap\bigcup_{k=K}^{\infty}\mathscr{D}_{k}
so that
Q^{(K)}
satisfies
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q^{(K)}})>\delta
. Note that
Q^{(K)}\subseteq Q_{0}
in either case. Then,

(B.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

(B.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

(B.11)

\displaystyle=I_{0}+I_{1}+I_{2}.

Let us estimate
I_{1}.
For
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}
let
Q^{*}=Q^{(K)}
. Let
R\in\mathscr{D}
be so that
\mathscr{C}_{1}(R)=\{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q^{*}=R\}
is non-empty. By Lemma
2.9
,
\beta^{d}_{E,\infty}(\tfrac{C_{0}}{2}B_{R})\lesssim\delta^{\frac{1}{d+1}}
so there exists a
d
-plane
P
so that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,P)\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(R)
for all
y\in E\cap\tfrac{C_{0}}{2}B_{R}.
Then, for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, a similar argument to Lemma
B.1
shows the map
Q\mapsto Q^{*}
is at most
C
-to-
1
. Now, since
MB_{Q}\subseteq C_{0}B_{Q^{*}},
Lemma
2.9
implies

(B.12)

\displaystyle I_{1}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{1}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q^{*}})^{2}\ell(Q^{*})^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

Now for
I_{2}.
For
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}
, define
Q^{*}
to be the smallest cube so that
Q\subseteq Q^{*}
and
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q^{*}})>\delta.
Let
R\in\mathscr{D}
for which the set
\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)=\{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q^{*}=R\}
is non-empty. We will show

\#\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\lesssim 1.

Note, there are at most
K
scales for which
\#\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}\not=\emptyset.
Choose
k
to be one of these scales and let
Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}.
Since
R
is the smallest cube containing
Q
with
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{R})>\delta
, there exists a child
R^{\prime}
of
R
so that
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{R^{\prime}})\leq\delta.
As above, since
B_{R}\subseteq C_{0}B_{R^{\prime}}
(because we assumed
C_{0}\geq 2\rho^{-1}
), we can find a
d
-plane
P
so that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq\frac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(Q)
and
c_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq B_{R}\subseteq B(x_{R},\rho^{-K}\ell(Q)).
Then, by Lemma
2.14
,
\#\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\cap\mathscr{D}_{k}\lesssim 1
and so
\#\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)\lesssim 1.
Hence,

(B.13)

\displaystyle I_{2}\lesssim_{\delta}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{C}_{2}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q^{*}})^{2}\ell(Q^{*})^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Finally, we deal with
I_{0}.
Let
\delta>0
be as above. If
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q_{0}})\leq\delta
then the arguments above (
B.12
) show that
\#\{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k},0\leq k\leq K-1\}\lesssim 1.
So, in this case,

I_{0}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{d}.

Suppose instead that
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q_{0}})>\delta.
For each
Q\in\bigcup_{k=0}^{K-1}\mathscr{D}_{k}
let
Q^{*}
be the smallest cube so that
Q\subseteq Q^{*}
and
\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q^{*}})>\delta.
In this case, by exactly the same arguments as for
I_{2},
we get

I_{0}\lesssim_{\delta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Combining the above gives

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,1}(MB_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta_{E}^{d,p}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

as required.
∎

Appendix C
Reduction to Euclidean space

Here we prove Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
5.2
. However, since Lemma
4.1
is a special case of Lemma
5.2
, it suffices to only prove Lemma
5.2
, which we restate below for convenience.

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose for any
n\geq 2
,
1\leq d<n,

1\leq p<p(d),

C_{0}>1
, and
A>\max\{6C_{0},10^{5}\}
, that there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that (
1.8
) holds with constants
d,p,C_{0},A,
and
\varepsilon
, for any lower content
d
-regular set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with constants independent of
n
. Then, there exists
C\geq 1
so that (
1.8
) holds with constant
d,p,C_{0},2A
, and
C\varepsilon,
for all
E\subseteq H
lower content
d
-regular sets
E\subseteq H.

Let
1\leq d<\infty,\ 1\leq p<p(d)
,
C_{0}>1
,
A>\max\{6C_{0},10^{5}\}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
E\subseteq H
be a lower content
d
-regular set. Let
X_{m},\ m\in\mathbb{Z},
be a sequence of maximally
\rho^{m}
-separated nets and let
\mathscr{D}
be the Christ-David cubes for
E
from Theorem
2.1
. Fix
Q_{0}\in\mathscr{D}
and assume without loss of generality that
x_{Q_{0}}=0
and
\ell(Q_{0})=1.
We may also assume
\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})<\infty
since otherwise (
1.8
) is trivial. For
k\geq 0
let

\mathscr{D}^{k}(Q_{0})=\bigcup_{m=0}^{k}\{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\subseteq Q_{0}\}.

Notice that

(C.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}^{k}(Q_{0})}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d},

so it suffices to show

(C.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}^{k}(Q_{0})}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)

with constant independent
k
. This is our goal for the rest of the section. Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
k^{*}
be the smallest integer so that
\rho^{k^{*}}\leq 5\varepsilon\rho^{k}.
Since
E
is lower content
d
-regular,
\#\mathscr{D}^{k^{*}}(Q_{0})<\infty
, because for each
0\leq m\leq k^{*}
,

(C.3)

\displaystyle\#\{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\subseteq Q_{0}\}\rho^{md}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{m}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap c_{0}B_{Q})\leq\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})<\infty.

Let
V
be the linear space spanned by the centres of the cubes in
\mathscr{D}^{k^{*}}(Q_{0})
(recall
x_{Q_{0}}=0
). By the above, we can identify
V
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\leq\#\mathscr{D}^{k^{*}}(Q_{0})<\infty.
We now define a subset
\tilde{E}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
which well approximates
E
. Let
T
be a
d
-dimensional simplex centred at the origin, with vertices in
S^{n-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
(the unit sphere). For
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}},
define
T_{Q}=x_{Q}+c_{0}\ell(Q)T.
Then each
T_{Q}
is Ahlfors
d
-regular and contained in the ball
B_{n}(x_{Q},c_{0}\ell(Q))=B_{n}(x_{Q},5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}).
Define
\tilde{E}
by setting

\tilde{E}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}}T_{Q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Lemma C.2
.

The set
\tilde{E}
is lower content
d
-regular

Proof.

Let
x\in\tilde{E}
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(\tilde{E})
. Assume first of all that
r<20c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}.
Let
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}
be the cube so that
x\in T_{Q}.
Since
T_{Q}
is lower content
d
-regular (by virtue of being Ahlfors
d
-regular) and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(T_{Q})=10c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}\gtrsim r
, this implies

(C.4)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r))\geq\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(T_{Q}\cap B(x,r))\gtrsim r^{d}.

Assume instead that
r\geq 20c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}.
For
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
0<\delta<\infty
, set

(C.5)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\delta,\infty}(A)=\inf\left\{\sum_{i}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(A_{i})^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\subseteq\bigcup_{i}A_{i}\mbox{ and }\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}A_{i}\geq\delta\right\}.

This is like the Hausdorff content except we include a
lower
bound on the diameters of the covering sets. The lemma will be proved once we show the following,

(C.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r))\gtrsim\mathscr{H}^{d}_{5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}},\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r/2))\gtrsim r^{d}.

We start with the left-hand side. First note that
\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r))>0
by (
C.4
). Thus, we can find a collection of sets
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
so that
\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
and

\sum_{i\in I}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}\leq 2\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r)).

We will modify
\{U_{i}\}
to replace those sets with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})<5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}.
Let
\mathscr{G}
be the set of cubes in
\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}
so that
T_{Q}\cap B(x,r/2)\neq\emptyset
. For each
Q\in\mathscr{G},
let
I_{Q}
be the set of
i\in I
so that
T_{Q}\cap U_{i}\not=\emptyset
and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})<5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}.
Let
\alpha>0
(to be chosen shortly) and further sub-divide
\mathscr{G}
into

(C.7)

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{Q\in\mathscr{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{i\in I_{Q}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}>\alpha(10c_{0})^{d}\rho^{dk^{*}}\right\};

(C.8)

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{Q\in\mathscr{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{i\in I_{Q}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}\leq\alpha(10c_{0})^{d}\rho^{dk^{*}}\right\}.

If
Q\in\mathscr{G}_{1}
then
T_{Q}\subseteq B(x_{Q},5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}})
and

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(B(x_{Q},5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}))^{d}=(10c_{0})^{d}\rho^{dk^{*}}<\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{i\in I_{Q}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}.

For
Q\in\mathscr{G}_{2}
we claim there exists
i_{Q}\in I
so that
T_{Q}\cap U_{i_{Q}}\not=\emptyset
and
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i_{Q}})\geq 5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}.
in particular
i_{Q}\not\in I_{Q}.
Assuming this to be true for the moment, we let
\tilde{I}\subseteq I
be the set of indices for which there exists
Q\in\mathscr{G}_{2}
so that
i_{Q}=i
. For each
i\in\tilde{I}
we pick a point
x_{i}\in U_{i}
and set
B_{i}=B(x_{i},2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})).
In this way

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(B_{i})\geq 20c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}\mbox{ and }\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{G}_{2}\\
i_{Q}=i\end{subarray}}T_{Q}\subseteq B_{i}.

Putting everything together we have

\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r/2)\subseteq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathscr{G}}T_{Q}\subseteq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathscr{G}_{1}}B(x_{Q},5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}})\cup\bigcup_{i\in\tilde{I}}B_{i},

hence,

(C.9)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}_{5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}},\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r/2))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}_{1}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(B(x_{Q},5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}))^{d}+\sum_{i\in\tilde{I}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(B_{i})^{d}

(C.10)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+2\right)\sum_{i\in I}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r)).

This completes the left-hand side of (
C.6
) modulo our claim. Let us see why the claim is true. Since
T_{Q}\cap B(x,r/2)\not=\emptyset,\ \mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(T_{Q})=10c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}\leq r/2,
we have
T_{Q}\subseteq\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r).
In particular, the collection of set
\{U_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\in I,\ T_{Q}\cap U_{i}\neq\emptyset\}
form a cover for
T_{Q}.
Then, since
T_{Q}
is lower content
d
-regular, we have

(C.11)

\displaystyle\rho^{dk^{*}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
T_{Q}\cap U_{i}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\setminus I_{Q}\\
T_{Q}\cap U_{i}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I_{Q}\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\setminus I_{Q}\\
T_{Q}\cap U_{i}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{d}+\alpha\rho^{dk^{*}}.

For
\alpha>0
small enough, the penultimate term must be non-zero which implies the existence of the required index
i\in I
and proves the claim.

Now for the right-hand side of (
C.6
). Let
\{V_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a collection of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that
5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}}\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(V_{j})<\infty
,
\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r/2)\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in J}V_{j}
and

\mathscr{H}^{d}_{5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}},\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r/2))\sim\sum_{j\in J}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(V_{j})^{d}.

For each
j\in J,
let
y_{j}\in V_{j}
and
\tilde{B}_{j}=B(y_{j},c_{0}^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(V_{j}))\subseteq H.
For each
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}
so that
x_{Q}\in B(x,r/2)
, there exists
j\in J
so that
x_{Q}\in V_{j},
which by the triangle inequality and the lower bound on
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(V_{j})
, implies
B(x_{Q},2\rho^{k^{*}})\subseteq\tilde{B}_{j}.
In particular,

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}\\
x_{Q}\in B(x,r/2)\end{subarray}}B(x_{Q},2\rho^{k^{*}})\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in J}\tilde{B}_{j}.

By maximality, the collection
\{B(x_{Q},2\rho^{k^{*}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}},\ x_{Q}\in B(x,r/2)\}
forms a cover of
E\cap B(x,r/4)
. By the above, the same is true of the collection of balls
\{\tilde{B}_{j}\}_{j\in J}.
Since
E
is lower content
d
-regular, this implies

(C.12)

\displaystyle r^{d}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}(E\cap B(x,r/4))\leq\sum_{j\in J}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(\tilde{B}_{j})^{d}\sim\sum_{j\in J}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(V_{j})^{d}\sim\mathscr{H}^{d}_{5c_{0}\rho^{k^{*}},\infty}(\tilde{E}\cap B(x,r/2))

as required.
∎

Let
Y_{m},\ m\in\mathbb{Z}
, be a sequence of nested maximally
\rho^{m}
-separated nets in
\tilde{E}.
We can construct these so that
Y_{m}=X_{m}
for each
m\leq k^{*}
(recall that
X_{m}
were the nets we used to construct the cubes for
E
). Let
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}
be the Christ-David cubes for
\tilde{E}
constructed from the
Y_{m}.
In this way, for each
Q\in\mathscr{D}^{k^{*}}
there exists a unique
\tilde{Q}\in\tilde{\mathscr{D}}^{k^{*}}
so that
x_{Q}=x_{\tilde{Q}}
and
\ell(Q)=\ell(\tilde{Q}),
and vice-versa. These also satisfy

(C.13)

\displaystyle Y_{m}\cap Q=Y_{m}\cap\tilde{Q}\mbox{ for all }m\leq k^{*}.

Lemma C.3
.

We have
\mathscr{H}^{d}(\tilde{Q}_{0})\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}).

Proof.

First observe that

(C.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{0}\subseteq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}T_{Q}.

Indeed, suppose the opposite was true. Then we could find a point
z\in\tilde{Q}_{0}
so that
z\in T_{Q}
for some
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}
satisfying
Q\cap Q_{0}=\emptyset.
Let
\tilde{Q}
be the corresponding cube in
\tilde{\mathscr{D}}.
Then
x_{\tilde{Q}}\not\in Q_{0}
and so
x_{\tilde{Q}}\not\in\tilde{Q}_{0}
by (
C.13
). Thus
\tilde{Q}\cap\tilde{Q}_{0}=\emptyset.
This, however, is a contradiction since

z\in T_{Q}\subseteq\tilde{E}\cap c_{0}B_{Q}=\tilde{E}\cap c_{0}B_{\tilde{Q}}\subseteq\tilde{Q}.

Continuing from (
C.14
), since each of the
T_{Q},\ Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}},
are Ahlfors
d
-regular, and the balls
E\cap c_{0}B_{Q}
are disjoint and contained in
Q_{0}
, we have

(C.15)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d}(\tilde{Q}_{0})\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(T_{Q})\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap c_{0}B_{Q})\leq\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})

∎

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\tilde{Q}\in\mathscr{D}^{k}(\tilde{Q}_{0})
and let
Q\in\mathscr{D}^{k}(Q_{0})
be the corresponding cube from above. If
\tilde{Q}\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(\tfrac{1}{2}A,c\varepsilon)
then
Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon).

Proof.

We will prove the contrapositive. In particular, let us suppose that
Q\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
, we will show
\tilde{Q}\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(\tfrac{1}{2}A,c\varepsilon).

For brevity, we write
B=AB_{Q}=AB_{\tilde{Q}}.
Since
Q\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon),
there exists a
d
-plane
P_{Q}
through
x_{Q}
so that
d_{B}(E,P_{Q})\leq 2\varepsilon.
Choose a collection of point
z_{i},\ i=1,\dots,d
, in
P_{Q}\cap(B\setminus\tfrac{1}{2}B)
so that the vectors
z_{i}-x_{Q}
are mutually orthogonal. Since
d_{B}(E,P_{Q})\leq 2\varepsilon,
there exists
y_{i}\in E\cap\tfrac{3}{4}B
so that
|y_{i}-z_{i}|\leq 2\varepsilon A\ell(Q)
. By maximality, there exists
Q_{i}\in\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}
so that
x_{Q_{i}}\in B
and
|y_{i}-x_{Q_{i}}|\leq\ell(Q_{i})=5\rho^{k^{*}}\leq\varepsilon\ell(Q).
Let
P_{\tilde{Q}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the
d
-plane spanned by the vectors
x_{Q_{i}}-x_{Q}.
Since, by the triangle inequality,
|z_{i}-x_{Q_{i}}|\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q)
, it follows that

(C.16)

\displaystyle d_{B}(P_{Q},P_{\tilde{Q}})\lesssim\varepsilon.

Let us now estimate
d_{B/2}(\tilde{E},P_{\tilde{Q}}).
Let
y\in\tilde{E}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
, we will find
z\in P_{\tilde{Q}}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
so that
|y-z|\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q).
First, since the centres of cubes in
\mathscr{D}_{k^{*}}
are contained in
E
and form a maximally
\rho^{k^{*}}
separated net for
\tilde{E},
we can find
y_{1}\in E\cap(\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)B
so that
|y-y_{1}|\leq\rho^{k^{*}}\leq\varepsilon\ell(Q).
Since
d_{B}(E,P_{Q})<2\varepsilon
, there exists
y_{2}\in P_{Q}\cap(\tfrac{1}{2}+3\varepsilon)B
so that
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq 2\varepsilon A\ell(Q).
By (
C.16
), we can find
y_{3}\in P_{\tilde{Q}}\cap(\tfrac{1}{2}+C\varepsilon)B
so that
|y_{2}-y_{3}|\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q).
Finally, by considering the line segment joining
y_{3}
and
x_{\tilde{Q}}
, we can find a point
z\in P_{\tilde{Q}}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
so that
|y_{3}-y_{z}|\leq CA\varepsilon\ell(Q).
Combining the above chain of inequalities shows
z\in P_{\tilde{Q}}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
is the required point.

A similar argument (essentially working backwards) shows that for each
z\in P_{\tilde{Q}}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
we can find
y\in\tilde{E}\cap\tfrac{1}{2}B
so that
|y-z|\lesssim\varepsilon\ell(Q).
This concludes the argument.

∎

We can now prove Lemma
C.1
.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

Recall that it suffices to show (
C.2
). Let
\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}
denote the set of cubes
Q
in
\mathscr{D}^{k}(Q_{0})
for which there exists a point
y\in E\cap 3C_{0}B_{Q}\setminus Q_{0},
and let
\mathscr{G}=\mathscr{D}^{k}(Q_{0})\setminus\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}.
Then, the left-hand side of (
C.2
) can be written as

\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}.

Let us estimate the sum over
\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}
. First, we claim that the collection of balls
\tfrac{c_{0}}{2}B_{Q},

Q\in\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}
, have bounded overlap. To see this, suppose
Q,R\in\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}
are such that
\tfrac{c_{0}}{2}B_{Q}\cap\tfrac{c_{0}}{2}B_{R}\neq\emptyset.
The claim will follow once we show
\ell(Q)\sim\ell(R)
since the balls
c_{0}B_{Q}
are disjoint for cubes in the same generation. Assume without loss of generality that
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R).
Assume towards a contradiction that
\ell(Q)\leq\tfrac{c_{0}}{12C_{0}}\ell(R).
It follows that
E\cap 3C_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq E\cap c_{0}B_{R}
since for any
y\in 3C_{0}B_{Q},

(C.17)

\displaystyle|y-x_{R}|\leq|y-x_{Q}|+|x_{Q}-x_{R}|\leq 3C_{0}\ell(Q)+\frac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(Q)+\frac{c_{0}}{2}\ell(R)\leq c_{0}\ell(R).

But this means that
E\cap 3C_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq Q_{0}
which contradicts the definition of
\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}.
Using this, we have

(C.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{\text{Bad}}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap c_{0}B_{Q})\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}).

We turn our attention to the sum over
\mathscr{G}.
It will be useful to note (for later on) that if
Q\in\mathscr{G}
then
E\cap 3C_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq Q_{0}.
We begin by applying Lemma
2.13
,

(C.19)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}}\beta^{d,p}_{E}(C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}}\beta^{d,p}_{\tilde{E}}(2C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

(C.20)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}}\left(\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d}}\int_{E\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,\tilde{E})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}

(C.21)

\displaystyle\eqqcolon I_{1}+I_{2}.

By construction, for each
Q\in\mathscr{G}
there exists a unique
\tilde{Q}\in\tilde{\mathscr{D}}
so that
2C_{0}B_{Q}=2C_{0}B_{\tilde{Q}}.
Using this along with Theorem
1.9
, Lemma
C.3
, and Lemma
C.4
, we have

(C.22)

\displaystyle I_{1}\leq\sum_{m=0}^{k}\sum_{Q\in\tilde{\mathscr{D}}_{m}}\beta^{d,p}_{\tilde{E}}(2C_{0}B_{Q})^{2}\ell(Q)^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(\tilde{Q}_{0})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(\frac{1}{2}A,c\varepsilon)\cap\tilde{\mathscr{D}}^{k}(\tilde{Q}_{0})\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{d}

(C.23)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)\cap\mathscr{D}^{k}(Q_{0})\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{d}

(C.24)

\displaystyle\leq\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

We are left to estimate
I_{2}.
We claim

(C.25)

\displaystyle I_{2}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon).

Indeed, notice that

(C.26)

\displaystyle I_{2}\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d}}\int_{E\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,\tilde{E})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(Q_{0},A,\varepsilon)

Denoting the first term above by
I_{3},
the proof of (
C.25
) will be complete once we show

I_{3}\lesssim\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}).

Let
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}=\mathscr{G}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
and for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
set
\mathscr{G}_{m}^{\prime}=\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{D}_{m}.
First, we may assume
p\geq 2
by Lemma
2.10
. Notice, if
y\in 2C_{0}B_{R}
for some
R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}
then
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,\tilde{E})\lesssim\ell(R)
since
x_{R}\in\tilde{E}.
Hence,

(C.28)

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{k}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}_{m}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
B_{R}\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\int_{E\cap B_{R}}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,\tilde{E})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{p}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d}_{\infty}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}

(C.29)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{k}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}_{m}^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
B_{R}\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d+p}}{\ell(Q)^{d+p}}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{k}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}_{m}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
B_{R}\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d\frac{2}{p}+2}}{\ell(Q)^{d(\frac{2}{p}-1)+2}}

Let
R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}
. For
m\leq k
, let
\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime}
be the set of cubes
Q
in
\mathscr{G}_{m}^{\prime}
so that
B_{R}\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset.
We will show

(C.30)

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime}\lesssim 1.

If
\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime}
is empty then there is nothing to show. Let us assume it is not empty, and fix some
Q^{*}\in\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime}.
For any other
Q\in\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime}
, since
B_{R}\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\neq\emptyset
and
\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q),
it follows that
c_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq B\coloneqq 6C_{0}B_{Q^{*}}.
Also, since
A>6C_{0}
and
Q\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{BWGL}(A,\varepsilon)
(by definition of
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
) we can find a
d
-plane
P
through
x_{B}
so that
d_{B}(E,P)\lesssim\varepsilon.
Thus, for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, the balls
c_{0}B_{Q},\ Q\in\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime},
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
2.14
, and we conclude (
C.30
) since

(C.31)

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime}\rho^{md}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{G}_{R,m}^{\prime}}\ell(Q)^{d}\lesssim\rho^{md}.

Let us return to
I_{3}.
Notice, if
R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}
,
m\leq k
, and
Q\in\mathscr{G}^{\prime}_{m}
is such that
B_{R}\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset
, then
E\cap B_{R}\subseteq E\cap 3C_{0}B_{Q}\subseteq Q_{0}.
Thus, continuing from (
C.28
) by swapping the order of summation, using (
C.30
), and summing over a geometric series, we have

(C.32)

\displaystyle I_{3}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
R\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathscr{G}_{m}^{\prime}\\
B_{R}\cap 2C_{0}B_{Q}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{d\frac{2}{p}+2}}{\ell(Q)^{d(\frac{2}{p}-1)+2}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
R\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{d}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}_{k}\\
R\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{H}^{d}(E\cap c_{0}B_{R})\leq\mathscr{H}^{d}(Q_{0}).

∎
